- Basilisk (2025)
    - title: Basilisk: Using Provenance Invariants to Automate Proofs of Undecidable Protocols
    - what: A Dafny-based tool generates history-preserving asynchronous protocol models and mechanically checked provenance, monotonicity and ownership invariants. Atomic sharding traces current local values or collection entries to past steps and messages. Users supply host definitions, selected hints/labels and final safety proofs. Evaluation reports 16 protocol-model proofs, with 10 Kondo comparisons, final proof sizes and verifier times. Reconstructs host/network provenance and the atomic-shard algorithm, collection correlation failure and epoch-reset counterexample. Records formal safety obligations, all 16 protocol properties and every numerical comparison cell. Separates generated invariant proofs from user safety proofs, model correctness from implementation correspondence, and measured final LOC from development effort. Preserves incompleteness, syntax restrictions and all trust limits.
    - number: 16 protocols
    - number: Paxos: 418 lines, 0 user invariants, 486 proof lines, 24.6 s
    - number: Multi-Paxos: 447 lines, 0 user invariants, 565 proof lines, 61.5 s
    - number: Paxos row: 418 model lines, no user invariant clauses, five monotonicity annotations, no provenance hints, 444 safety-lemma lines, 486 total proof lines, 24.6 verification seconds
    - limits: §§3,5.2: Model has arbitrary host counts, nondeterministic single-host atomic steps, history of all Hosts snapshots and a monotonically growing set of sent messages. Receive selects an addressed message already sent; repeat selection and nondelivery overapproximate duplication, delay, reordering and loss. Messages carry sender identities; arbitrary Byzantine forgery or compromised hosts are not modeled. History is proof state, not a measured runtime logging cost. §3: Network provenance links a sent message to an adjacent pair of historical sender states and a matching send step. Host provenance for witness q requires q now, no initial state satisfying q, and a historical crossing from not-q to q through a candidate modifying step. These statements concern well-formed initialized protocol executions, not arbitrary malformed history sequences. §§3.3,5.3: Provenance alone identifies a past producing event. Monotonicity/non-equivocation invariants are needed to move from past sender conditions to present facts. Ownership invariants/labels are inherited from Kondo; generated invariants are individually/local mechanically checked, not assertions trusted merely because the generator produced them. §4.1: An atomic shard is a subset of local variables always modified together. If the current tuple is not an admissible initial tuple, it was established in one candidate update step. Maximal shards retain shared-message correlations lost by independent singleton witnesses. This does not mean every member of a collection remains correlated with the latest scalar value. §4.2: Static footprints overapproximate variables a step may update; excess candidates weaken the resulting provenance disjunction. Underestimating footprints would produce incorrect invariants. Prototype rejects a step updating different variables under different conditions, e.g. if c then x:=5 else y:=6. User must split this into separately guarded steps. §§4.2,5.3: Collection fields also receive refined singleton shards, allowing each noninitial set/map entry to be traced to its insertion step. Prototype supports explicit Dafny record-update syntax only, rather than general relational updates. A step can receive and send together; multiple sends/broadcasts in one step are described as an easy extension but not implemented or evaluated. §5.4 reader qualification: Formal obligations are Init⇒I∧φ and I∧φ∧Next⇒I′∧φ′. Thus the actual method proves safety with induction on I∧φ and may use current φ in the step proof. Figure 5/prose describing a proof that I implies safety must not be read as requiring or establishing the stronger standalone I⇒φ in every example. The final safety proof remains user-written. §§5.4,6.4: Failure may reflect a too-weak generated invariant or an unsafe model; the verifier points to the failing host-step obligation, but does not automatically decide which explanation is true. Reader qualification: SMT proof failure can also arise from solver incompleteness. Additional provenance witnesses or manually written invariant clauses may be needed; Basilisk is explicitly incomplete for undecidable logics. §6: First six protocols were used to develop/refine provenance and atomic sharding; the remaining ten were applied later. All sixteen models/invariants are reported outside EPR. This is an author-built benchmark suite, not a blinded independent user study or proof of completeness for all protocols outside EPR. §§6.1–6.2: Zero manually supplied invariant clauses across 16 does not mean zero user reasoning. Six of 64 host-provenance invariants need explicit witness hints, across Paxos-Dynamic, Raft election and Multi-Paxos; monotonicity annotations, ownership labels and safety lemmas remain. Distributed Lock and both ShardedKV variants already had fully automatic Kondo ownership proofs. §6.2: Kondo comparisons cover only ten protocols and modify models to split receive/send; Kondo also needs synchronous protocol definitions. Final proof LOC excludes failed/revised proof attempts and does not measure person-time, cognitive difficulty or novice success. Basilisk uses more proof LOC for some simple cases and is not faster for every ownership example. §6.3: Timings check final history-preserving asynchronous proofs including generated/user invariants and lemmas; Kondo synchronous-proof verification is excluded. Invariant generation is under two seconds in every case. Table 1 reports Multi-Paxos 61.5 seconds; the prose phrase in a minute is approximate, not a strict sub-60-second bound. No repetitions/variance or controlled proof-authoring experiment is reported. §6.4: Epoch resets create implicit multi-step relations that sharding cannot infer. Adding an event at current year and clearing events on year increment requires a witness relating every current entry to current year; merely finding its historic insertion year is too weak. All three hinted benchmark protocols have collection resets at epoch changes. Footprint imprecision is another possible weakness, not observed by authors in these protocols. §6.4: Safety specification is trusted. The user must trust the model describes actual code or separately prove correspondence; this work does not verify production implementations. Dafny, dependencies and hardware are trusted. Crash-fault safety is the scope; no liveness or Byzantine correctness proof is provided. §§6–8,Appendix A: No runtime throughput, production bug-finding or LLM-agent experiment is evaluated. Artifact developed/tested on M3 MacBook Pro with MacOS Sequoia; this appendix statement is not an explicit platform declaration for every published timing. Artifact and external proof bodies were not executed in this reading.
    - how much was read: most of it read
    - [source](https://www.usenix.org/conference/osdi25/presentation/zhang-tony)
    - record id: `basilisk-2025`
- CCF smart casual verification (2025)
    - title: Smart Casual Verification of the Confidential Consortium Framework (CCF)
    - what: Industrial experience applying TLA+ specification/model checking, weighted simulation and implementation trace validation to the existing C++ Confidential Consortium Framework. Separate node-consensus and client-history specifications are aligned with production protocol details and incorporated in CI. The paper reports six consensus bugs found/fixed and a clarified read-only consistency limitation, plus tooling/effort and state-exploration measurements. Reconstructs all six safety/liveness cases and the unsuccessful first fix, distinguishes trace compatibility from universal implementation refinement, records granularity alignment via composition/stuttering and incomplete-state reconstruction. Saves all scale/coverage rows, effort figures, counterexample sizes and issue pointers. Preserves TEE/model trust, bounded exploration and non-linearizable read-only semantics; no all-executions C++ correctness theorem is claimed.
    - number: Six subtle bugs found, five concerning safety and one liveness
    - number: 400 LoC Trace spec; 88 commits to it and 107 changes to the consensus spec; implementation 63 kLoC C++
    - number: six subtle bugs found
    - number: 63 kLoC in C++ (§2)
    - limits: §§1–2: CCF runs application logic inside trusted execution environments while treating operators, host OS/hypervisor, network and storage as corruptible. This paper checks distributed protocol behavior under modeled failures, not hardware/firmware isolation, enclave security, cryptography, all 63k C++ LOC, or arbitrary Byzantine behavior of the protected consensus code. Production use in Azure Confidential Ledger is context, not evidence that every production behavior is covered. §§1.1,4,8: Smart casual verification combines formal specification, finite model checking/simulation and tests/trace checks. Consensus spec is unbounded but exhaustive checking restricts terms, requests and reconfiguration sequences. No TLAPS safety/liveness proof for all sizes or universal code-refinement proof is reported. Most CI exploration relies on bounded simulations because sufficiently realistic exhaustive exploration is too expensive. §2: CCF returns transaction results before replication; clients separately query Pending/Committed/Invalid status. A returned result may later be invalidated. Committed read-write transactions are strictly serializable; committed read-only transactions are serializable and may be stale. Pending branches offer fork-like consistency rather than arbitrary results. Broad prose about all committed transactions being linearizable needs the explicit read-only exception. §2: Transaction IDs are lexicographic term/index pairs. If two transactions commit, ID ordering agrees with execution ordering. Ancestor Commit says committed <t,i> implies same-term <t,j>, j≤i, committed. This concerns transaction identity/status, not a proof that any stale read immediately observes the newest committed write. §2.1: CCF customizes Raft with signed Merkle-root transactions, messaging without RPC correspondence, optimistic sentIndex advances, express catch-up, CheckQuorum, bootstrapping/reconfiguration and retirement/ProposeVote. A transaction is committed only after a subsequent signature is committed. The actual combined protocol needs verification; citing the base Raft proof does not establish these extensions. §2.1 reader qualification: Early description says an AE-ACK carries the last transaction in the follower log, while the Inaccurate AE-ACK bug/fix later constrains that index to the end of the received AE. The historical/protocol-description distinction must be retained rather than treating the early sentence as the final safe response rule. §§2.1,4: Reconfiguration needs majorities in each active configuration, rather than a majority of their union. A removed node remains retiring until retirement information is safely committed for future leaders. CheckQuorum steps down an isolated leader, but absent delivery/scheduling assumptions this is not a theorem that all executions make progress. §4: Consensus has 17 actions/13 variables, 12 local-state mappings plus network. Parameterization includes every nonempty initial node subset with any member as initial leader. Locally initiated timeout/signature/candidacy/step-down choices impose no clock synchrony. LogInv compares pairwise committed-log prefixes; AppendOnlyProp prevents committed-prefix rollback over time. Another 27 properties include MonoLogInv, requiring term increases only after signatures. §4: Simulation explores behaviors to bounded depth under a time quota; authors manually downweight failures to promote forward progress. Q-learning was implemented in TLC but no state-hash feature set beat manual weighting. More progress-rich traces may help find bugs but do not constitute exhaustive failure coverage or proof of liveness. §5: Client model uses append-only history and logBranches, independent of node count. History includes read-only/read-write requests/responses and status responses, but omits status requests/Pending replies. A simple shared value appends transaction identifiers so conflicts and observations are visible. It is a deliberately selected application workload, not a proved reduction of all arbitrary CCF application semantics. §5: A new branch can be any prefix of an existing branch containing the last committed transaction. Requests may execute at any still-active leader/term. PrevCommittedInv checks same-term committed ancestor statuses; ObservedRoInv demands that a subsequently committed read-only response observe a read-write transaction whose response preceded its request. The latter expectation is falsified and documented, not a proved CCF invariant. §6.1: Scenario driver serializes consensus execution deterministically and mocks governance/networking/other components, injecting loss/reordering/partition/delay. Original 13 scenarios and about 1.6k unit-test/2k Python end-to-end LOC precede the effort; Table 1 gives later exact scope counts. Initial fuzzing was abandoned for insufficient interesting coverage. Driver/mock accuracy is a boundary, as demonstrated by retirement bugs. §6.1: Driver uses a global logical ordering clock; distributed Lamport/vector alternatives are suggested but not implemented here. Fifteen added logging statements capture stable states at message and role-change points, immediately after a global lock for state changes. Constant-space values such as log lengths are recorded, not full log entries. Production logging compiles out, so the checks use instrumented test executions rather than continuous production monitoring. §6.1/§6.5: Traces initially remove/deduplicate bootstrapping events because early consensus spec omits that phase; later specification is expanded to model bootstrapping more faithfully. These describe successive coverage stages. A successful preprocessed trace validates only its retained observations and modeling assumptions, not excluded initialization behavior. §6.2: Trace spec checks existence of a spec behavior consistent with observed events/values: T∩S is nonempty. Nondeterministically reconstructed hidden state is existential. A successful finite trace check is evidence for those observations, not proof that all completions, all execution traces or unlogged state transitions meet the spec. If logging or mappings omit a decisive distinction, an incorrect implementation could remain compatible. §6.2: Send actions constrain current state, invoke high-level action and assert matching successor messages. Network changes from set to multiset to represent duplicate/resend occurrences. Trace network abstractions also explore ordering/duplication/loss guarantees. Such abstraction choices affect what is observable and accepted; they are not runtime network guarantees inferred from traces. §6.2.1 Listing 5: UpdateTerm·HandleAppendEntriesReq composes two model actions into one implementation receive. IsFault·Next admits unlogged network faults before a step; send-response events may stutter high-level variables when already modeled by another action. Composition hides intermediate states and stuttering changes observation granularity, so correctness depends on the mapping retaining semantically important events. Merely making a mismatch disappear is not an independent proof. §6.2.2–6.3: Start with a happy-path trace, add many assertions and cross-references, debug source and formulas. Invalid traces may indicate bogus logging, incorrect mapping, specification error or implementation bug; failure lacks an ordinary model-checking counterexample. Longest matching prefixes, unreachable-state graphs, failing subformulas and an unsatisfied breakpoint support diagnosis. §6.4: DFS stops after finding one compatible behavior, avoiding BFS enumeration of alternatives; a consistency trace goes from about one hour to under a second. This is an author example without trace-size distribution or controlled speedup suite, and does not mean all traces validate in under a second. Existential acceptance is essential to that optimization. §6.5: Logging/driver enhancements took about one day. Consensus alignment/tooling took two engineer-months over four months, including TLC composition/DFS/debug support, discrepancy diagnosis and abstraction design. Writing about 400 lines of Trace was minor; Table 1 counts 369. Trace saw 88 commits and consensus spec 107 changes. This is observed project effort, not a controlled productivity study. §6.5: Client consistency validation uses REST observations without source instrumentation, reconstructs other clients’ hidden transactions from observed IDs, and took about one engineer-week over two weeks, mainly by CCF experts. Smaller spec, previous experience and reused TLC changes confound a simple comparison with initial consensus effort. §7 Table 1: Approximate state counts/rates are measured on Azure DC8s v3. A log line is largely equivalent to a model action, according to authors, not a formal equality of semantic coverage. Different abstraction sizes, bounds, durations and distinct/generated-state counting prevent treating orders-of-magnitude state counts as a calibrated defect-detection probability. §7: Six serious consensus bugs comprise five safety issues and one liveness issue, found by combined modeling, source scrutiny, trace mismatch, simulation and functional/end-to-end reproduction. They are not six independent discoveries by model checking alone. Read-only non-linearizability is an additional documented consistency ambiguity and accepted behavior, not a seventh fixed consensus bug. §§7–8: Authors report all six fixed before user impact/customer reports; software had already been deployed. One-node-at-a-time operational reconfiguration masked the election bug, and absence of an incident from the first bad commit fix was attributed to chance. No external incident audit, current repository verification or independent bug reproduction was done in this reading. §§8–9: Paper chooses model checking for developer accessibility rather than deductive proof, despite compute cost. AI-assisted proof search is future possibility with a reference to neural theorem proving, not an experiment using LLM agents. Related-work sentence says IronFleet and Verdi both proved Raft; reader qualification: IronFleet uses a Paxos-based replicated state machine, so do not propagate that comparison as fact.
    - how much was read: most of it read
    - [source](https://www.usenix.org/conference/nsdi25/presentation/howard)

- CLEVER (2025)
    - title: CLEVER: A Curated Benchmark for Formally Verified Code Generation
    - what: CLEVER is a Lean benchmark. A model generates a spec, which must be proved equivalent to a hidden, human-written reference spec. The model then generates code, which must be proved against that reference. The reference specs are noncomputable so that models cannot copy code logic.
    - number: 161 problems (from 164 HumanEval; 3 excluded)
    - number: default budget 600 seconds for pass@k-seconds
    - number: human effort per problem: 25 minutes plus an additional 15 minutes, in places over an hour
    - number: reference proofs 10-225 lines
    - limits: It depends on the quality of the reference. Failed equivalence proofs give false negatives. HumanEval data may have leaked into training. It covers only list and numeric tasks, and has no liveness.
    - how much was read: most of it read

    - record id: `clever-2025`
- Crossword (2025)
    - title: Crossword: Adaptive Consensus for Dynamic Data-Heavy Workloads
    - what: # Crossword: Adaptive Consensus for Dynamic Data-Heavy Workloads (authored by agents unless marked 🧑)  Guanzhou Hu, Yiwei Chen, Andrea C. Arpaci-Dusseau and Remzi H. Arpaci-Dusseau, arXiv:2509.07157v1, 8 September 2025. This reading covers all 30 PDF pages: design, implementation, every evaluation, discussion, 144 references, complete PlusCal/TLA+ specification and model-checking configuration. All eight contact sheets and close views of pages 5, 8, 10–12 and 19–30 were read. PDF and printed page numbers agree. The findings below distinguish author reports from static reader reasoning; no author code or checker was run. [Evidence: Q0, title, abstract and §1, PDF pp.1–2; Q24, Appendix A model-check report and explicit finite scope, PDF pp.19–19; Q37, Appendix A complete Crossword MC.cfg, PDF pp.30–30; literal spans in quotes.json.]  Crossword changes how much erasure-coded data each replica must durably store before a log entry commits. More data per replica permits a smaller reply quorum; less data requires more replies. Its motivating Figure 1 profiles Raft payloads under 200-warehouse TPC-C in TiDB and CockroachDB: “~45% are 4KB” and the distributions reach 290 KB and 63 MB, respectively. The paper derives a shard-survival condition, combines it with majority voting, and implements per-entry adaptation in Rust and in a CockroachDB Go prototype. Followers exchange missing shards after commitment. Its evidence is algebraic design reasoning, a reported finite model check, tests and system measurements. The paper says, “It has been model-checked for both linearizability and fault-tolerance properties” and immediately names the finite inputs. The artifact supplies neither an unbounded proof nor a refinement from the model to Rust or Go. [Evidence: Q4, §3.3 complete derivation and Figure 4, PDF pp.5–5; Q7, §3.6 complete protocol changes, PDF pp.7–7; Q8, §4 implementation and Gazette/CockroachDB, PDF pp.7–7; Q24, Appendix A model-check report and explicit finite scope, PDF pp.19–19; Q37, Appendix A complete Crossword MC.cfg, PDF pp.30–30; literal spans in quotes.json.]  ## Assumptions and the actual contract  The system model uses an odd number n of replicas, asynchronous communication and fail-stop failures. Set m=ceil(n/2) and f=n−m. Balanced policies use Reed–Solomon (n,m) coding, so any m distinct shards reconstruct a payload. A vote means both acceptance of a ballot and durable storage of its assigned shards: “s votes yes to the ballot number of this Accept message and has durably remembered the shards it carried.” The leader retains the entire payload in memory but persists only its assignment. Background gossip remains in memory. Restart recovery, Byzantine behavior and cryptographic shard authenticity are outside this model. Time-based read leases separately assume bounded clock drift; the asynchronous network model alone does not establish lease correctness. [Evidence: Q1, §2 complete background, system model and motivation, PDF pp.2–3; Q3, §3.1–3.2 assignments and Figure 3, PDF pp.4–5; Q4, §3.3 complete derivation and Figure 4, PDF pp.5–5; Q6, §3.5 gossip and Figure 5, PDF pp.6–7; Q11, §4.3 heartbeats, snapshots and leases, PDF pp.8–8; literal spans in quotes.json.]  For an acceptance pattern ap, C1 requires at least m distinct replying nodes, including the leader. C2 requires at least d distinct recoverable shards after removing any f replying nodes, with d=m for the balanced family. Majority intersection preserves the voting argument; the second condition preserves payload availability after the allowed crashes. These are different obligations. C2 alone does not prove preservation of the chosen value across changing ballots and assignments, and neither condition promises termination in an arbitrary asynchronous execution. [Evidence: Q4, §3.3 complete derivation and Figure 4, PDF pp.5–5; Q7, §3.6 complete protocol changes, PDF pp.7–7; Q28, Appendix A PreparedConditionAndCommand, PDF pp.22–23; literal spans in quotes.json.]  Balanced round-robin policies assign c consecutive shard indices to each node, wrapping around the n indices, with 1≤c≤m. The derived valid region is n≥q≥m and q+c≥n+1; the candidate performance boundary is q+c=n+1. For n=5,m=3,f=2, its three choices are (c,q)=(1,5),(2,4),(3,3). Full replication and single-shard coding become endpoints of one family. Section 3.2’s written range s through s+c would contain c+1 shards if both endpoints were included; the figures and Appendix A’s upper endpoint ShardToIdx[r]+na−1 establish the intended c-shard interpretation. Figure 2 gives the motivating failure: with single shards and q=4, crashing the leader and another accepting node leaves only two shards, below the three needed. The paper's RSPaxos/CRaft comparisons commonly use f=1, whereas Crossword retains f=2. Their throughput comparisons therefore include different availability guarantees, which the graphs label. [Evidence: Q2, §2.4 failure example, Figure 2, PDF pp.3–3; Q3, §3.1–3.2 assignments and Figure 3, PDF pp.4–5; Q4, §3.3 complete derivation and Figure 4, PDF pp.5–5; Q5, §3.4 tradeoff, PDF pp.6–6; literal spans in quotes.json.]  One printed intermediate bound needs a saturation qualification. The paper writes SubCover(ap,f)≥q−f+c−1, but shard coverage cannot exceed n. Reader example: n=5,q=5,c=3,f=0 gives a right side of seven although only five distinct shards exist. The round-robin lower bound should be capped by n. The later threshold test against m≤n, including q+c≥n+1 at f=n−m, is unaffected. This is arithmetic about the displayed derivation, not an implementation finding. [Evidence: Q4, §3.3 complete derivation and Figure 4, PDF pp.5–5; literal spans in quotes.json.]  ## Protocol, adaptation and concrete cases  Each Accept message carries the chosen per-instance assignment; recipients persist their assigned bytes before acknowledging. On failover, a majority Prepare exchange preserves a reconstructible highest-ballot value; the paper permits replacing it with a new command if fewer than d shards of that value can be recovered. The appendix selects a maximum-ballot command and combines reported shards for that command, including matches at other ballots. A complete correctness argument must connect that rule to chosen-value preservation over all earlier ballots, not just count surviving shards in one acceptance pattern. This reading identifies that proof obligation without claiming a counterexample. [Evidence: Q3, §3.1–3.2 assignments and Figure 3, PDF pp.4–5; Q7, §3.6 complete protocol changes, PDF pp.7–7; Q28, Appendix A PreparedConditionAndCommand, PDF pp.22–23; literal spans in quotes.json.]  Figure 5 distinguishes committed-but-partial data from committed-and-known data and execution. Followers periodically request missing shards from peers; reconstructed shards are memory-only. Requests are batched about every 20 ms and normally exclude the last 400 KB of accumulated payloads. A peer without a reconstruction reply for ten cycles is skipped. A new leader reconstructs its partial tail synchronously. Low-traffic tails, retrying skipped peers, eventual delivery and leader recovery consequently matter to progress; the text provides mechanisms and experiments rather than a temporal proof covering them. [Evidence: Q6, §3.5 gossip and Figure 5, PDF pp.6–7; Q10, §4.2 gossip details and Figure 6, PDF pp.8–8; Q11, §4.3 heartbeats, snapshots and leases, PDF pp.8–8; literal spans in quotes.json.]  The default chooser fits per-follower response time t_s(v)=d_s+v/b_s from a two-second sliding window, updates every 200 ms, and discards the slowest five percent of observations. It considers balanced assignments and uses the (q−1)th smallest follower response-time estimate, because the leader already counts toward q. The stated limitations are no automated unbalanced search and no reaction to fluctuations shorter than roughly one second. Heartbeats and random 300–600 ms election timeouts track failures; the healthy-node count limits q. The CockroachDB integration instead uses payload thresholds, not this regression chooser. [Evidence: Q8, §4 implementation and Gazette/CockroachDB, PDF pp.7–7; Q9, §4.1 complete regression chooser and limitations, PDF pp.7–8; Q11, §4.3 heartbeats, snapshots and leases, PDF pp.8–8; literal spans in quotes.json.]  A concrete source inconsistency occurs in §4.1, PDF p.8: the chooser evaluates t_s(v_p/c). Each of c assigned shards actually has v_p/m bytes, so the bytes per recipient are c·v_p/m. With n=5,m=3, c=1 sends v_p/3 but the printed expression predicts v_p; c=3 sends v_p but predicts v_p/3. Thus the printed expression reverses the size trend. It should not be used as the specification of an implementation without resolving the mismatch. No repository revision was inspected, so this is not a claim about executed code or the validity of measured results. [Evidence: Q3, §3.1–3.2 assignments and Figure 3, PDF pp.4–5; Q9, §4.1 complete regression chooser and limitations, PDF pp.7–8; literal spans in quotes.json.]  ## Implementations and measured evidence  Gazette is async Rust/Tokio with channels. The paper lists 12.7k infrastructure lines and six implementations: Chain Replication 1.1k, MultiPaxos 2.3k, Raft 2.3k, RSPaxos 2.5k, CRaft 2.6k and Crossword 3.4k. It reports “extensive unit tests and fuzz tests” but gives no counts, fault schedules or coverage. The Go prototype modifies CockroachDB v24.3.0a by approximately 1.6k lines. Those listed amounts total 26.9k lines, slightly below §1’s “a sum of 27.3k lines of async Rust”; the paper does not reconcile the counting scope. The paper promises to open-source the code; this reading establishes no repository version or reproduction. [Evidence: Q8, §4 implementation and Gazette/CockroachDB, PDF pp.7–7; literal spans in quotes.json.]  Gazette experiments use CloudLab c220g2 hosts, 40 cores, 160 GB RAM and Linux 6.1.64. The regional setting has 1 Gbps links and 4 ms average delay; the wide-area setting spans CloudLab datacenters with about 200 Mbps and 30 ms average delay. Clients share hosts with servers but use disjoint pinned cores; batching is one millisecond. Default static tests have five servers and fifteen closed-loop clients. Requests are half lease-local Gets and half Puts, with eight-byte keys and small, 128 KB or mixed payloads whose normal distributions have ten percent standard deviation. Reported throughput includes reads; latency curves show means and arrows mark P95, not confidence intervals. [Evidence: Q12, §5 evaluation questions and §5.1 setup, PDF pp.8–9; Q13, §5.1 Figure 7 complete static comparisons, PDF pp.9–9; literal spans in quotes.json.]  Figures 7–9 report that Crossword follows the favorable endpoint across small, large and mixed data: up to 1.9× coded-protocol throughput for small data, 2× MultiPaxos for large data, and 2.1× alternatives in the mixed workload. Figure 8 varies payload means from 8 B to 256 KB, replica counts 3/5/7/9 and write shares 10/50/100%; at ≥64 KB, the chosen c=1,q=5 case exceeds MultiPaxos throughput by four times in the plotted setting. Figure 8’s cluster-size/Put-share sensitivity reports “up to 2.3x over MultiPaxos” and 1.4× over RSPaxos in its tested settings. These are workload-specific ratios, not universal bounds. At 64 KB Figure 9 reports 71% less critical-path data-transfer/WAL time and logs of 913 MB versus 1,467 MB, a reader-calculated 37.76% storage reduction. [Evidence: Q13, §5.1 Figure 7 complete static comparisons, PDF pp.9–9; Q14, §5.1–5.2 Figures 8–9 and storage/critical-path measurements, PDF pp.9–10; literal spans in quotes.json.]  Figure 10 changes conditions about fifteen seconds apart: payload size 64→4 KB, bandwidth 1 Gbps→100 Mbps→1 Gbps, and then two replicas with tenfold worse delay and bandwidth. Throughput is sampled once per second. Adaptation is shown by time traces, not a regret or convergence guarantee. Figure 11 crashes leaders at 15 and 55 seconds, uses a special 1.5-second timeout and disables snapshots. Crossword's first recovery gap is about twice classic protocols' gap because of tail reconstruction; afterward it selects c=2,q=4. RSPaxos becomes unavailable after the second crash, whereas CRaft completes its fallback before it. These particular separated failures do not test every crash spacing. [Evidence: Q15, §5.2 Figure 10 adaptation measurements, PDF pp.10–10; Q16, §5.3 Figure 11 crash measurements, PDF pp.10–11; literal spans in quotes.json.]  Figure 12 manually chooses an unbalanced assignment for follower links of 1/1/0.4/0.1 Gbps, using the (8,5) codeword example with 5/5/3/1 assigned shards. It obtains roughly 1.4× MultiPaxos or balanced Crossword throughput. Automated unbalanced adaptation remains future work. Figure 13 forces c=1 and compares 0/400/800 KB deferral gaps at 3/10/100 ms write intervals, measuring follower version distance from the leader. Section 5.5 says, “The replies are not linearizable but sequentially consistent,” followed by “possibly stale versions in the history”. This is evidence about staleness, not a history-based proof of the text's sequential-consistency claim. Figure 14, at fixed throughput, reports 13% less follower-to-follower bandwidth from batching; it moves two-thirds of payload replication to that background path. The 13% is not a reduction of all network traffic. [Evidence: Q17, §5.4 Figure 12 unbalanced assignment, PDF pp.10–11; Q18, §5.5 Figures 13–14 staleness and gossip traffic, PDF pp.10–11; literal spans in quotes.json.]  Figure 15 uses YCSB-A key popularity on 1,000 keys, but all requests are Puts with a TiDB-derived size distribution. Five partitions rotate leaders over the same five regional servers. Crossword's peak throughput exceeds MultiPaxos but falls below RSPaxos and CRaft, which omit its background gossip. Chain Replication has the highest unsaturated latency from chain propagation and does not reach its throughput limit over the plotted load range. This experiment tests shared follower bandwidth, not the usual half-read YCSB-A operation mix. [Evidence: Q19, §5.6 Figure 15 partitioned-store experiment, PDF pp.11–12; literal spans in quotes.json.]  The CockroachDB TPC-C experiment uses twelve nodes, 200 warehouses, five replicas per range and 400 workers. It disables leader/leaseholder rebalancing and write pipelining. The chooser uses two shards above 4 KB and one above 8 KB. Aggregate throughput is 2,520 tps for vanilla, 3,330 for Crossword and 2,450 for an always-one-shard variant; reader arithmetic gives 32.14% gain over vanilla. The reported “up to 44%” refers to transaction-specific speedup. Table 1 separately measures RS(5,3) coding on 32 cores with SIMD: 4/16/64/256 KB and 1/4 MB take 1/4/16/77 μs and 1/6 ms; reported per-request leader CPU overhead is about 1.24–1.26%, with reported memory 1.6 KB through 1.6 MB. These costs have a different measurement denominator from throughput ratios. Repetition counts, warm-up and confidence intervals are not reported; the dynamic plots show their trace duration. [Evidence: Q20, §5.7 Figure 16 CockroachDB experiment, PDF pp.11–12; Q21, §5.8 Table 1 coding overhead, PDF pp.11–12; literal spans in quotes.json.]  ## What the formal appendix actually checks  Appendix A reports three replicas, at most one failure, two ballots, three requests and both balanced choices over three shards with two data shards. The supplied configuration specifically has three writes, no reads, CommitNoticeOn=FALSE and NodeFailuresOn, replica/write symmetry, TypeOK and Linearizability invariants, and deadlock checking. It gives no TLC version, explored-state counts, timing or run log. “These inputs should be large enough to explore all the distinct, interesting execution paths of the protocol” is the authors' belief; no parameterized theorem justifies it. In particular, three nodes cannot instantiate the interior five-node policy c=2,q=4 or the unbalanced example. [Evidence: Q24, Appendix A model-check report and explicit finite scope, PDF pp.19–19; Q27, Appendix A coverage and valid round-robin assignments, PDF pp.21–22; Q37, Appendix A complete Crossword MC.cfg, PDF pp.30–30; literal spans in quotes.json.]  The PlusCal model represents shard identifiers, abstract command values and votes. It does not encode Reed–Solomon byte algebra, disk operations, the Rust or Go program, gossip/reconstruction messages, the chooser, leases, snapshots or reconfiguration. The single rloop label makes the macros atomic transitions; messages accumulate in a set. Nodes fail permanently. ReplicaCrashes additionally requires balMaxKnown<MaxBallot, suppressing crashes after learning the maximum modeled ballot: “∧node[r].balMaxKnown < MaxBallot” (Appendix A, PDF p.28). This is an explicit state-space restriction, not the protocol's ordinary fail-stop rule. [Evidence: Q25, Appendix A constants, assumptions and state types, PDF pp.20–21; Q27, Appendix A coverage and valid round-robin assignments, PDF pp.21–22; Q29, Appendix A message types and commitment condition, PDF pp.22–24; Q35, Appendix A remaining process and MC definitions, PDF pp.29–29; Q34, Appendix A crash guard and process loop, PDF pp.28–29; literal spans in quotes.json.]  The model's Linearizability predicate is conditional: “terminated ⇒” a complete order exists that respects observed real-time precedence and old values. Terminated means no pending requests and all requests acknowledged. Therefore the checked expression constrains fully completed runs; arbitrary incomplete finite histories are not checked by that expression. Writes return their old value, so the all-write model still has meaningful observable ordering constraints, but it does not exercise read operations or leased local reads. Request identities are unique, admission picks the first unseen pending command, and InitPending fixes writes before reads under a stated W.L.O.G. reduction. Its comment says, “all other cases are either symmetric or less useful” (Appendix A, PDF p.21). No theorem establishes that reduction for a larger mixed workload. [Evidence: Q26, Appendix A initial request order and server states, PDF pp.21–21; Q30, Appendix A main state, termination and observation helpers, PDF pp.24–24; Q32, Appendix A complete new-request and Accept handling, PDF pp.26–27; Q36, Appendix A complete observable-order predicate, PDF pp.30–30; Q37, Appendix A complete Crossword MC.cfg, PDF pp.30–30; literal spans in quotes.json.]  No fairness condition or eventual-completion temporal property is supplied. CHECK_DEADLOCK tests the absence of an enabled next step under TLC's deadlock semantics; it is not a proof that every admissible execution eventually completes. With CommitNoticeOn=FALSE, the loop can select a branch whose conditional body does nothing while a node remains live, illustrating why a no-progress execution need not be a deadlock. The displayed theorem statements have no TLAPS proof. Thus the author's “fault-tolerance” report must be read together with the configured invariant, finite crash restriction and deadlock check, rather than promoted to unbounded liveness. [Evidence: Q34, Appendix A crash guard and process loop, PDF pp.28–29; Q35, Appendix A remaining process and MC definitions, PDF pp.29–29; Q36, Appendix A complete observable-order predicate, PDF pp.30–30; Q37, Appendix A complete Crossword MC.cfg, PDF pp.30–30; literal spans in quotes.json.]  Two further model-to-design obligations are concrete. TakeNewRequest puts all Shards in the leader's voted.shards while its own AcceptReply records only assign[r]; the prose persists only assigned shards but expressly keeps the complete codeword in leader memory. Since failures are fail-stop with no restart, the abstract full-shard knowledge is consistent with a live leader’s memory. A refinement must explain that knowledge and its lifetime; this difference does not establish a crash-recovery durability counterexample. CommitNotice has only an index, no ballot or origin, and its handler applies a local command without shard reconstruction; the supplied configuration disables that handler. These are source differences and missing coverage, not checked exploit traces. The prepared-value fallback also spells a field “shard” rather than “shards”; HandlePrepareReplies awaits exam[s].prepared for every slot before reading shards, so that false-prepared branch alone does not establish a missing-field failure. No translator was run. The handler also overwrites cmd/shards while preserving a Committed status and sends its own reply before its voted record is updated by any later self-Accept. Chosen-value preservation and consistent self-voting must justify those intermediate states; no reachable counterexample is established. [Evidence: Q3, §3.1–3.2 assignments and Figure 3, PDF pp.4–5; Q28, Appendix A PreparedConditionAndCommand, PDF pp.22–23; Q29, Appendix A message types and commitment condition, PDF pp.22–24; Q32, Appendix A complete new-request and Accept handling, PDF pp.26–27; Q33, Appendix A commit and CommitNotice handlers, PDF pp.27–28; Q37, Appendix A complete Crossword MC.cfg, PDF pp.30–30; literal spans in quotes.json.]  ## Contribution to the human's topic  For distributed verification, this is a useful case separating voting intersection from durable data reconstructibility while policies change per instance. A proof would need cross-ballot chosen-value preservation, shard-survival and data-known/executed invariants, plus explicit assumptions for eventual gossip and failover. TLA+ can express the distributed contracts; Verus could connect assignment bitmaps, persistence-before-ack and reconstruction to Rust execution. Those are reader-proposed next steps, not supplied proofs. The static chooser mismatch gives a precise specification-to-code question. [Evidence: Q4, §3.3 complete derivation and Figure 4, PDF pp.5–5; Q7, §3.6 complete protocol changes, PDF pp.7–7; Q9, §4.1 complete regression chooser and limitations, PDF pp.7–8; Q8, §4 implementation and Gazette/CockroachDB, PDF pp.7–7; literal spans in quotes.json.]  For evaluating LLM-generated specifications or proofs, the artifact offers concrete scope checks: whether an agent notices the all-write/CommitNoticeOn=FALSE configuration, completed-run predicate, ballot-dependent crash restriction, atomic actions, idealized shards and leader-knowledge abstraction. A convincing generated proof would have to state which model and which property it proves and show the link to implementation. The paper makes no claim of LLM-generated specifications, proofs or evaluation, and references Verus only as related verification work. Its strongest contribution here is a nontrivial Rust systems case with a published finite specification and measurable adaptation, together with explicit gaps a later proof effort must close. [Evidence: Q22, §6 discussion and §7 conclusion, PDF pp.12–13; Q24, Appendix A model-check report and explicit finite scope, PDF pp.19–19; Q32, Appendix A complete new-request and Accept handling, PDF pp.26–27; Q34, Appendix A crash guard and process loop, PDF pp.28–29; Q36, Appendix A complete observable-order predicate, PDF pp.30–30; Q37, Appendix A complete Crossword MC.cfg, PDF pp.30–30; literal spans in quotes.json.]
    - limits: Odd n, fail-stop, asynchronous network; majority m=ceil(n/2), f=n−m; balanced RS(n,m), c consecutive wrapped shards, q+c≥n+1. Durable assigned shards before ack. Bounded clock drift separately assumed for read leases. Printed chooser uses inverse shard count instead of assigned bytes; intermediate coverage bound needs saturation; cross-ballot preservation proof obligation; finite formal scope/reductions/crash restriction/completed-run predicate; leader-knowledge abstraction requires refinement; disabled commit notices; no Rust refinement; heterogeneous policies manual; <1s fluctuations outside chooser scope; f1 baselines differ; staleness not a consistency proof. Prose RR interval endpoint differs from intended c-shard range; listed line counts total26.9k versus headline27.3k; prepared-state overwrite/self-vote obligations remain conditional static observations.
    - how much was read: most of it read
    - [source](https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.07157v1)
    - record id: `crossword-2025`
- DafnyBench (2025)
    - title: DafnyBench: A Benchmark for Formal Software Verification
    - what: Asks LLMs to restore missing assertions and loop invariants in Dafny programs that already have specs. The pass rate measures whether the proof gets finished. It does not measure whether the intent was formalized.
    - number: 782 Dafny programs
    - number: 782 verified ground-truth files; over 750 programs and about 53,000 lines (abstract)
    - number: about 68% for the best model/prompt scheme
    - number: built with Dafny 4.3.0
    - limits: Only bans {:verify false} and assume false. This does not show that every way to cheat is caught. The public GitHub source may be in training data. Some specs just repeat the implementation. Scoring generated specs is only proposed (Appendix C). Verifying a weak contract that was handed in counts as success.
    - how much was read: most of it read

- DafnyComp (2025)
    - title: Local Success Does Not Compose: Benchmarking Large Language Models for Compositional Formal Verification
    - what: The benchmark has 300 Dafny programs written by LLMs. Each has 2 to 5 chained functions, with contracts removed. The model must rebuild the contracts that connect the functions.
    - number: 300 programs selected from 564 verified of 1,200 attempts
    - number: 300 programs
    - number: 95.67% syntax correctness
    - number: 3.69% verification rate
    - limits: The review notes found no check against assume or admit (unproved assumptions) or against vacuous specs (specs that hold trivially). Over-restrictive requires clauses (preconditions) could make verification easier. The prompt in Appendix G contradicts Section 3.3. It excludes liveness, resource bounds and security policies. It has no concurrency.

- Ellsberg (2025)
    - title: Runtime Protocol Refinement Checking for Distributed Protocol Implementations
    - what: It monitors the messages going into and out of a deployed process. It searches for an allowed run of a TLA+ protocol, translated to Go, that explains them. If none exists, it reports an error. It was applied to Etcd, ZooKeeper and Redis Raft.
    - number: executable spec sizes: ETCD 952, RedisRaft 894, Zookeeper 989 lines (prose says about 500 and 2,000)
    - number: mapping code 356, 370, 380 Go lines; 11, 31, 10 modified functions
    - number: translation tested on 8,750,468 Raft and 1,904,456 ZooKeeper traces
    - number: ten bugs, nine previously known, one new Redis Raft reconfiguration bug
    - limits: It handles fail-stop faults only. It assumes the supplied protocol is correct and the trace is faithful and ordered. It cannot prove correctness or catch livelock or deadlock. Humans wrote the translations and mappings. There is no public repository.
    - how much was read: most of it read
    - [source](https://www.usenix.org/system/files/nsdi25-ding.pdf); [notes: Runtime Protocol Refinement Checking for Distributed Protocol Implemen](../distributed_verification_review_b/deep_12_second_pass_intent_eval.md); [notes: Runtime Protocol Refinement Checking for Distributed Protocol Implemen](../distributed_verification_review_b/deep_11_second_pass_agents_invariants.md); [notes: Runtime Protocol Refinement Checking for Distributed Protocol Implemen](../distributed_verification_review_b/deep_15_runtime_foundations.md)

- FormalBench (2025)
    - title: Can LLMs Reason About Program Semantics? A Comprehensive Evaluation of LLMs on Formal Specification Inference
    - what: Introduces FormalBench, a benchmark of manually validated Java/JML programs and semantic-preserving variants for evaluating LLM specification inference using deductive consistency, mutation-based completeness, robustness and self-repair metrics. FormalBench-Base contains 700 validated Java programs; 18 semantics-preserving transformations produce 6,219 diverse variants. OpenJML determines success/failure/unknown and Major generates mutants; completeness is the fraction of non-equivalent mutants rejected. The study evaluates eight open-source/proprietary LLMs under zero-shot, few-shot, chain-of-thought and least-to-most prompts, diagnoses syntax/quantifier/loop-bound errors, and tests targeted self-repair prompts.
    - number: 700 manually validated programs and 6,219 augmented variants
    - number: 14 LLMs reported in conclusion; core table covers eight models/settings
    - number: Best listed success: DeepSeek-V3 + LTM 16.6%; GPT-4o + LTM 15.0%; Claude-3.5 + LTM 15.4%
    - number: Robustness flip rates: DeepSeek-V3 27.2%, Claude-3.5 39.2%, GPT-4o 29.2%
    - limits: Mutation completeness is a proxy: equivalent mutants can evade detection (3 of 232 manually checked, 1.3%). Unknown verifier results are separated from failures but complicate metrics. The benchmark is Java/JML-specific; generated specifications may be syntactically invalid or semantically incomplete. Experiments omit OpenAI-o1 and DeepSeek-R1 due cost/access and use finite token/GPU budgets.
    - how much was read: most of it read
    - [source](https://arxiv.org/abs/2503.04779)
    - record id: `formalbench-2025`
- FormalSpecCpp: A Dataset of C++ Formal Specifications created using LLMs (2025)
    - what: # Whole-paper read: FormalSpecCpp: A Dataset of C++ Formal Specifications created using LLMs (authored by agents unless marked 🧑)  Madhurima Chakraborty, Peter Pirkelbauer and Qing Yi; arXiv2502.15217v1,21February2025,5pages. The paper constructs105small C++ programs/contracts and translated tests using GPT-4-turbo, starting from verified Dafny-synthesis programs. Its evidence is compilation, finite tests and expert review of a subset. The introductory description of “verified C++ programs” is an author claim; no C++ deductive verification, verified translator or preservation theorem is supplied. [Q0,Q1,Q4,Q5,Q6,Q7]  ## Construction, contracts and trust  Only Dafny programs containing both preconditions and postconditions were selected. GPT-4-turbo translates their code and specifications; MBPP tests are separately translated to C++. Figure1 shows the Python→Dafny→C++ provenance and test/manual-inspection branches. This is translation from existing specifications, rather than an evaluation of inferring unknown C++ contracts. Figure2 asks the model to select signed/unsigned integer types, identify failure conditions, express preconditions with REQUIRE, loop invariants with assert, postconditions with ENSURE, and omit main. Both macros expand to assert. These are executable checks on reached states, not discharged proof obligations over all allowed inputs. They may also be disabled by the usual C++ assertion mechanism. [Q2,Q3,Q4,Q5,S2]  The paper argues that translating verified Dafny specifications preserves correctness and avoids unnecessary constraints. That does not follow just from a verified source: integer domains, containers, evaluation order, exceptional behavior and the translation of the property itself also need a correspondence argument. The paper's own manual review found semantically incorrect but syntactically valid specifications. The source Dafny verifier, selection, language mapping, generated tests and reviewer judgment therefore remain trust dependencies. There is no independent exhaustive audit of all105contracts in this paper or in this reading. [Q4,Q6,Q7,Q10]  The paper contrasts unbounded Dafny integers with C++ fixed-width types, but incorrectly says C++ has “well-defined overflow behavior for signed integers.” Ordinary out-of-range signed arithmetic has undefined behavior. The saved C++ draft's expression rules state that arithmetic-expression behavior is undefined when the mathematical result is outside representable values (with specified floating-point exceptions); unsigned modular arithmetic is distinct. This is a reader correction of a language claim, not an executed counterexample to every translated program. [Q10,L0]  ## What the evaluation establishes  The initial linked-test run had62programs pass all tests and43fail due to assertions, linking or compilation; 62/105≈59.05% is my arithmetic, not a reported semantic-correctness rate. Authors found misordered test arguments, incorrect expected outputs and unsigned-int/double mismatches. Compiling successfully did not exclude incorrect contracts or logical inconsistencies. One author reviewed/refined failing cases and a subset of successes; the paper gives no exact reviewed-subset size, final complete semantic-pass count, independent reviewer agreement or measured coverage. [Q6,Q7]  TableI/§IV report105/105first-attempt program compilations,105files,$2.07program-translation cost (about$0.02/file),17.3average Dafny lines,33.1average C++ lines and91.2%growth. The text reports about27minutes for programs and an additional$1.31/about15minutes for tests. These are historical generation/compilation figures, not105proved C++ implementations. There is no contradiction merely between first-attempt program compilation and later failing linked tests: they are different stages. The artifact makes the distinction concrete with g++ -std=c++11 -c for generated functions and linked executables for tests. No hardware, exact GPT snapshot/date, seed, repeated-run distribution, token totals or human-review time is given in the paper. [Q8,S2,S4,S7]  The introduction proposes precision, recall, correctness, fine-tuning and inference-tool evaluation as uses. It reports none of those downstream comparative experiments. No baseline, prompt ablation, contamination study or evidence of scaling to large legacy C++ is supplied. The authors' efficiency/scalability language is broader than the measured105small-program conversion. Future work explicitly includes larger and more complex programs. [Q1,Q8,Q11,Q12]  ## Pinned artifact and concrete cases  The paper-linked GitHub repository was inspected at commit3f70080088d6d6ad3fe7923f94d2a91968cdc78c. Its README says the benchmark was published at MSR2025; I did not establish separate proceedings metadata. A nontruncated tree lists105with-contract C++ sources,105NoSpec sources and105test sources plus105same-basename executable entries. I did not download or execute those binaries. I read both READMEs, all four Python scripts, requirements, the human-readable conversion report, five program/test pairs (IDs3,14,101,105,106), and ID3's NoSpec counterpart. That is a targeted static sample, not an audit of the entire dataset. Upstream Dafny source and raw serialized experiment logs were not acquired/loaded. [S0–S18]  The translation script calls model gpt-4-turbo with temperature0 and max_tokens2000, extracts a fenced cpp block, compiles an object, and can repair compilation errors for up to10attempts. The checked analysis report records105first-attempt successes,105total attempts,$2.07 and no compilation errors, agreeing with TableI. The report is author-supplied evidence, not a reproduced run. The code's cost tracker counts only the initial response, so hypothetical repair costs would be omitted; because this report says all attempts were first attempts, this observation does not establish an undercount for the reported run. [S2,S7]  getTests.py uses temperature0/max_tokens500 and up to5attempts to extract a code block, then writes a test including the translated program. The script labels response.usage.total_tokens as input_tokens and also charges completion_tokens at output rates. Static accounting therefore charges completion tokens once at input rates as well as output rates; only the final successful response's usage is returned, omitting failed extraction attempts. Actual request histories are unavailable, so I do not compute a corrected$1.31total or assert that these two effects cancel. The runner counts process success/failure, but its error-category tests search str(CalledProcessError) without captured stderr, limiting reliable diagnosis of assertion/linker/compiler categories. Neither issue turns a successful test return into a proof. [S3,S4]  The static cases show what the annotations mean. IsNonPrime(ID3) checks a divisor-search invariant and a quantified-by-loop postcondition; its tests cover2,10,35. KthElement(ID101) checks1≤k≤array size and returns arr[k−1]; three tests confirm specified positions, not sorted-order statistics. CountTrue(ID105) compares the running count with a recursive helper and excludes empty vectors. AppendArrayToSeq(ID106) preserves the input prefix and appended elements with explicit loops; it requires !a.empty(), commented “equivalent to a != null in Dafny.” Nonempty and nonnull are different conditions: an empty C++ vector is a valid value. Without the upstream Dafny source I flag the local comment/contract mismatch, rather than claiming a checked source-to-target counterexample. All five sampled tests contain three explicit input cases. [S8–S17]  TriangularPrismVolume(ID14) accepts positive unsigned arguments, computes (base*height*length)/2, and asserts equality with that identical expression. The contract checks the same finite-width, integer-division expression, so by itself it does not establish unbounded arithmetic or fractional-volume semantics. Its tests use products with integral halves (10,8,6→240;3,2,2→6;1,2,1→1). IDs3/105/106 also execute additional loops or recursive helpers for assertions, which is actual checking work while assertions are enabled. No runtime-overhead measurement is reported. ID3's NoSpec file removes those checks; the removal script is textual pattern matching rather than a verified transformation. [S5,S8–S18]  ## Contribution and read scope  This adds an accessible LLM-assisted contract dataset and concrete translation/test failures to the specification-evaluation collection. My inference for evaluating proof/specification agents is to separate source verification, target compilation, finite testing, expert repair and target proof; inspect input-domain restrictions and arithmetic semantics; and distinguish repeated computations from independently adequate contracts. The paper has no distributed-protocol, Verus/Rust, TLA+ or proof-writing-agent experiment. [Q4,Q6,Q7,Q10,Q12]  Read all5pages,15references,both figures including the full prompt image,TableI and all printed code; viewed all5page renders. Official arXiv and inventory PDFs are byte-identical. Read19pinned static text files with SHA256/Gitblobidentities, plus the cited C++ draft paragraph. No author code was executed/imported; no compiler, checker, prover or solver was run; no serialized scientific experiment results were loaded. Reported outcomes remain author-reported.
    - limits: 105smallDafny-derivedprograms;assertiontests/subsetexpertreviewnotdeductiveC++proof;signedoverflowclaimcorrected;targetedartifactsample
    - how much was read: most of it read
    - [source](https://arxiv.org/abs/2502.15217)
    - record id: `formalspeccpp-a-dataset-of-2025`

- HornStr (2025)
    - title: sfHornStr: Invariant Synthesis for Regular Model Checking as Constrained Horn Clauses.
    - what: HornStr synthesizes regular-language inductive invariants for string-theory CHCs using SAT-based DFA learning or L* active learning. Standard SMT-LIB2.6 string solvers check initiation,transition closure and bad-state exclusion; reachability enumeration supports active membership and inductive-counterexample handling. It connects parameterized-protocol regular model checking to existing string solvers and generates string-query benchmarks. Describes restricted unary-predicate problem,learning/refinement flow,DFA-to-regex translation,heuristic reachability cutoff,incremental solver sessions and separate membership/equivalence backend choice. Reports complete query-solver and protocol results,including active-learner timeouts,unlisted hard protocols and prose/table inconsistencies. No general completeness theorem or verified implementation is supplied.
    - number: 18 benchmark protocols (Table 2); SAT-based learner solved all 18, mostly in under a second; MU puzzle 11.01 s with a 3-state automaton
    - number: The active learner timed out on 6 of the 18 (Burns, Mux-Array, Eqdist, MU Puzzle, Water-Jug, Dining-Crypt)
    - number: Z3-noodler solved all 848 non-incremental equivalence queries; cvc5, Z3, Z3-alpha solved 403 to 457
    - number: Generated more than 30000 string constraints (abstract) or more than 10,000 (Sec 5); the paper gives both numbers
    - limits: §2: one unary unknown predicate over strings; sought solutions regular languages. General CHC definition allows more predicates/nonlinearity, but that syntax is not evidence that implemented solver handles all general string CHCs. Symbolic alphabets and broader CHC support deferred in §5. §§2.1–2.2: safety expressed by initial inclusion,transition closure,error exclusion; configurations are modeled words. Correctness of protocol→CHC modeling/automatic translation assumed,not mechanized here. Arbitrary number of processes does not establish real Rust implementation correctness. §2.1: reachable/bad-predecessor languages can be nonregular while a separating inductive invariant is regular. Failure to find a regular invariant need not mean unsafe; no complete procedure for all infinite-state systems. §3: SAT unsat means current DFA size cannot fit examples and triggers larger size; string-query unsat means no violation of the particular checked clause. These are different meanings,not contradictory CHC conclusions. §3: active membership enumerates reachable words and may answer negative after same-length exhaustion or fixed cutoff. Explicit heuristic inspired by length preservation; bounded failure to find a word does not generally prove unreachable in length-changing rewriting. Could impair learning/completeness. Final all-clause validation remains distinct,conditional on solver correctness and handling of unknown/timeouts. §3: DFA→regex uses Brzozowski/McCluskey state elimination; induction counterexamples can be converted to positive/negative via reachability following strict-but-generous teacher. No in-paper full correctness/termination proof,complexity bound,formalized checker or proof-producing string backend. §3/Fig1: learners exchange regex equivalence queries and reachability membership queries through independent interfaces. Each CHC can have dedicated incremental solver thread with push/pop; optional single solver. Trust includes parsers,translation,learners,regex conversion and SMT solvers; artifact not executed. §4.1: query families have unequal counts: incremental523membership/396equivalence versus nonincremental30902/848. Raw aggregate runtimes cannot isolate causal per-query incrementality benefit without matched workloads. Table excludes timeouts from times; lower solved-time can simply reflect fewer solved queries. §4.1: prose calls523membership queries an average109.7s and says other solvers slightly higher runtimes. Table1 labels Time(s),caption solved time;Z3 33.7,cvc5 97.8,Z3-alpha86.4 are lower than109.7. Preserve table and inconsistent prose;109.7s as per-query average would exceed stated30s timeout. No silent correction or invented timing definition. §4.1: visual PDF p.8 confirms disequality x≠c; text extraction drops stroke and yields x=c. Regular-expression simplifier/flags marginal or harmful overall,not robust improvement;no quantitative ablation table. §4.2:18listed protocols all solved by SAT;active12solved/6timeouts. Kanban/German timeout60s in prose but absent from Table2;therefore SAT success on every listed row does not mean all benchmark protocols solved. Full-suite count and which learners timeout on unlisted protocols not supplied. §4/Table2: automaton sizes and learning seconds;no printed invariant automata/independent certificate or unsafe-trace count per row. MU3states/11.01s is reported discovery,not this reader rerunning solver or inspecting artifact invariant. First-solver claim is authors’ claim,not independently established priority. §4:IntelCorei7-10510U1.8GHz/16GB/Windows11.30s query and60s protocol limits;solver revisions,repeat counts/variance,full timeout overhead,independent baseline RMC comparison and frontend correctness unreported here. §§abstract/5:abstract says more than30000newQF_S constraints;conclusion says more than10000. Compatible lower bounds but inconsistent specificity;do not sum query categories as disjoint unique files. Liveness,bisimulation,further CHCs future work. No Verus/Rust/TLA+ or LLM experiments. Full version[30]arXiv2505.15959 and artifact[31]Zenodo15195124 contain deferred detail;not read/executed. This is proceedings-paper read only.

- LAW (2025)
    - title: The LAW theorem: Local Reads and Linearizable Asynchronous Replication
    - what: The paper proves that no replicated register can have all three of these: a local read (a read with no messages), linearizability, and a guarantee that operations finish despite async delays and one crash. It designs almost-local reads (batched read validation) in Hermes, ZAB and Raft variants.
    - number: Raft-ALR 2.6x baseline throughput at 2% writes, 1.8x at 5%; up to 156 million requests/s
    - number: ZAB-ALR within 4% of ZAB at 1% writes; Hermes-ALR within 11% at 1% writes
    - number: 5 CloudLab servers, one million keys; reads batched up to 128
    - limits: A human wrote a mathematical proof about a mathematical register (deterministic servers, one active operation each, at most one crash). The correctness of the read algorithms is argued in prose. The Raft baseline relaxes linearizability, so the performance comparison is an upper bound. There is no LLM, no code proof and no person-hours.
    - how much was read: most of it read
    - [source](https://www.vldb.org/pvldb/vol18/p2831-giortamis.pdf); [notes: Added related work: The LAW theorem: Local Reads and Linearizable Asyn](../distributed_verification_review_b/deep_19_law_related.md)
    - record id: `law-2025`
- Lilo (2025)
    - title: Lilo: A Higher-Order, Relational Concurrent Separation Logic for Liveness
    - what: Lilo is a Coq logic for the liveness of shared-memory programs. It uses obligations (promises to finish) and progress credits. The authors give proofs for a spinlock, a ticket lock, Treiber and elimination stacks, and infinite message passing.
    - limits: Stratified propositions limit contracts that refer to themselves. Logically atomic triples for the elimination stack are future work. It is not a result about networks or crashes.
    - how much was read: most of it read

- LLM document-to-temporal-spec extraction (2025)
    - title: Extracting Formal Specifications from Documents Using LLMs for Automated Testing
    - what: Builds a dataset of 603 temporal specifications from 37 ArduPilot, PX4 and Autoware documents and evaluates GPT-4o, Claude-3.5-Sonnet and Llama-3.1-405B extracting formal requirements. A two-stage annotation-then-conversion pipeline improves coverage and accuracy over direct end-to-end prompting. Experts cross-validated ground truth; prompts first mark requirement-bearing sentences and then convert annotations into temporal logic, reducing oversimplification and hallucinated clauses. The paper quantifies false positives and studies a DeepSTL non-LLM conversion baseline. Documents average 2,966 words; each model runs three times and unique outputs are aggregated.
    - number: 603 validated specifications from 37 documents
    - number: End-to-end accuracy: Claude 51.7%, GPT-4o 47.1%, Llama 45.4%
    - number: Annotation-then-conversion best accuracy 71.6%; +14.0 percentage points average and +29.2% correctly extracted
    - number: Best false-positive rate 14.5%; each LLM run three times
    - limits: Ground truth may be incomplete or contain expert errors; temporal formulas require interpretation of informal documents. LLMs oversimplify dense requirements and fabricate plausible but unsupported conditions. Dataset is limited to three open-source autonomous-system projects and temporal logic; extraction quality does not prove implementation correctness.
    - how much was read: most of it read

- Neural model checking with invariants (2025)
    - title: Let a Neural Network Be Your Invariant
    - what: For hardware designs, one small neural network is trained to be both an inductive invariant (a set of states that contains all reachable states and is closed under steps; this proves safety) and a ranking function (a number that drops each time something bad could recur; this proves liveness). A solver then checks the network exactly, so a yes answer is a real proof. Training uses a constraint solver (MILP) rather than gradient descent.
    - number: 634-task benchmark: 11 SystemVerilog designs; 206 safety, 219 liveness, 209 safety+liveness tasks
    - number: solved: 190/206 safety (92%), 192/219 liveness (88%), 172/209 safety+liveness (82%)
    - number: nuXmv solved 82% of liveness and 74% of safety+liveness tasks; rIC3 50% and 61%; ABC 36% and 46%; prior neural method NMC'24 79% of liveness
    - number: pure liveness: faster than the best other tool on 66% of tasks, 10x faster on 46%
    - limits: Only finite-state hardware-style systems (no arrays or strings of parametric length, no procedure calls or dynamic data); the benchmark is the authors' own, built from textbook-level designs, so results may not carry to other workloads; relies on sign-activation feed-forward networks.
    - how much was read: most of it read
