- Reusable Formal Verification of DAG-based Consensus Protocols (2025)
    - what: # Reusable Formal Verification of DAG-based Consensus Protocols (authored by agents unless marked 🧑)  Nathalie Bertrand, Pranav Ghorpade, Sasha Rubin, Bernhard Scholz and Pavle Subotić, arXiv2407.02167v2,18March2025. The inventory year2024 identifies the original submission; this read uses the retained2025revision. The authors report TLA+/TLAPS safety proofs for five protocol models by combining three DAG-construction components with two ordering components. The five are DAG-Rider, Cordial Miners, Hashgraph, Eventually Synchronous Bullshark and a minor variant of Aleph. The result concerns Integrity and Total Order; eventual delivery, probabilistic progress and implementation correctness remain outside it (Q0,Q1,Q2,Q12).  ## Reading scope and source identity  Read all21PDFpages, sections1–6 and51reference entries, all three figures including both panels of Figure2, seven listings and numerical Table1. Individually viewed pages6–16, covering the figures, listings, refinement mapping and numerical table. The paper refers to “Appendix ??” but includes no appendix. References were read as bibliography entries, not recursively read papers.  Acquired the paper-linked repository's complete nontruncated tree and22static blobs at9e4c9629eb283c7c34b6ffcf7956bc72a3e26e5d,22December2024, the latest retained commit before this paper revision. This selects a historical version without establishing that it produced the reported timings. Whole reads cover README and the five component Specification.tla files. Proof files and complete-protocol sources are inspected only in explicitly recorded ranges. Acquisition of a file does not imply its whole read. No author code, specification, checker or solver was executed/imported; no serialized experimental data or model was loaded.  ## What is proved and how composition works  A vertex embeds its entire rooted causal history, rather than only an opaque identifier. Thus two processes possessing the same vertex have identical histories for it. A separate anchor-agreement property requires processes that have selected anchors for a frame to select the same anchors. Delivering each frame's previously undelivered causal-history vertices in a fixed deterministic order then prevents duplicate delivery and gives consistent delivery order. A lagging process may deliver a shorter prefix; the proof is not simultaneous equality of local delivery states (Q2,Q3).  The decomposition is three construction models, reliable structured, unreliable structured and unreliable unstructured, plus Global Perfect Coin (GPC) and Virtual Voting (VV) ordering. Reliable construction ensures one agreed vertex per position. Unreliable structured construction allows equivocation and uses ratification to agree on a vertex at a position. GPC ordering separately agrees on leader positions; combining these properties establishes anchor agreement. Hashgraph first converts its unstructured events to a structured witness DAG. Reference consistency constrains witnesses' predecessor references; VV establishes agreement on famous witnesses (Q4,Q6,Q7,Q8).  The full protocol maps constants and new state variables into an INSTANCE of the ordering specification. Each construction action performs an appropriate ordering action or leaves its mapped variables unchanged. Protocol-specific refinement obligations remain necessary: five reusable components do not eliminate the proofs connecting five concrete models to them. DAG-Rider and ES Bullshark share reusable components but differ in wave lengths and commit/leader rules. Aleph is explicitly a variant; neither the paper nor its one-line repository README gives a precise delta against every published Aleph rule (Q4,Q9,C6).  ## Assumptions and abstractions  The system explanation assumes signed identities and fewer than one-third Byzantine processes. Reliable broadcast is weakened to integrity because validity and eventual agreement support liveness. Its model retains the first broadcast for each creator/round; the authors argue a faulty process's chosen delivered message could equivalently have been sent first. This is an abstraction argument to retain in correspondence work, not a separately measured implementation equivalence (Q1,Q5).  Unreliable communication copies a vertex from another local DAG; Byzantine participants can insert arbitrary modeled vertices. Procedures retain input/output behavior while omitting internal loop states. GPC becomes an arbitrary fixed function shared by all processes: randomness, unpredictability and fairness are absent. Agreement of the function's answers suffices for the stated safety argument; it supplies no probability-one termination theorem or implementation of a cryptographic coin (Q5,Q7,Q8).  The retained component models use finite symbolic bounds on waves/rounds or event IDs, with no fixed numerical test instance. Proof claims quantify over their parameters; a single TLC run at one bound would not establish that claim. The record does not independently establish an infinite-execution refinement beyond these bounded model families. The VV component incorporates its reference-consistency premise directly into both the prestate and poststate of Next. Its source proof of that premise therefore depends on the action restriction; a concrete protocol still must justify the mapping (C1–C5).  ## Consequential findings in the retained artifact  The printed self-referential VertexSet listing is explanatory shorthand. ReliableStructuredDAGconstructionSpecification.tla instead constructs vertices recursively by decreasing round with UntilRoundVertex. That resolves the apparent circular-definition concern for this component. However, its received/added-vertex guards access v.edges, while the actual constructed records contain strongedges and weakedges. This is a static field mismatch in the saved model; no checker error or reachable protocol failure was observed (C1).  In the unreliable structured component, Approves(v,u) requires Observes(v,u) and forbids observing every vertex with u's source and round. Taking that quantified vertex to be u yields both Observes(v,u) and its negation. Thus the literal definition admits no approval. With n-f positive under3f<n, ratification requires a nonempty set of approving sources and cannot occur under this reading. Since ratified vertices initialize to Nil, the displayed agreement property can hold without any ratification progress. This is a reader derivation from the retained definitions, not a claim that the real Cordial Miners protocol fails (C2).  The unstructured component's DonotSeeFork(v,u) has the same self-including universal condition. Taking x=u contradicts its Path(v,u) conjunct. Its Sees predicate consequently cannot witness a seen ancestor under the intended reflexive Path interpretation. This is another source-definition concern to resolve before trusting correspondence to actual Hashgraph behavior; the paper excludes liveness and no safety counterexample is established (C3).  VVOrderingProofs.tla contains several named lemma statements without proof text. Most concretely, intInv14 asserts, for integers a,m, that a>=m and m+a>2f imply m>f. The permitted assignment f=1,a=2,m=1 makes both premises true and its conclusion false. The file cites this lemma in its Inv1 preservation proof. The nearby intadd statement has the plausible intended conclusion a>f with strict a>b. This suggests a proof-support defect or mistaken statement in the retained artifact; it does not show a checker accepted a false theorem. The actual preservation goal is a majority-count fact that can be mathematically true even though this cited general lemma is false (C7,C8).  The same proof preamble asserts, without accompanying proof text, that every witness has more than2f predecessor sources in the preceding frame. Combined literally with its positive finite frame range and predecessor-frame condition, that leaves no nonempty witness set: a witness of minimum frame would require an earlier witness. This preamble should not silently become an established domain theorem. A simpler nonvacuity concern appears in the VV specification itself: all witness-DAG cells initially are empty, while adding a witness requires more than2f predecessor sources already present. With f nonnegative and no genesis exception, no first witness can be added under the intended typed interpretation. Whole proof closure and tool handling of unproved declarations have not been replayed. These artifact findings limit independent confirmation of the paper's mechanized-proof claim; they do not refute the high-level decomposition or establish a reachable fork (C5,C7).  ## Evaluation and significance  Table1 reports, in order reliable structured/unreliable structured/unreliable unstructured/GPC/VV: specification lines403/160/230/272/136; invariants6/6/7/10/18; proof lines460/594/554/822/2120; maximum proof depths10/9/8/9/13; maximum degrees7/8/7/7/11; leaf obligations633/895/665/1302/3316; and checking seconds49/68/74/125/651. Environment:2.10GHzCPU,8GBRAM,Windows11,TLAPS1.4.5. These are author-reported component checks, not reader replications or total timings for every full-protocol refinement. No repeated-run variability or controlled comparison appears (Q10).  The authors report approximately14person-months across five people. Their “almost half” proof-effort claim corresponds to reducing an illustrative ten separate construction/ordering components to five reusable ones. There is no controlled person-time baseline or ablation demonstrating a50% labor saving; refinement work and component complexity also differ. A reusable component count is concrete, while a measured causal labor reduction remains unestablished (Q0,Q4,Q10,Q12).  This is directly relevant to reusable TLA+ specifications and distributed-protocol proofs. It has no Verus/Rust artifact or LLM-agent experiment. A reader proposal for evaluating proof agents is to score specification correspondence, nonvacuous progress examples, closure of helper lemmas and refinement obligations separately from reported leaf-obligation throughput. The retained field/predicate/lemma issues make those distinctions concrete without treating static concerns as observed solver results.
    - number: approximately 14 person-months across 5 people
    - number: Table 1 spec lines 403/160/230/272/136; proof lines 460/594/554/822/2120; obligations 633/895/665/1302/3316; TLAPS time 49/68/74/125/651 s
    - number: TLAPS 1.4.5 on 2.10 GHz, 8 GB
    - number: Reuse reduces proof effort by almost half
    - limits: Safetyonly;abstractbroadcastandcoin;finiteparameterizedhorizons;implementationrefinementfuturework;retainedsourcefield/predicate/helperlemmafindingslimitindependentconfirmation;nocheckerreplay
    - how much was read: most of it read

- SpecVerify (2025)
    - title: Supporting Software Formal Verification with Large Language Models: An Experimental Study
    - what: SpecVerify converts natural-language requirements and implementation code into an intermediate formalized requirement/test design, then generates C assertions checked with ESBMC. It compares Claude-based verification with FRET/CoCoSim and SLDV on Simulink-generated aerospace C code. Analyzes variable mapping and precondition/function/postcondition correspondence, gives triple-redundant voting and missing aircraft-weight-assumption cases, and traces a floating-point median-selection bug that rational arithmetic missed. Counterexamples are replayed against code when results differ. The authors report a mistaken +1-to-0 connection in a prior REG model.
    - number: 9 systems; 58 requirements; 41–251 C lines
    - number: 46/58 logical equivalence (79.31%)
    - number: Claude+ESBMC completion 27/58 (46.5%)
    - number: Table V false negatives: CoCoSim 6; Claude+ESBMC 2
    - limits: Nine retained systems contain only 41–251 C lines; one original benchmark cannot be converted. Benchmark code is generated and structured; larger manually written programs are untested. The logical-equivalence assessment is manual and can oversimplify temporal relationships. LLM and verifier effects are confounded. ChatGPT model naming varies across the source; Llama 3.1 8B failed tasks and was excluded. Table II/full-automation claims coexist with a stated need for considerable manual checking of generated code. Arithmetic inconsistencies remain in the source: 29/58 undetermined is labeled 39%; Table V false negatives are 6 versus 2, while the conclusion claims 6 fewer. These are source issues, not corrected measurements.

- Taming Floating-Point Rounding Errors with Proofs (2025)
    - what: Overview of formal verification for NASA avionics floating-point software, distinguishing an incorrect real-number requirement from rounding errors in a finite-precision implementation. Reports CPR verification with Frama-C/PVS/Gappa, PRECiSA certified error estimation using abstract interpretation and Kodiak, and ReFlow generation of annotated C with warnings for potentially unstable branches. Summarizes earlier results; supplies no new detailed proof or benchmark. The entire publication is an extended abstract. Records its actual toolchain stages, trace-wise symbolic versus numerical certificates, warning semantics, expert manual versus automated verification scope, concrete avionics cases, cited-source pointers and explicit future-work status of LLM-generated-code verification. Does not upgrade an overview into an independent check of cited systems.
    - limits: p1: Original CPR operational requirements reportedly failed intended precision even over exact reals. Tightened real-number requirements and a proved double-precision implementation are separate results. The overview supplies neither numeric preconditions/tolerances nor a counterexample position; source[6] VSTTE2017 and source[19] FM2018 hold the detailed analyses. No guarantee for the unmodified original requirements follows. p1: Improved CPR expressions reduce numerical complexity; double-precision correctness is attributed to Frama-C/PVS/Gappa. The early workflow requires expertise and is not fully automatic. Arithmetic format alone does not establish correctness; relevant input ranges and semantics are not reproduced here. p1: PRECiSA abstracts collecting semantics, builds symbolic error expressions along traces and invokes Kodiak for sound numerical enclosures. Externally checkable certificates cover both symbolic and numerical error estimates, relying on PVS floating-point and branch-and-bound formalizations. Certificate format, proof size, checker version, trusted components, coverage of exceptional values and termination are not specified in this overview. p2: ReFlow extracts floating-point C from a PVS real-number specification, emits warnings where control flow may differ due to rounding and generates ACSL annotations linking the two. A warning is a disclosed possible divergence; this statement does not establish unconditional branch agreement or exact real-number equality for every input. No detailed unstable-branch example, emitted C/ACSL or warning-return contract appears. p2: Frama-C generates PVS verification conditions; ad hoc PVS strategies discharge them automatically in the described toolchain. The user-expertise claim concerns these supported generated programs. The overview reports no corpus, supported syntax/loop bounds, failure rate or theorem proving timings that would justify universal automatic verification of arbitrary C. p2: Applications are winding-number point-in-polygon for geofencing and a fragment of DAIDALUS. This is not verification of all DAIDALUS, all ADS-B communication, complete aircraft software, a distributed fault model or system liveness. p2: LLM-generated code and quantized neural networks are future challenges, not an implemented/evaluated proof-agent or verified neural inference result. No Verus, verified Rust or TLA+ system is presented. Three-page invited-talk overview has no original formal statements/proof scripts, measurements, quantitative error table, performance comparison, source audit or reproduced artifact check. Tool guarantees are author reports of cited results.
    - how much was read: most of it read
    - [source](https://doi.org/10.4230/LIPIcs.ITP.2025.41)
    - record id: `taming-floating-point-rounding-2025`

- Autoformalizing Euclidean Geometry (2024)
    - what: Introduces LeanEuclid, 173 manually formalized Euclidean geometry statement/proof/diagram examples, and a Lean implementation of a variant of system E. A symbolic SMT engine fills implicit diagrammatic proof gaps and evaluates statement equivalence as E3. January2024 GPT-4/GPT-4V few-shot experiments report up to21.0% solver-proved statement equivalence and23.1% proof acceptance across the143-item evaluation set. SMT answers are admitted through an explicitly unsound axiom rather than reconstructed as Lean proof terms, so results require trusting the translation and SMT solvers as well as the Lean kernel. Separates explicit construction steps, chosen by humans/models, from non-construction diagrammatic/metric/transfer reasoning handled by SMT refutation. euclid_apply instantiates a rule, proves missing premises with SMT, and adds conclusions/witnesses; euclid_finish/assert discharge goals. E3 checks both implication directions plus contradiction detection; approximate comparison requires matching numbers/types of bound variables, selects top-n renamings by string similarity, and counts four directions of pre/postcondition obligations, adding preconditions to postcondition comparisons only after precondition equivalence. Dataset contains all48 Elements BookI propositions and125 UniGeo examples (25 each in five categories); texts are manually augmented/abridged. Appendices give a full axiom catalogue, a missing-case reconstruction for Elements24, formalization comparisons, exact prompts, partial semantic outcomes, and before/after repair scripts.
    - limits: Correctness is relative to the chosen geometric axioms and Lean-to-SMT encoding. AppendixC explicitly introduces an unsound axiom to admit successful SMT queries; there is no end-to-end kernel certification or SMT proof reconstruction. The reader did not audit or execute the released implementation. SystemE soundness/completeness is attributed to Avigad etal for its original axioms. This implementation adds construction, diagrammatic and transfer rules, strengthens one construction rule, and uses SMT real-number theories. The supplied paper does not mechanize a soundness proof of the modified implementation. The diagrammatic engine uses diagrammatic,metric,transfer rules. Equivalence checking additionally uses nine simple construction rules. Limited construction/instantiation makes E3 incomplete; failure to establish equivalence is not proof of inequivalence. Logical equivalence of closed true theorems can be weaker than faithful correspondence of hypotheses,objects and informal meaning. The paper itself reports a logically equivalent but unfaithful quadrilateral/parallelism example; success should not be read as comprehensive intent validation. Approximate equivalence is a bounded heuristic over variable unifications and clause counts, not a semantic distance with a correctness theorem. It excludes formulas with differing variable counts/types and depends on chosen clauses and names. Evaluation uses gpt-4-1106-preview and gpt-4-1106-vision-preview in January2024, one domain, manually prepared prompts and a small curated test split. No significance intervals or multiple-run variance are reported. Proof autoformalization receives a correct formal statement and informal proof, whereas statement autoformalization is evaluated separately. Proof success cannot establish an end-to-end informal-statement-to-correct-proof rate. Manual Elements repairs use ground-truth proofs and a minimum-change preference; Levenshtein ratios measure textual alteration, not time,semantic effort,independent automation success or proof validity. Data-contamination improbability is the authors inference from LeanEuclid being unpublished at experiment time. Models may have seen Elements/UniGeo or other formalizations; no pretraining audit is supplied. No distributed systems,Verus,Rust or TLA+ evaluation occurs. Reuse of domain rules and semantic equivalence checks in those settings is a reader hypothesis, not established transfer.
    - how much was read: most of it read

- LVR (2024)
    - title: LVR: Mostly Automated Verification of Liveness Properties for Distributed Protocols with Ranking Functions
    - what: LVR turns a progress goal into ranking obligations and safety obligations: the measure decreases, stays nonnegative, cannot leave the pending condition without reaching the good state, and no deadlock occurs. It infers integer variables, bounds and polynomial coefficients. mypyvy and P-FOL-IC3 discharge the safety obligations. Humans supply any missing variables, bounds and tiers.
    - number: 37 out of 40 (§9.2, p.35:27) automatically; the rest manually in a few hours each
    - number: each final coefficient-solving query takes less than a second
    - number: eight models: ticket lock 68 lines, hybrid reliable broadcast 137, alternating bit 127, TLB shootdown 361, Paxos 133, multi-Paxos 135, stoppable Paxos 160, sliding window 119
    - number: all eight protocols succeed: ticket lock, hybrid reliable broadcast, alternating bit, TLB shootdown, Paxos, Multi-Paxos, Stoppable Paxos, sliding window
    - limits: By default, scheduling assumes a globally bounded interval. This is a slightly stronger fairness than "infinitely often". For example, scheduling at times 1, 2, 4, 8 satisfies "infinitely often" but not any fixed bound. Prophecy variables and a three-tier ranking keep the original fairness. LVR's multi-Paxos leaves out the local scheduler and the I/O queue. So fewer lines than IronFleet does not mean equally less effort. A cheap solver query is not the whole cost.

- Modelling the Raft Distributed Consensus Protocol in mCRL2 (2024)
    - what: # Whole read: Modelling the Raft Distributed Consensus Protocol in mCRL2 (authored by agents unless marked 🧑)  Bora, Pham Duc Minh and Willemse, MARS 2024, EPTCS 399, pp. 7–20; DOI 10.4204/EPTCS.399.4; arXiv 2403.18916v1, submitted 27 March 2024. Read all 14 PDF pages, §§1–5, Figure 1, Table 1, acknowledgement and 14 references, in text and rendered pages. Also read the official MARS mCRL2 model, all nine property files, README and batch script statically. The downloaded paper matches the indexed PDF byte for byte. No author code, model checker, prover or solver was executed. Raw sources, hashes, scope and exact excerpts are retained separately.  The paper contributes an action-based Raft model and modal µ-calculus formulas, with reported model-checking results for eight finite configurations. It does not establish unrestricted Raft correctness. More specifically, the authors write, “All main properties hold true for all configurations of Table 1; verification of each property takes roughly 2-3 times that of generating the state space” (§3, PDF p. 9 / printed p. 15; Q19). Static inspection also finds differences between the printed formulas, artifact formulas and model indexing. These narrow what a successful check can establish; they are not claimed reachable protocol counterexamples.  ## Model and assumptions  The model follows the TLA+ and LNT specifications and focuses on leader election and log replication: “Features such as cluster membership changes and log compaction have not been modelled” (§2, PDF p. 2 / printed p. 8; Q3). Node, Network and Client processes run in parallel; communicating actions and allow restrictions enforce synchronisation. Analysis requires a “fixed, concrete number of servers” (§2, PDF p. 3 / printed p. 9; Q4), rather than a proof quantified over all cluster sizes.  One client sends a bounded sequence of unique natural command IDs, abstracting away command contents and application behavior (Q5). Client-to-cluster communication is synchronous; communication among servers is asynchronous through the network. The network holds a finite set of payloads, permits reordering and limits capacity (Q7). Thus two identical pending payloads collapse to one set element. Reliable mode supplies no scheduler fairness guarantee. Capacity can block sends; the broadcast admission guard uses the number of servers rather than the exact outgoing set size (Q47). These are model restrictions, not a general unbounded or duplicate-preserving network semantics.  The scope description has a tension worth retaining. Section 2.1 says, “we do not analyse the Raft algorithm in the presence of message loss or duplication” (PDF p. 4 / printed p. 10; Q6). Table 1 nevertheless includes one lossy-and-crashing row, and §3 reports all main formulas true for every row (Q18–Q19). The model supplies an optional loss branch (Q8, Q47). The defensible reading is a primarily reliable model with one reported loss experiment; no duplication experiment is reported.  Each server has role, term, log, commit index, vote state, replication indexes and a pending reply. Figure 1 adds a Crashed role to Follower/Candidate/Leader (Q9–Q10). Entries are Command(term, commandID), stored in zero-based mCRL2 lists; the authors explicitly warn that Raft’s original indexing is one-based (Q11). Only leaders accept client commands. Elections and heartbeats use nondeterministic actions; “we cannot analyse real-time requirements, nor the real-time performance of the algorithm” (§2.2, PDF p. 6 / printed p. 12; Q12). There is no sampling of randomized timeout intervals or elapsed-time state.  A candidate votes for itself and sends vote requests to other nodes not already in its voter set (Q13). Stale RPCs are discarded. A received request computes and stores a reply before the later send, allowing a crash between the state update and reply transmission (Q14). The artifact’s resume transition preserves currentTerm, log and votedFor, but resets commitIndex, voterLog, nextIndex, matchIndex and the pending reply, and returns to Follower (Q46). Reply computation itself is atomic: the authors acknowledge it “does not accurately reflect real-world scenarios where the computation of a reply would involve multiple steps” (§5, PDF p. 13 / printed p. 19; Q35).  Leaders send one log entry at a time; the model uses nextIndex backtracking to find a matching prefix (Q15). Majority replication commits only a current-term entry directly; earlier entries are committed indirectly with it (Q16). This is a protocol model without a refinement proof connecting a Rust implementation, application state machine or storage system to these transitions.  ## What the properties check  The action logic cannot inspect process parameters directly. The model therefore adds self-loop actions exposeLeader, exposeLogLeader and exposeLog (Q19, Q46). Safety formulas compare exposed states across arbitrary later execution, rather than merely reading two process parameters in one state.  **Election Safety.** The printed formula forbids a different node from ever later exposing leadership in the same term (Q20); the artifact agrees (Q52). An additional existential formula asks whether a leader can be exposed at all; another asks whether two different node IDs can eventually expose leadership (Q21). The latter is reported true only with at least two terms. These are witness-existence checks, not eventual election on every execution.  The least-fixed-point formula counts possible leader announcements whose ID differs from the most recently stored ID, starting with id=1 and count=0 (Q22, Q53). It does not compare the associated terms or demand globally distinct IDs; a later reuse of an earlier ID can count. It is therefore more precise to describe its actual counter than to call it a proof of MaxTerm distinct leaders or guaranteed succession.  **Log Matching.** The intended statement is that the same index and term imply identical prefixes through that index. The paper’s guard instead tests complete entry equality, log1.index == log2.index, which is a stronger antecedent and makes the standalone requirement weaker (Q23). The artifact repairs this to term(log1.index) == term(log2.index) and checks both list bounds (Q49).  Both versions compare slice(log,1,index+1). The supplied slice helper begins at zero and excludes its finishing index (Q42). It therefore omits position 0, the first actual entry; initial logs contain no dummy element (Q48). Reader-created one-entry logs [Command(1,1)] and [Command(1,2)] have matching terms at position 0, but both compared slices are empty. This separates the intended first-entry equality requirement from the artifact formula. It does not establish that these two logs coexist in a reachable model state.  **Leader Completeness.** After advanceCommitIndex(old,new,term,log), the formula asks that selected entries occur somewhere in every later-term leader’s exposed log (Q24–Q25, Q51). The membership consequent is weaker than retaining each entry at its original index. There is also an indexing discrepancy: it selects log1.index for old < index <= new, while the model’s getTerm translates Raft index to list position index-1 (Q41). With a singleton log and new=1, the formula selects zero-based position 1 rather than 0. No semantic result for out-of-bounds list selection, checker failure or reachable counterexample is asserted here. For commits in the sole elected term of a one-term configuration, there is no later leader term to test; the auxiliary leader/commit existence checks do not make every main formula nonvacuous.  The event itself has a separate static inconsistency. On the successful AppendEntriesResponse branch, advanceCommitIndex computes its new index from the old matchIndex array, whereas the following Node transition computes commitIndex after incorporating the new acknowledgement (Q45). A local valuation with one entry, old matchIndex=[0,0,0] and an acknowledgement of entry 1 from another server yields event new index 0 but state commit index 1 using the supplied count helper (Q43–Q45). Reachability of that valuation was not established. The source expression mismatch is sufficient reason not to equate the event range automatically with all newly committed entries.  **State Machine Safety.** The intended statement forbids two nodes applying different entries at the same committed index. Paper and artifact compare slice(log1,1,commitIndex1) and the corresponding slice of a later other node with commitIndex2 >= commitIndex1 > 0 (Q26, Q50). Under the artifact helper, position 0 is excluded. For commitIndex1=1 both slices are empty, independent of the first entries. Thus a successful check of this formula alone cannot establish agreement on the first committed entry. Again, this is formula coverage established by symbolic values, not a reachable protocol failure.  An existential companion asks whether two different nodes can expose positive commit indexes in sequence, the later one at least as large (Q27). It does not repair prefix coverage or prove every request eventually commits. Append Entries is omitted as a separate checked property because the authors consider it immediate from the modeled leader operations (Q19).  ## Reported evaluation and concrete cases  The authors used manual interactive simulation and safety formulas throughout development to find model bugs (Q19). Table 1 gives eight configurations, all with three nodes and capacity three. The following preserves the reported rounded sizes and approximate generation times; every row is retained in numeric-evidence.json (Q18, §2.2, PDF p. 8 / printed p. 14):  - 2 commands, 1 term, reliable, no crashes: 2.14×10^5 states, about 1 minute - 1 command, 2 terms, reliable, no crashes: 1.17×10^6 states, about 2 minutes - 1 command, 3 terms, reliable, no crashes: 1.32×10^7 states, about 13 minutes - 2 commands, 1 term, reliable, crashes: 1.79×10^7 states, about 5 minutes - 2 commands, 2 terms, reliable, no crashes: 2.25×10^7 states, about 19 minutes - 2 commands, 1 term, lossy, crashes: 5.97×10^7 states, about 105 minutes - 1 command, 2 terms, reliable, crashes: 1.48×10^8 states, about 24 minutes - 1 command, 3 terms, reliable, crashes: 2.38×10^9 states, about 820 minutes  The first seven times use a 2017 Macbook Pro with 16GB; the last uses an unspecified compute server and is explicitly “not directly comparable to the other runtimes reported” (Table 1; Q18). No repetitions, error bars, exact CPU, per-formula times or implementation latency/throughput are supplied. The paper reports roughly 2–3× generation time for each main property using symbolic verification (Q19, technique reference Q38). The base configuration compresses by almost factor 5 under strong bisimilarity; no exact reduced size is given (Q17).  The artifact README names mCRL2 release 202307.1 and gives explicit and symbolic commands (Q56). Its default model has five servers, two requests, one term, capacity five, no loss/crashes, and PRIORITISE=false (Q40). Seven individually guarded Node instances support the small configured sizes (Q48). The batch script substitutes three servers/capacity three, requests 1 or 2, terms 1–3, and both Boolean loss/crash settings, for 24 combinations (Q57). It includes all .mcf files, including timeout/client-command checks (Q54–Q55); neither the script nor their inclusion is a saved result log. Do not infer that every batch combination or every extra formula was successfully checked. The official archive contains source and instructions, not experiment output or an identified experiment commit.  MAJORITY is ceil(NumberOfServers/2) in the artifact (Q40). That is a strict majority for the odd sizes three and five here, but only half for even sizes. No arbitrary-size correctness claim should be inferred from the configurable constant.  Section 4 illustrates how modeling style changes generated state count. A Boolean process with conditionals after the action yields 3 states/6 transitions; moving the choice before the action yields 1 state/2 transitions, strongly bisimilar to the first (Q29–Q30). The tool can then remove an unused process parameter. The example supports a concrete state-generation lesson; it is not a measured uniform threefold Raft reduction. The claim that an early inexperienced model was “orders of magnitude larger than needed” is an author observation without a controlled Raft benchmark (Q29).  ## Relation to the research topic  The direct contribution is a finite distributed-protocol model and action-logic encodings of selected Raft requirements, with bounded symbolic checking and concrete resource measurements. The authors describe the modal formalizations as apparently new while acknowledging the prior TLA+, LNT and Verdi work (Q2, Q33, Q39). This is useful evidence about specification design, state exposure, state-space growth, crash abstraction and model/property correspondence.  The TLA+ comparison is specific. The authors say TLA+ answers stale requests but drops stale responses; their model follows LNT in discarding both. They follow TLA+ when bounding transmitted commit information, and add reboot behavior they say is absent from the LNT model (Q31). These are attributed source discussions, not an independent audit of all upstream artifacts. Section 4’s view that imperative LNT is more appealing to average engineers is an author opinion, not a user study (Q32).  For evaluating LLM agents writing specifications or proofs, the useful checks are reader inferences: validate a formula against its helper definitions, include first/last-index examples, inspect whether instrumentation exposes the actual state transition, distinguish term equality from entry equality, and separate existential witnesses from inevitable progress. This paper supplies concrete reasons these checks matter, but it contains no LLM-agent, Verus, verified-Rust or implementation-refinement evaluation.  The liveness boundary is explicit: “Stronger liveness requirements, asserting that always inevitably a leader must be elected, are simply not true in our model” (§5, PDF p. 13 / printed p. 19; Q34), owing to crashes/loss and bounded terms. More complex configurations, stronger liveness, finer reply computation and real-time verification remain future work (Q33–Q36). Successful bounded checks therefore support the specified finite model and literal formulas, subject to the correspondence issues above. They do not independently establish the stronger prose requirements.  ## Evidence and review  quotes.json contains 57 literal excerpts with file, section, PDF/printed page where applicable, line ranges and character offsets. combined.quote-source.txt contains every excerpt for database validation. source-files.json retains raw hashes; source-scope.json distinguishes whole-paper and static artifact coverage. numeric-evidence.json preserves all table rows, and static-witnesses.json records reader-created separating values. reader-diagnostics.json separates source claims from reader findings. REVIEW.md and validation-final.json track consultation, independent review and mechanical checks.
    - limits: Fixed finite cluster,client commands,terms and finite-set network capacity; one synchronous client and asynchronous interserver RPC; no membership changes/log compaction; nondeterministic untimed timeouts,atomic reply computation,optional crashes/reboot/loss. Eight reported three-node configurations. No duplication/multiplicity or fairness guarantee. Static indexing/formula/event mismatches qualify property coverage.
    - how much was read: most of it read
    - [source](https://arxiv.org/abs/2403.18916)
    - record id: `modelling-the-raft-distributed-2024`

- VeriSMo (2024)
    - title: VeriSMo: A Verified Security Module for Confidential VMs
    - what: VeriSMo is a Rust security module at AMD SEV-SNP VM privilege level 0, verified with Verus. It protects its own data from an untrusted hypervisor and guest OS while providing guest page management, code integrity, runtime attestation and secret management. A machine model proves security under external interference; tracked permissions and locks verify the implementation under internal CPU concurrency. Security types constrain explicit and implicit information flow. Records exact successful-read and reachability conditions of security theorems, five preservation lemmas, initial page-table/permission assumptions, trusted hardware and byte-casting boundaries, shared-memory restrictions, register rules and declassification. Reconstructs the remapped-secret-page attack and zeroing fix, all API latency/LOC figures, baseline feature mismatch and the restricted-interrupt Linux/Hyper-V dependency. Distinguishes paper-reported proofs and fixes from independently replayed verification or exploits.
    - number: about 2:1 annotation/code ratio
    - number: about six minutes verification on 32 cores
    - number: 31 lines of unsafe Rust for hardware primitives
    - limits: §§2.1,3.1–3.2: Trusts CPU/firmware security behavior while hypervisor and guest OS may be malicious. Physical attacks are excluded and denial of service remains possible: the hypervisor can stop the host or refuse vCPU scheduling. This is a conditional safety result, not availability, scheduling fairness or distributed liveness. §§2.1,3.2: VeriSMo runs at VMPL0; guest at VMPL3, though VMPL1/2 could be used. Both use restricted interrupts, so the hypervisor can inject only #HV and a doorbell supplies the requested event. Mainstream Linux v6.8 lacked this support in KVM/guest, so experiments use Hyper-V and a modified Linux guest. The proof does not establish safety for an arbitrary unmodified VM/interrupt configuration. §§2.1,5.1: Hardware model includes CPU registers, memory, guest and nested mappings, RMP and cache/TLB-related state. E0 is VeriSMo, E3 guest, Ehv hypervisor. Guest and VeriSMo share an encryption key; VMPL access permissions separate them. Sibling VMs are omitted because the model grants the hypervisor a superset of their capabilities. Reads of externally shared memory/registers are unconstrained. §§4.2,5.1.2: External interference is handled in a machine state transition model; internal concurrency uses ownership and permissions. Primitive instruction wrappers contain trusted unsafe assembly and fully trusted postconditions derived from AMD manuals. Only critical memory/cache operations are modeled, under stated restrictions. Direct GHCB communication replaces code that might trigger #VC; unexpected #VC or other interrupts terminate the VM. Hardware-model completeness and faithfulness remain assumptions. §5.2 Listings 4–7: Confidentiality assumes an invariant-valid state, a successful secret E0 read, equal system physical addresses and a successful other-entity read; it concludes that the latter is encrypted. Integrity assumes invariant validity, attack reachability and successful E0 reads in both states; it concludes equal returned data. Neither theorem forces a read to succeed. Invariant preservation restricts E0 operations by software preconditions; attack-model closure covers arbitrary non-E0 operations. These are model-level security statements, not a complete cryptographic or microarchitectural side-channel theorem. §5.3 Lemma 1: After a validated VM-private GPA binds to an SPA, hypervisor changes leave it bound to the same SPA or make access fail, provided E0 does not revalidate an already bound GPA. Lemma 2: VM-private secrets remain encrypted to hypervisor reads; the guest C bit is required, whereas a permanently set RMP validation bit is unnecessary and cannot be assumed. A hypervisor can invalidate memory without gaining plaintext access. §5.3 Lemma 3: VMPL0-private memory remains unreadable by E3 and its E0 read either returns the original value or fails under Ehv/E3 actions. rmpadjust must not grant guest access to private pages. Lemma 4 extends same-or-fail translation from GPA to GVA using protected page tables. Lemma 5 requires a one-to-one mapping and separate page-table versus ordinary write APIs. §§5.3.3,6.1.1: Initial E0 page tables are trusted to reside in designated private PT memory and map GVAs one-to-one to GPAs. pt_write must preserve both conditions. Initial uniqueness of memory permissions is also assumed, then ownership/non-copyability preserves uniqueness. These initialization assumptions must be checked in a deployment; preservation alone does not establish the initial state. §§5.3.4,6.1: The connecting proof turns software-tracked constraints into hardware constraints when an operation succeeds. SnpPointsTo records GVA identity, optional value and software/hardware RMP/page-table/is_pt attributes. Tracked resources cannot be copied or freely constructed. Fine-grained permissions avoid carrying a global machine state through each implementation proof. §6.1.1: Object sizes, object/byte casts with consistent size and unique bindings, and raw-memory splitting/merging with consistent attributes are trusted specifications or axioms. Rust ownership does not verify these representation facts automatically. Listings 8–11 illustrate permission interfaces and deliberate rejected operations; their abbreviated syntax is not a separately compiled proof artifact. §6.1.2–6.1.3: Lock acquisition transfers a stored memory permission to the holder; release requires restoration of its invariant and returns ownership. Values can change between separate acquisitions. A local lock cannot prevent malicious guest/hypervisor access to a shared page: its permission allows copying and writing but no borrowed reference. Unrestricted reference access requires copying to private memory or safely making the page private. §6.1.4: Registers have per-CPU permissions. GHCB MSR is shared and reads are unconstrained. Writing IDTR/GDTR transfers ownership of the pointed-to memory to hardware. Explicit RIP/SP access is excluded. Updating CR3 requires mutable permissions for the page table and all usable virtual memory, so the single kernel page table is installed only once at boot. These restrictions limit the verified implementation, rather than verifying arbitrary register-manipulating code. §6.2: Security types pair a value with per-entity possible-value sets; full set means secret and singleton means public. Operators propagate sets; comparisons require public operands, and memory addresses must be public. Non-confidential memory may contain only values public to the relevant entity. Trusted downgrade converts a set to a singleton; downgraded keys fail crypto preconditions requiring full secrecy. §6.2 reader qualification: A possible-value set is not a probability distribution or an entropy bound. The paper describes a tracking discipline and trusted cryptographic interfaces, not a general proof that arbitrary full-support values are uniformly random. Binary-operator equation is printed with a op b inside a set quantified over v/u; it is illustrative notation and is not a checked artifact definition in this reading. No flaw in the external implementation is inferred. §§3.4,6.2,7.1: Only three secret categories are used: hardware VM communication keys, VeriSMo key pair and guest symmetric keys. Their uses go through a completely trusted external verified crypto library. Trust also includes ground-truth axioms, functional/security specifications, primitive APIs, Rust core interfaces, Verus and Rust compiler. External HACL verification is not re-established by this paper. §3.4: Guest page sharing must use the module API; otherwise those pages will not be validated again. LockKernel is callable once and removes guest write permission from declared kernel-code pages and supervisor-execution permission from other pages. The caller chooses code ranges; this does not establish that the chosen code is benign or that a malicious guest supplies a truthful semantic runtime measurement. §3.4: ExtendPCR maintains a hash chain beginning with starting code/configuration; Attest binds a nonce, a hardware-attested module identity and module-attested chain. DeriveKey ties a retained encryption key to the runtime measurement and guest uses Encrypt/Decrypt without receiving it. Protocol/crypto behavior and meaning of measured guest inputs depend on trusted specifications; no remote distributed attestation workflow is evaluated. §7.2: OSMEM executable ranges and tracked permission map share an invariant equating range membership with possession of page permissions. One lock protects both the bookkeeping and permission-controlled RMP modifications. Handler range checks are forced by the need to obtain a tracked page permission. This establishes specific safety/range constraints, not every possible API intent property without a specification. §8.1: API averages/standard deviations come from 100 calls each. Switch measures the full VMPL3→hypervisor→VMPL0→hypervisor→VMPL3 round trip. Attestation has much greater variability than cheap calls; LockKernel is expensive but once per session. Table 1 provides milliseconds and thousands of cycles, not proof runtime. §8.2: UnixBench compares VeriSMo, Linux-sm, C-sm and Linux. Authors report almost zero overhead and comparable performance, but do not publish per-bar confidence intervals or an all-workload equivalence bound. Figure 5 contains visible differences, including Linux above modules on some bars. Linux-sm/C-sm implement only WakeUpAP, SetPageShared and SetPagePrivate, whereas VeriSMo provides more APIs. These comparisons are not a controlled isolation of verification cost for identical feature sets. §§8.2,8.4: Up to 40% Hecate slowdown is attributed to prior work and its #VC trapping, not a new head-to-head run. AMD SVSM/COCONUT performance is not compared because the hypervisor differs. Unsafe block counts (32 VeriSMo, 150 AMD SVSM, 235 COCONUT) are source snapshots reported in 2024; lower block count alone proves neither comparative security nor absence of bugs. §8.3: Reports about six minutes for both proof layers using multithreaded Verus and Z3 4.11.2 on 32 cores. Table 2 includes 803 Verus-library axiom LOC and 101 trusted LOC as well as project axioms/trusted wrappers; the 31 unsafe Rust LOC for hardware instructions is not the total trusted code. The allocator interface is trusted, although its implementation is verified. Proof/spec annotations are approximately twice executable implementation size, not a uniform ratio for every module. §8.4.1: The remapping vulnerability was detected during verification; authors report an AMD disclosure in early May 2023 and security fix, with early COCONUT still retaining the older bug. The reading checks the paper account and cited commit pointer only, not a reproduced exploit, current project state, or independent fix audit. No artifact or proof replay was executed.
    - how much was read: most of it read
    - [source](https://www.usenix.org/conference/osdi24/presentation/zhou); [notes: VeriSMo: A Verified Security Module for Confidential VMs](../distributed_verification_review_b/deep_1_verified_implementations.md)
    - record id: `verismo-2024`

    - title: Verus: A Practical Foundation for Systems Verification
    - what: A Rust verifier for systems code. The evaluation (Section 6, Figure 9) is at the component level.

- ByMC (2023)
    - title: ByMC: Byzantine Model Checker, and Survey on Parameterized Verification with Threshold Automata
    - what: ByMC models nodes that count messages against thresholds, abstracts the counts, and uses an SMT solver to check safety and liveness for every allowed number of processes and faults. Verified for both safety and liveness: folklore and Srikanth-Toueg reliable broadcast, asynchronous Byzantine agreement, nonblocking atomic commit, condition-based consensus, CF1S, C1CS, and BOSCO. The randomised protocols (Ben-Or, Byzantine consensus, k-set agreement and RS-BOSCO) get safety plus almost-sure termination (they finish with probability 1).
    - number: Table 4 (§6.2, p.5:20) lists the verified protocols
    - limits: The randomised cases hold only against adversaries that respect round boundaries. The Tendermint one-round agreement analysis covers safety only. The benchmarks are compact models, not runnable implementations. The review notes did not recover the original 2018 ISoLA chapter and read the 2023 survey instead. The review notes found no human person-time.

- Decouchant-KulahciogluOzkan-Zhou liveness testing (2023)
    - title: Liveness Checking of the HotStuff Protocol Family
    - what: Extends Twins automated BFT testing with temperature-based and lasso-based liveness detection using partial/hot state abstractions, and compares them with bounded-time liveness checks on HotStuff, 2-Phase HotStuff, and Sync HotStuff. Defines partial states that retain liveness-relevant view/lock information and hot states used by temperature and cycle detection; lasso detection caches repeated hot states without controlled scheduling. Twins scenarios use two partitions, four honest processes and one Byzantine twin, with 1,000 unit tests for execution timing and 10,000 random scenarios per protocol evaluation. Baseline timeout checks report network-partition delays as violations; temperature/lasso distinguish persistent conflicting locks. Sync HotStuff’s early version exposes both safety and liveness faults.
    - number: 1,000 timing scenarios per configuration
    - number: 10,000 random scenarios
    - number: HotStuff small-timeout 20-round 97.6% false positives
    - number: 2-Phase 20-round temperature 0.17–1.92% liveness, 0 false positives
    - limits: Testing is bounded by generated rounds and state abstraction quality; temperature threshold selection trades false positives against runtime, while lasso can use more memory. Twins models Byzantine behavior through twin replicas and injected network faults; results are not formal proofs and depend on implementation/version (Sync HotStuff eprint 20190312:115828, Δ=50 ms).
    - how much was read: most of it read

The evaluation uses small 22–58-line files and one-hour checker timeouts, establishing bug-finding value rather than broad performance.

- Netrix guided consensus testing (2023)
    - title: A Domain Specific Language for Testing Consensus Implementations
    - what: Presents Netrix, a central networking server plus DSL for programmer-guided exploration of consensus implementations. Filters control message drops, delays, modifications and reordering; monitor state machines express safety/bounded-liveness assertions; residual nondeterminism is explored by PCTCP and tests are replayable. Formalizes protocol, monitor and product transition systems and proves monitor expressivity for complete runs. Instrumentation shims intercept RPC/message APIs; filters and key-value context encode semantic waypoints. Case studies cover Tendermint, Raft and BFTSmart across Go and Java, including regression tests and protocol-coverage measures. Filter-distance analysis predicts when PCTCP alone can find reorderings.
    - number: 34 unit tests total: 15 Tendermint, Raft tests, 8 BFTSmart (§5)
    - number: Instrumentation: 600 LOC added to 150k LOC Tendermint; 120/16k Raft; 150/16k BFTSmart
    - number: Tendermint found 4 new deviations; one liveness bug increased consensus time 30s→6min; fixes acknowledged
    - number: Raft tests found 4 documented bugs; PCTCP alone found none of new Tendermint or known Raft bugs
    - limits: Requires instrumenting communication APIs and serialization adapters; tests explore bounded executions (e.g., MaxEvents=1000, depth=10, 30s iterations), not exhaustive correctness. PCTCP cannot introduce Byzantine behavior and rapidly loses probability on deep reorderings. Findings are implementation-specific scenarios; passing tests do not prove safety or liveness. BFTSmart case found no bugs, possibly reflecting prior testing maturity.

- PRISM proof repair infrastructure (2023)
    - title: Proof Repair Infrastructure for Supervised Models: Building a Large Proof Repair Dataset
    - what: Introduces the initial PRISM Coq proof-repair dataset and its extraction infrastructure, with roughly 200 unique changes at publication. Defines proof repair with known repaired theorem statements and kernel-checking metrics; explains build, serialization, command alignment and project-history obstacles. It proposes infrastructure and evaluation practices, not a trained proof-repair model. Inputs include old theorem/proof, new theorem, changed dependencies, project state, raw text/ASTs/identifiers and intermediate goals/hypotheses. Partially applying an aligned pair of Git commits while leaving out one proof edit produces a broken proof plus supervised repair target. Across-project and chronological splits test different generalizations but no fixed split is committed. SwiM selects/clones cached opam environments; bwrap remaps cloned switches because binaries hard-code paths. strace recovers I/Q/R dependency flags from actual builds. SerAPI supplies states, while a heuristic parser and Vernacular queries work around extensible syntax, unqualified names, missing definition/conjecture queries, nested proofs and version drift. Command alignment solves a thresholded normalized-Levenshtein bipartite assignment over changed commands; it ignores proof bodies when comparing command text, supports moves across files/reordering, and uses t=0.4 to reject poor matches. Supplementary material lists target/candidate projects and a concrete hoare-tut exgcd.v repair replacing cutrewrite with replace, showing compact commit/diff/environment encoding of a much larger state.
    - number: initial release: a few Coq projects, roughly 200 unique changes
    - number: main text expansion target: 60 opam projects; appendix lists an additional 224 candidate projects
    - number: supplement Table 1 reports 171,775 proofs and 2,984,471 sentences; Table 2 reports 335,223 proofs and 6,078,063 sentences, each approximate one-snapshot inventory rather than repair counts
    - number: build attempts: about 68% failure across Coq versions 8.9–8.15 (seven major versions)
    - limits: The primary task assumes the new theorem and dependencies have already been repaired; successful checking establishes that stated theorem, not that a changed specification captures intent. Specification/definition repair lacks a satisfactory metric; exact equality is conservative and BLEU-like distances are inadequate. Initial release has a few projects and roughly 200 changes, whereas 60-project expansion and later large-scale coverage are planned. Main text and supplementary Table 1 use inconsistent wording about whether all 60 projects are already included; appendix prose explicitly calls repositories current and pending. Supplementary proof/sentence totals describe approximations from one snapshot per repository and are not repair-instance counts. Name resolution relies on handcrafted scopes and assumptions about shadowing; conjecture tracking assumes Show Conjectures order and proof-boundary behavior; grammar extensions can defeat serialization. Roughly 68% build-failure rate counts attempts across seven Coq versions 8.9–8.15, so it is not a rate of irreparably broken projects. No trained ML model, proof-repair accuracy, fixed benchmark split, or model comparison is reported.
    - how much was read: most of it read
    - [source](https://doi.org/10.4230/LIPIcs.ITP.2023.26)
    - record id: `prism-proof-repair-infrastructure-2023`
- Real-Time Double-Ended Queue Verified (Proof Pearl) (2023)
    - what: Isabelle/HOL verification of a persistent purely functional deque based on Chuang and Goldberg. Refines its representation into explicit rebalancing phases and proves list-based functional behavior and invariant preservation. Fixed work on each update yields worst-case constant-time operations under a unit-cost arithmetic/list-primitive model; paper gives a code-inspection complexity argument, not timing benchmarks. The main work is specifying intermediate rebalancing states: two simultaneous abstractions describe current observable contents and the eventual balanced ends; a remaining-step function and size_ok inequalities ensure both ends survive updates before rebalancing finishes. Split stack representations avoid final linear append. Small bounded deques are expressed with constructors rather than an implicit list-length restriction.
    - limits: Sequential immutable structure with persistence, not a concurrent deque. Interleaved enqueue/dequeue operations occur BETWEEN bounded rebalancing slices, not simultaneous threads. Prior versions remain available through functional values. Correctness equations require invar, except the stated empty abstraction equation. first/dequeue behavior has a nonempty-list precondition. Missing empty/invalid-state pattern cases are outside that specified behavior; no total checked error-return API is described. The abstract meaning is listL, with listR=rev(listL). Left/right enqueue prepends in the corresponding orientation; valid nonempty dequeue removes the first corresponding element. Isabelle HOL lists/naturals and total logic do not imply defined useful behavior for arbitrary malformed constructors. Rebalancing has phase-dependent invariants. A local Small1 state cannot independently determine its final list because transferred big-side elements are missing; the joint States abstraction supplies that information. Specifications over the partial local abstraction must stay in phase-appropriate contexts. Runtime code is the yellow-background material. list/list_current, remaining_steps and proof invariants may traverse large lists; these are ghost definitions for verification, not functions called by every deque update. Similarly size_new and some overloaded size meanings are logical descriptions; executable counters implement needed constant-time size tests. Worst-case O(1) uses constant-time arithmetic, list cons/head/tail and fixed-depth nonrecursive helper calls. It is not a measured physical real-time deadline or a claim about unbounded-integer bit complexity, garbage collection, code-generator/runtime correctness or allocator latency. §5.7 argues constant time from nonrecursion and primitive operations. The paper presents no separate instrumented cost semantics/theorem, exact instruction bound, executable benchmark, space-allocation measurement or proof-effort comparison. Public updates on a valid Rebal state perform four joint steps; an update starting rebalancing performs six. Phase transitions and invariant slack, not merely the 3:1 idle balance condition, establish safe completion before an end can run out. The publication says the formalization has 4,400 definition/proof lines and changes some names for presentation. Several printed code snippets have type/name/direction inconsistencies, so the displayed pseudocode should not be copied as an executable artifact without consulting the formal source. This record has not checked the repository.
    - how much was read: most of it read
    - [source](https://doi.org/10.4230/LIPIcs.ITP.2023.29)
    - record id: `real-time-double-ended-2023`

Hardware affects timeouts. 5,861 generated programs were not classified due to Clang compilation failures or ESBMC crashes. CWE-242 reports were omitted because reported locations could point to a library declaration. Code and results files were not inspected.

    - what: Builds three linked TLA+ specifications for ZooKeeper/Zab: an abstract protocol specification, a detailed system specification serving as a super-document, and a test specification that drives explorative implementation testing. - Cosmos DB TLA+ (2022)
    - title: Understanding Inconsistency in Azure Cosmos DB with TLA+
    - what: # Whole-paper read: Understanding Inconsistency in Azure Cosmos DB with TLA+ (authored by agents unless marked 🧑)  A. Finn Hackett, Joshua Rowe and Markus Alexander Kuppe; arXiv2210.13661v1,24October2022,11pages. This is a client-facing behavioral model and industrial explanation case study. It reports390lines of core specification developed over2person-months,24non-type properties checked by bounded exploration/simulation, two documentation issues and an abstract explanation of an Azure outage. The service implementation is represented through expert feedback, without automated conformance checking. [Q0,Q1,Q11,Q19]  ## Model and assumptions  The four conceptual variables are an ordered write log, a universally replicated prefix readIndex, a durable majority-replicated prefix commitIndex, and a failover epoch. Their relationship is readIndex≤commitIndex≤Len(log). Replication nondeterministically increases either prefix; data loss truncates only the non-durable suffix and increases epoch. The log abstracts replica/region arrangements rather than enumerating them. The retrieved source additionally carries the configured write level as an unchanged state variable; the paper calls it a constant. [Q3,Q4,Q5,S1]  One write consistency level is configured for a deployment; individual reads may select an allowed weaker level. The model concerns individual single-key operations. Transactions and optimistic concurrency are outside its scope. Bounded staleness models operation counts, omitting the wall-clock bound. VersionBound limits unreplicated entries for all write levels; StalenessBound separately limits non-durable entries when bounded-staleness writes are configured. These abstractions should not be mistaken for a proof covering arbitrary implementation arrangements merely because the model does not explicitly contain servers. [Q3,Q5,Q14]  A write begins by appending its key/value and obtaining an epoch/checkpoint token. It can later succeed or fail. Failure need not delete the value: the request can have taken effect even if its response was lost. Weak reads can see the value while the write remains pending. Successful strong writes require durability. An epoch change invalidates all old session tokens in this model; authors acknowledge that real data loss need not invalidate every token. [Q6,Q7,Q9,Q18]  GeneralRead(key,index,allowDirty) returns the latest mapping at or before index, or a not-found marker if absent. If dirty reads are allowed, it also permits later matching entries. Strong reads use commitIndex without dirty values; bounded-staleness reads use commitIndex with dirty values; session reads use max(token.checkpoint,readIndex) with dirty values and require a current-epoch or null token. The client updates its token with the maximum prior/read checkpoint. Prefix/eventual reads both use readIndex with dirty values. This last equivalence concerns single reads/writes, not transactions. [Q8,Q9,Q10,Q14]  ## Validation and its limits  Authors report10liveness and14safety properties across4distinct levels, excluding basic typing, using exhaustive logs up to length6 and depth-first random traces up to100steps. Examples include durable committed prefixes, read-your-writes, monotonic reads, staleness bounds, token lifetime and eventual write completion. The paper gives no complete per-property run logs, state counts, runtimes or mapping of every property to each exploration bound. These are bounded model checks and sampled traces, not a parameterized proof or implementation test. [Q11]  The pinned artifact gives useful additional scope. MCCosmosDBProps uses two keys, three values, StalenessBound2,VersionBound3,Len(log)<3 and epoch<3. Its configuration labels several temporal checks “very very slow (1hr+)” and three “overloads TLC (out of memory), probably holds”. SimCosmosDBProps has a finite natural-number replacement0..100 and omits some costly properties. These comments are evidence about the retrieved snapshot, not proof that the paper's historical runs failed; neither do they substantiate completion of every stated check. The history specification assumes weak fairness of the disjunction WriteSuccess∨WriteFail; the simulation specification omits that fairness clause. Thus completion claims depend on the specification and bounds actually used. I did not establish an unbounded per-write progress theorem. [S2,S3,S4]  CosmosDBLinearizability maps strong read results to an atomic dictionary view. A commitIndex jump can correspond to multiple dictionary writes in one abstract step. The saved configuration asks for DictSpec under epoch<3 and Len(log)<4. This supports the paper's model-level refinement argument; I did not independently establish a full invocation/response-history linearizability theorem. The old-model refinement goes from the older model to this newer behavioral specification, so old behaviors are included in the new model. The claimed strictness additionally rests on the newer model admitting data loss and dirty reads omitted before. The retrieved mapping fixes two regions/two clients per region/one write region and constrains history length<5. I inspected the mapping, not its old-model dependency closure. [Q12,S5,S6]  The read hierarchy is checked while holding write consistency fixed and considering all session-token choices. Comparing differently configured write levels does not give the same inclusion: strong writes can admit more pending non-durable values than bounded-staleness writes. The four retrieved adjacent read-refinement modules use fixed write configuration and small finite state constraints; no unbounded theorem proof is attached to their THEOREM declarations. [Q13,S7]  ## Concrete cases and industrial results  The first documentation issue is a claim that prefix reads cannot observe A,C or B,A,C after sequential writes A,B,C. A,C is possible simply by interleaving reads around writes. For B,A, use five replicas: A reaches1,2,3;B reaches1,4,5;one read hits5 and another2 before replication catches up. Each write reached a local majority, yet the client seesBthenA. These are allowed service behaviors, not newly demonstrated implementation failures. Authors report the documentation was rewritten. [Q14]  The second issue concerns regional guarantees. Bounded-staleness operations in one region may appear strongly consistent until a region failover loses or exposes older data; they lack global durability. Authors report their argument was confirmed for individual operations, while regional documentation bullet points remained because transactions distinguish prefix and eventual behavior. The paper's section title about regions must be read with that explicit restriction. [Q14]  The outage affected thousands of subscriptions over28days and was noticed after26days. FrontDoor wrote task data, then asked Worker to read it. Session consistency was configured, but the processes used separate/unconfigured session tokens. Their cross-process interaction therefore had only eventual-read guarantees. A routing change exposed the existing semantic problem through latency. A PlusCal model reproduces a missing read before replication; forwarding FrontDoor's token to Worker removes the modeled error according to the authors. The original postmortem author confirmed the explanation made sense. This is an abstract causal explanation and proposed/rechecked model fix, not evidence that the fix was deployed or that every production trace was matched. [Q15,Q16,Q17]  Other cases show why the model has two-step writes: a weaker read can observe an incomplete strong write; a failed write can remain durable; and a bounded-staleness read under strong-write configuration lacks the separate bounded-staleness write throttle. Although the paper says strong consistency has no bound on in-progress requests, the model still has VersionBound on partially replicated entries; completed replication permits more pending requests. These are distinct quantities. Expert review caught an earlier incorrect atomic-write abstraction, which was rewritten and rechecked in2–3days. [Q18,Q19]  ## Printed text versus pinned source  The displayed WriteCanSucceed on paperp4 checks token validity and, for strong writes, token.epoch=epoch. It omits the checkpoint≤commitIndex condition that the immediately following prose says is required. The pinned CosmosDB.tla includes that durability conjunct. Thus the displayed formula and prose disagree; this does not establish that the checked source permits non-durable successful strong writes. [Q7,S1]  The outage text's expected formula is eventually workerValue="workerValue", but Listing1writes"taskValue". The source's temporal property uses eventually workerValue="taskValue" and its terminal-state property checks the same literal. I treat the printed literal as a presentation mismatch and preserve the intended missing-task-value scenario rather than presenting the printed formula as a valid test of that scenario. [Q16,S8]  These comparisons use a retrieved, pinned repository snapshot, not an asserted reconstruction of the exact2022experiment checkout. No author code or proof/checker was run. [S9]  ## Contribution and reading scope  For this database, the contribution is a reusable specification of a distributed dependency's visible behavior and a concrete demonstration that model correctness also depends on choosing faithful abstractions. Its relevance to LLM-written specifications is my inference: evaluation should test behavior the model must permit as well as properties it must forbid, record expert corrections, and distinguish bounded self-consistency from service conformance. The paper has no Verus, Rust or LLM experiment. [Q19,Q20]  Read all11pages,28reference entries, every displayed definition, Listing1,all4figures and TableI; inspected all11page renders across the preceding and current context. Read the complete core/property/read-wrapper/client/linearizability modules, both PlusCal outage modules, both model-check/simulation wrappers and configurations, four read-level refinement modules and their common-pattern constraints, the general-model mapping/configuration, and two repository READMEs.26static files acquired with hashes and Git blob identities. No old general-model dependency closure or broader implementation inspected. Official and inventory PDFs are byte-identical. No author execution/import, solver, compiler, checker or serialized scientific-results loading.
    - number: 2 person-month development
    - number: 10 liveness and 14 safety properties across 4 consistency levels
    - limits: single-keyindividualoperations;countnotwallclock;alltokensinvalidatedonepoch;boundedchecks;expertconformanceonly
    - how much was read: most of it read
