- Demystifying and Checking Silent Semantic Violations in Large Distributed Systems (2022)
    - what: Empirical study of 109 production silent semantic failures from nine systems, followed by Oathkeeper: a runtime checker that learns likely event-relation rules from patched regression traces, keeps rules failing on corresponding buggy traces, filters against the remaining patched tests, and monitors future execution. It detects six of seven selected newer known failures and reports activation-conditioned false-positive rates. This is trace-based inference and runtime detection, not an inductive proof of distributed-system correctness. Records the ticket-sampling population and bias, thirteen study findings, exact before/after regression workflow, event and state instrumentation boundaries, counter-based implication semantics, inactive-rule distinction, finite-window/recheck tradeoffs, all seven newer contracts, cross-case transfer limits, all throughput values and activation-conditioned false-positive denominators.
    - limits: §2: Semantics are guarantees about output, state and subsequent operations in response to requests, commands, messages, environment changes or time. Silent violations leave the system operational without explicit crash/timeout/exception signals. A later error in another operation can expose the earlier violation. The scope includes system-specific contracts beyond protocol consistency. §3: Tickets must be resolved server bugs above minor priority. Iterative random sampling balances candidate counts across systems; nonproduction/internal-test cases and explicit failures excluded. In-depth 109 cases prefer information-rich discussions among 268 candidates from 747 valid cases in 1,922 sampled tickets out of 38,786 total. Multiple inspectors reconcile classifications. Selection, reporting, survival and Java/C++ biases prevent interpreting percentages as universal production failure probabilities. §4: Reported mean 39% (range 20–57%) is the paper’s average of candidate/valid ratios across sampled systems. Priority labels normalized to blocker/critical/major; 45% of studied cases are blocker or critical. This is historical issue prioritization, not independent measurement of severity. Consequences in Figure 3 differ from the violated guarantee itself and can arise later. §5: 87% of violated semantics explicitly documented (53% API, 24% internal behavior, 10% configuration); remaining 13% implicit expectations. Documentation can be vague/incomplete and is not mechanically enforced. Old semantics means present in first major stable release. Their 68% share has no denominator of all old/new contracts, which authors explicitly did not count; it does not estimate per-contract risk. §5–7: 74% of violations judged locally, sometimes using replicated local session/node state; 26% need global information. This is case classification, not a proof that every locally observed trace represents a consistent distributed snapshot. Safety/liveness classification follows official documentation: 86% safety, 14% liveness. Finite absence of an eventual response alone cannot establish general liveness failure. §6–8: Only 12% assigned familiar memory/race/overflow bug patterns; most are varied system logic/design defects. Two-thirds violate guarantees extending beyond operation return, so single-entry assertions can miss later failures. Existing semantic test coverage in 73% of cases does not cover all triggering combinations. MongoDB’s production assertions correlate with its lower silent-failure fraction, without causal identification. §8.3: Observability percentages derive from only 34 tickets explicitly describing how users noticed the problem, not all 109. Every observed case had already been discovered, creating survival bias. Overlapping APIs and state-query APIs are recommendations from these observations rather than evaluated interventions. §9.1–9.2: Users provide build/test configuration and past failure commit IDs/test names, optionally classes and compatibility patches. Tool executes patched and preceding buggy versions; regression tests copied to buggy code may require interface fixes. Evaluation instead adds switches to enable/disable old patches. Useful input therefore depends on reproducible old failures and adequate regression tests. §9.2: Java load-time bytecode hooks at method entry, return and exception; state hooks target primitive/collection members in patch-affected classes plus optional user classes. These are selected observable events, not every semantic state or an automatically proved mapping from documentation. Event records contain context such as variable name/location/time. Operation-event uniqueness in the illustrated template uses method signature; paper does not prove per-client/object obligation matching or cross-node ordering soundness. §9.3: Eighteen manually designed templates constrain candidate search to event types and relation structures; seven examples appear in Table 3, full list deferred to technical report. Parameterized matches are likely invariants of the observed traces, not exact/full high-level semantics. No induction, model checker or universal proof validates unseen executions. Templates require domain knowledge but avoid writing each concrete rule manually. §9.3/Algorithm 2: p⇒q uses one counter per distinct ordered event pair. Each p increments outstanding count; subsequent q decrements positive count. Earlier q does not satisfy later p; one q discharges one outstanding p. Zero final counter yields candidate rule. This is stronger than existence of one later q for all p and still depends on event identity/context. Enumeration is affordable for reported test traces, not claimed scalable to arbitrary production histories. §9.4: PASS requires activated antecedent and no contradiction, INACTIVE means antecedent absent, FAIL means contradiction. Retain rules passing patched regression and failing buggy trace; then discard those contradicted by any patched test, excluding start/shutdown/thread-run regions via test markers. Inactivity is not positive evidence of universal correctness; test filtering can retain coincidences and eliminate desirable properties if tests differ in intended scope. Listing 1/Algorithms 1–2, p.100: Printed Verify interface says true means continue scanning; generic Algorithm 1 breaks when scan returns true, while Algorithm 2 returns true for every event. Generic Verify also omits the prescan initialization called for by the interface/example. Record these as apparent pseudocode inconsistencies; no implementation inspected, so do not infer a tool defect. §9.5–9.6: Production checker runs periodically, logs counterexamples, rechecks shortly to tolerate transient violations, and alerts rather than crashes the service. Recent-event window exceeds checking period; time-related events retained by parameter expiry. No formal proof of event completeness, buffer-loss freedom, ordering or preservation of arbitrarily long pending obligations under window expiry. Periodic finite-trace checks and short recheck are practical monitors, not general liveness verification. §9.6–9.8: Survivor optimization stops validating a rule once disproved; related tests prioritized. Ring buffers decouple emission/checking, reuse expired objects and preallocate per event type. Assumptions: contracts expressible through simple event relations, enough good tests, and old patches without major redesign/interface changes. Java/JDK 8, Javassist and JUnit implementation; no Rust/Verus/TLA+ or LLM specification/proof experiment. §10.1–10.2: Eight ZooKeeper, ten HDFS and eight Kafka old failures selected with regression tests. Seven later cases are known bugs chosen for related semantics but different root causes, nine to 34 months later. Six detections show transfer within that selected scope; not prospective discovery of six unknown bugs or recall over all failures. Unseen semantics expected to be harder. ZK-1667 fits a template but training test quality yields wrong rules. §10.2: Mean 0.91 s measured from violation onset after deliberately triggering reproduction soon, not client-operation start or production incident time. Comparator uses Daikon on instrumented state variables, the invariant engine underlying Dinv, and detects one case with noisy rules; not a comprehensive unchanged Dinv deployment comparison. §10.2 additional experiment: 22 ZooKeeper failures sorted by ticket time. Six individual failure-derived rule sets transfer to a median five failures; 16 detect only their own failure within the pool. Figure 11 aggregates a sliding subset as caption states. This assesses overlap in a historical pool, not independent prospective validation or a universal specification. §10.3–10.5: Runtime overhead measured on three stated workloads, not arbitrary production load; checker asynchronous. Only 11%, 66%, 48% of rules activate on ZooKeeper/HDFS/Kafka, respectively. False-positive rates 4%, 9%, 12% apply among activated rules, not all rules, requests or failures. Paper does not report observation duration, confidence intervals or exhaustive false-negative rates. Suggested profiling/banning false rules not evaluated here. Scope: All evidence below is read from the proceedings paper; no tool, benchmark, regression tests or repository executed. Artifact availability/functionality/reproduction badges are printed on p.91; they are not an independent reproduction by this reader.
    - how much was read: most of it read

- DuoAI (2022)
    - title: DuoAI: Fast, Automated Inference of Inductive Invariants for Verifying Distributed Protocols
    - what: Infers inductive safety invariants for Ivy distributed-protocol models by filtering formulas on reachable simulation states and refining candidates with Ivy/Z3. A finite directed implication graph organizes formula weakening. Runs top-down weakening, bottom-up universal-core strengthening and a universal-only search in parallel. Reports26of27protocols solved, including five Paxos variants; vertical Paxos exhausts memory. Completeness is relative to an adequate finite decidable formula space and graph/optimization correctness, with a reader counterexample to the printed arbitrary-alternation decomposition extension. Supplies exact/approximate implication graphs, sample-preserving coverage theorem, monotonic-refinement proof, counterexample-pruned powerset search proof, quantified-variable/type ordering, mutual-implication optimizations and all benchmark outcomes. Explains which refinement wins per protocol, why hundreds of existential candidates can stall SMT, what human choices baselines require, and how specification/retrofitting differences affect claims of automation. Reader checks distinguish sufficient invariant inference from implemented-system verification and identify a quantifier-dependency flaw in a printed optimization lemma.
    - number: solves 26 of 27 protocols including Paxos variants; Vertical Paxos runs out of memory
    - number: Fast Paxos takes 26,979 seconds (about 7.5 hours)
    - number: solves all but 1 of 27 protocols (Vertical Paxos unsolved)
    - number: evaluation permits a week per tool and protocol
    - limits: Inputs are an Ivy protocol transition model and safety property, not arbitrary executable distributed code. Trusted Ivy/Z3 checks initialization and transition preservation of the conjunction and safety implication for all modeled instances, rather than bounded simulation being the certificate. Scope is safety; no liveness, crash-recovery implementation refinement, verified Rust/Verus proof or LLM-agent evaluation. Sampled states are assumed genuinely reachable; incorrect simulation could discard needed true invariants and compromise completeness, though final sound SMT checks still guard returned results. Finite formula search space uses prenex DNF, relational predicates/equality; extension to uninterpreted functions referred to DistAI. Bounds are quantified variables per sort, max_exists, max_literal, max_and and max_or. Tautologies/contradictions excluded. Nonempty sorts are implicit in universal-to-existential implication. Quantifier alternation must follow a fixed sort order, inferred partly from axioms or supplied by user; absent user choice multiple orders are tried in parallel. The actual full verification condition, including protocol axioms/transitions, must remain in the intended decidable fragment. Exact graph is transitive reduction of strict implication over finite formulas; syntactic implementation instead uses five sound weakening shapes and may connect equivalent formulas. Lemmas1–5 establish claimed acyclicity, implication and coverage of nontrivial formulas from selected roots. No theorem says every semantic implication is an edge/path in the approximate graph; coverage plus sound directed weakening is what enumeration/refinement need. Enumeration checks original variable-length states at multiple finite instance sizes, rather than DistAI incomplete fixed-length subsamples. A candidate is retained if it holds on all sampled states; descendants can be skipped if dominated by an existing candidate. Theorem1 says every true invariant in S is either itself retained or has a stronger retained ancestor. Retained candidates can still be false on unseen reachable states; simulation removes candidates, not proof obligations. Top-down refinement checks conjunction plus safety, replaces a failed candidate by noninvalidated immediate successors not already dominated by a reachable ancestor, and returns NotProvable if safety fails. Reachable ancestor here means a graph path avoiding invalidated nodes, not a reachable protocol state. Theorem2 maintains domination of every member of an adequate inductive solution; finite formulas each invalidated at most once give finite refinement assuming every solver check terminates. Bottom-up first weakens all universal candidates until inductive, explicitly ignoring safety, to obtain universal core. noncore includes existential candidates AND universal candidates not in the core whose inductiveness may require existential support. Enumerates subsets by size, calls top-down on core plus subset, and skips subsets holding on an already returned negative prestate. A failed safety-induction counterexample is a possibly unreachable state that invariants must exclude; strengthening can remove it. Theorem3 eventually tries all noncore, reducing to full top-down, conditional on correct pruning and finite solver decisions. Relative completeness is not a guarantee to prove every correct distributed protocol. Needed inductive solution must be expressible in chosen finite S and decidable fragment, samples/graph/optimizations must preserve it, solver calls must terminate, and resources must suffice. NotProvable means this search space cannot prove the property, not the protocol unsafe. Increasing size in round-robin has no practical resource bound; vertical Paxos memout is concrete failure despite expert invariants existing. Defaults max_literal4,max_or3,max_and3,max_exists1 unless safety already has>=2existentials; quantified variables per sort initially maximum arity occurrence in any relation. On failure increments literal/OR/AND/existential/per-sort-variable bounds round-robin. Runs a third max_exists0top-down worker along with general top-down/bottom-up; benchmark walltimes benefit from parallel simulation/refinement and possible sort-order searches, with no separate CPU-time/worker-count-normalized comparison. Mutual implication optimization suppresses Q if retained P1...Pk jointly imply Q, then reintroduces Q if a supporting candidate invalidated. Equivalence decomposition suppresses a formula permanently only when conjunction of smaller formulas is truly equivalent. Examples separate two independent existential witnesses, same witness, and distinct witnesses: existential dependency matters to logical validity, not just speed. Decomposition can require representation of each component within allowed search space. Reader counterexample to Lemma6 claimed arbitrary-alternation/skolemization extension: sorts A,X each{0,1}, fixed order A before X. Let p(x) iff x=0, q(x) iff x=1, r(a,x) iff a=x. F=exists a forall x.((p(x) AND q(x)) OR r(a,x)) is false. The selected conjunction p(x) AND q(x) has two components sharing no existential variable. Claimed components exists a forall x.(p(x) OR r(a,x)) and exists a forall x.(q(x) OR r(a,x)) are both true, choosing a=1 and a=0 respectively. A globally chosen existential witness in the other disjunct cannot be chosen separately. Proof for forall-X exists-YZ is valid under its independence conditions; extending it to arbitrary alternation is unsupported and false as stated. Restrict prefix/dependency structure or preserve shared Skolem choices. This challenges printed optimization/completeness scope, not observed artifact behavior; final independent sound SMT checking can still reject unsafe outputs. Printed Lemma6 proof also calls f/g disjunctions of component literals although components of original conjunction must remain conjunctions for Eq19. This is a terminology/algebra transcription issue for multi-literal components, separate from the arbitrary-alternation counterexample. Lemma5 existential-root argument omits stated no-OR root condition; treat as proof detail needing completion rather than infer a specific artifact failure. Printed Algorithm2 loops only while candidates nonempty, then returns NotProvable; it omits explicit check for already-inductive safety when no strengthening candidates exist. For an unchanged Boolean b initiallyfalse and safety notb, an empty candidate set suffices but displayed procedure returns NotProvable without checking. Algorithm3 likewise needs defined empty universal core when Procedure1 returns no candidates. These are boundary/pseudocode qualifications; do not treat them as reproduced implementation failures. This empty-input example concerns the displayed procedure: default enumeration may retain not-b, and Theorem1 prevents an empty candidate set when a nonempty sufficient II-star is represented. It is not an end-to-end failure of default search. The O(m^2) discussion counts a possible pairwise invariant-dependency workload, not a formal polynomial bound on quantified SMT runtime. Solver cost can dominate even with few formulas. Paper compares14needed Paxos invariants verified in<1s, +10correctunnecessaryalternating invariants5min,+20over3h;149universal Chordinvariants8s. This motivates existential sparsity but is not repeated statistical scaling evidence. Evaluation27small Ivy models (21–123LOC,2–13relations),15universal-sufficient and12requiring existential solution in their presentations. Tools SWISS,IC3PO,FOL-IC3,DistAI,UPDR,I4, week cutoff. Fail,toolerror,Z3error,memout,timeout distinguished; no common success criterion should collapse these. Walltimes single reported numbers, no repeated-run distributions/seed sensitivity/memory measurements or solver-query-count ablations reported. Hardware paper reports DellPrecision5829,4.3GHz28-coreXeonW2175,62GBRAM,512GBSSDPro600p,Ubuntu18.04; reported core description preserved without independent hardware validation. Baselines configured with their documented settings: SWISS uses own published formula bounds per protocol; I4/IC3PO need finite-instance sizes, I4 starts minimum and increases on failure; FOL-IC3 runs universal/default modes and reports first success. Tablefootnotes attribute FOL-IC3Chord difference to threebugs in a competitor file and IC3PO successes to retrofitted protocols/manually supplied alternating clauses. These are DuoAI authors claims, not independently audited competitor defects or controlled equal-input performance results. Concurrent PFOLIC3 excluded from main comparison; its failure rates cited from other work, not own experiments. DuoAI solves26/27,15/15universal and11/12existential, fastest outright on15; SWISS19,IC3PO18,FOLIC318,DistAI14,UPDR14,I412. No competitor solves verticalPaxos here. Not every row is a speedup: Chord200.9s vsIC3PO17.1/DistAI58; locks/tablecontain subsecond differences and learning-ring alternatives. Paxos60.4s vsSWISS16665s(~275.91x),flexible78.7vs28337(~360.06x); comparison is measured selected tool/model configuration, not universal algorithmic superiority. Universal-only worker wins all15universal cases; top-down winsfive simpler existential cases and bottom-up wins hybridreliablebroadcast plusfivePaxosvariants. MultiPaxos615candidates/581alternating butonly2needed noncore; Paxos/flexible/multi/stoppable require subsetsize2,fastsize6. Vertical expert invariant8literals reduced7,60predicates/120signed gives search order120^7≈3.58e14; fast38predicates/5literals gives76^5≈2.54e9. These coarse powers are magnitude estimates ignoring syntax/equivalence, not measured exhaustive candidate counts. Implementation6.1KC++LOC(gcc7.5.0),2.3KPythonLOC(3.8.10) simulator. No machine-checked metatheory or independently executed artifact. Proof soundness/trust distinct from search completeness and practical automation; returned inductive safety certificates do not connect protocol abstraction to concurrent network code. Table1 has four IC3PO successes, three failures and five memory failures among the12existential rows. The §8,p.496 prose instead says six memory failures; the table, not that narrative count, is preserved in structured results. This discrepancy does not change IC3PO total18successes.

- Endive (2022)
    - title: Plain and Simple Inductive Invariant Inference for Distributed Protocols in TLA+
    - what: Samples candidate lemmas from a predicate grammar the user gives. Checks them on small finite instances. Greedily picks the lemmas that remove counterexamples to the induction step. A separate TLAPS proof is still needed for the protocol at unbounded size.
    - number: Invariant for 25 of 29 protocol benchmarks
    - number: Table I has 30 rows incl. MLDR; 2 rows have no TLA+ translation
    - number: Paper's Endive rerun in IC3Syn solves 24 shared cases
    - number: endive solved 29 benchmark protocols plus MLDR; MLDR row: 2,025 s, 7,751 CTIs, 1,272 s checking, 651 eliminated, 102 s CTI generation (Table II)
    - limits: The generated invariant is not guaranteed to be truly inductive, because counterexamples are sampled at random. Needs a grammar from the user.
    - how much was read: most of it read
    - [source](https://arxiv.org/abs/2205.06360); [notes: Endive: TLA+ candidates, followed by an unbounded proof](../distributed_verification_review_b/deep_4_invariant_inference_benchmarks.md); [notes: papers inspected](../distributed_verification_review_b/review_b_invariants_20261007.md)

- FRET realizability (2022)
    - title: Realizability Checking of Requirements in FRET
    - what: It checks whether requirements can be implemented, using Lustre and JKind/Kind2. It finds minimal unimplementable sets of requirements with delta debugging, and shows counterexample traces of the environment.
    - number: aircraft study: 11 requirements; removing the hover-initialization requirement made a conflicting requirement realizable
    - number: infusion pump: 12 requirements rewritten to 26 scoped ones, 8 minimal unrealizable cores

- Invariant inference with quantifier alternations (2022)
    - title: Inferring Invariants with Quantifier Alternations: Taming the Search Space Explosion
    - what: Presents a PDR/IC3 invariant-inference algorithm that learns first-order invariants with quantifier alternations. It lifts quantifier-prefix choice to the inductive-generalization level for parallel breadth-first search, introduces k-term pseudo-DNF (k-pDNF) matrices, and combines EPR restrictions, incremental SMT, multi-block generalization and heuristic may-proof-obligations. The IG procedure partitions prefix space into categories/workers, orders prefixes by quantifier depth/alternations/universals/existentials, shares constraints only from immediate sub-prefixes, and interleaves separation with SMT checks of Init and relative inductiveness. k-pDNF represents a negated cube followed by up to k−1 cubes, expressing implication-shaped Paxos lemmas more compactly than CNF/DNF. The complete P-Fol-Ic3 algorithm has learning and heuristic tasks, optional EPR edge restrictions, and solver robustness via restarts, parallel Z3/CVC5, and incremental assertions. Evaluation covers 30 safe distributed-protocol problems, including Paxos variants, with ablations for pDNF, EPR, incremental SMT and multi-block generalization.
    - number: 30 benchmark problems
    - number: 5 trials per algorithm; 16 threads, 20 GiB RAM, 6-hour timeout
    - number: paxos-epr 920 s (5/5), flexible-paxos-epr 418 s (5/5), multi-paxos-epr 4272 s (4/5)
    - number: k=1 universal and k=3 non-universal pDNF settings
    - limits: Benchmarks are safe transition systems with known human invariants; inference success is not a refutation method for unsafe systems. EPR requires a user-provided acyclic set of allowed sort/function edges, and prenex separation may need a special push-down mode. Full FOL search can be intractable; the method failed vertical-paxos-epr and several hardest cases, and stochastic parallelism yields variable success. Experiments use fixed 6-hour timeouts and 16 threads/20 GiB; performance and learned lemmas depend on solver behavior, random seeds and prefix restrictions.

- Learning to Find Proofs and Theorems by Learning to Refine Search Strategies: The Case of Loop Invariant Synthesis (2022)
    - what: Proposes self-training AlphaZero-style teacher and solver agents that refine generic nondeterministic programs for automated theorem proving without human proof examples. Demonstrates Looprl on loop-invariant synthesis: abductive strategy code defines a search space, a teacher generates constrained diverse Code2Inv problems, and a solver learns greedy action choices. On 132 single-loop C programs, trained policy solves 61.5% without backtracking versus 39.7% using an untrained-teacher dataset and 18.4% random actions; MCTS with the generic strategy solves all benchmarks but is not the learned no-search metric. Defines choose/event nondeterministic strategy operators, conditional generative teachers, abductive reasoning and bounded event rewards. A strategy induces an MDP whose choice points are actions; AlphaZero/MCTS policy and value heads refine both teacher and solver. Looprl compiles an OCaml DSL to RL environments, provides graph-format UI/debugging, integer-linear-arithmetic abduction, and parallel Gumbel AlphaZero. Teacher constraints include invariant shape, conditionals, usefulness of guard and equalities; solver reward penalizes proof steps. A complete run uses 20 teacher +20 solver iterations, 1.6M-parameter graph transformers, 50K solver training problems, and one16-hour RTX3080 run. Supplement findings: Appendix A expands the solver strategy to disjunctions of at most three atomic candidates, optional candidate strengthening, lazy constant metavariables with global constraints, and conjectures from preserved linear combinations, relaxed guards and unmodified initial variables. Conjecturing costs -0.3, abduction -0.2, each counted at most four times, with successful reward at least zero. Figure 7 provides all 94 lines of the strategy and a pending stack encoding. This makes the hand-designed search space and shaped reward explicit; the neural policy chooses within it. Appendix B/Table 3 lists 15 sampled constraint types; penalties are -0.5 except the last four at -0.2, and successful reward is at least -0.5. Table 4 hard constraints require correctness, nontrivial/nonredundant formulas, satisfiable invariants, entering the loop, and a restricted loop-terminates test. The paper explicitly says that test rules out only a subset of nontermination cases. It must not be described as a general termination guarantee. Constraint combinations and correlations are partly hardcoded; formula/program templates and parameter-abduction shape the generated distribution. Table 5 gives 12 final transformations, including irrelevant guards/formulas, commuting or shuffling instructions, comparison normalization, moving assumptions and weakening the final assertion. An application is cancelled if it violates a hard constraint or increases soft violations. Table 6 gives Code2Inv problems 3,7,93,110 and explanatory invariants: disjunctive x<5 or y≤z; x-y≤10; 3i=x+y with i≤n; i-s=1 with i≤n+1 or s=0. Table 7 prints three generated programs/invariants, with final transformations disabled for clarity and invariants hidden before solver training. They are illustrations, not additional held-out measurements. Appendix F uses Fourier–Motzkin elimination on negated CNF clauses in linear integer arithmetic. For a single clause, negations of derived consequences suggest sufficient assumptions; contradiction can prove validity. For multiple conjuncts, the implementation returns the union of clause-local suggestions rather than all conjunction combinations. F.2 explicitly withdraws the guarantee that any suggestion implies the entire input formula. Subsequent proof checks and recursive refinement matter; treating every abduction result as a complete proof would overstate the method. Table 8 specifies teacher 20×8000 generation episodes, 800 validation episodes/iteration, max 60 steps, 64 MCTS simulations; solver 20×20000 episodes, 5000 validation/iteration, max 12 steps, 32 simulations. Both use 600 asynchronous/parallel episodes, 128-dimensional embeddings, four attention heads, 6 state/3 action/1 combiner blocks, state/action encoding limits 80/12, 16 identifier IDs and 256-node MCTS cap. Teacher/solver dropout is 0.05/0.1, batch size 400/300, learning-rate maximum 5e-4/3e-4, maximum epochs 6/1. The last five teacher iterations and 10000 extra noiseless generated problems supply solver problems. The 20000 solver episodes per iteration are distinct from the inherited 50000-problem training pool. Appendix H uses separate Dynamic Graph Transformers for state probe and each action, a combiner for softmax action scores, and a value head for outcome/event predictions. AST Polish-order tokens retain syntactic edges, data/control-dependence edges and numerical comparison edges. Identifier IDs are randomized per choice point; overflow names share a flagged ID. H.2 describes binary constant encoding generally, but Table 8 sets const-emb-size=0 for both reported agents, disabling that binary value encoding. Numerical comparison edges remain enabled. Search exceeding state/action/step limits fails or discards actions; no unrestricted completeness claim follows. Figures 8–9 expose policy probabilities, outcome/event estimates and MCTS statistics. The solver example gives prior 0.81 for abduct x>0 and predicted value 0.46 versus described true shaped reward 0.4; teacher screenshot predicts success 0.97 and soft-constraint violation risk 0.31. These are individual UI examples, not calibration experiments. Figure 7 as printed has inconsistent/unbound names in prove_preserved (suggs/assum/action) and proved_invs versus invs_proved; it is explanatory pseudocode, not a standalone executable listing. No author code was inspected to resolve the differences.
    - limits: The proof domain is Code2Inv:132 C programs with one loop, linear integer arithmetic and selected conditionals/assumptions/nondeterministic tests. The authors leave other domains for future work; general theorem-proving claims are a design vision, not demonstrated coverage. The learned result evaluates greedy highest-policy actions with no search/backtracking. It measures ability to generate an invariant of minimal size; MCTS can solve all benchmark problems, so no-search success is the reported challenge. An invariant is accepted through the benchmark/verifier pipeline; the paper does not establish semantic robustness beyond those checks. Teacher generation uses manually defined constraints and validity-preserving transformations. Constraints can be incompatible; rewards are shaped so success beats failure. Generated problem distribution and benchmark generalization depend on strategy design, not an unrestricted theorem distribution. Training protocol uses two random seeds for error intervals; one complete run takes about16h on specified hardware. No comparison across many independent training runs, hardware settings or random datasets is reported. The approach formalizes expert strategy fragments and learns choices; it does not produce Lean/Coq/Rust/Verus proof terms or TLA+ specifications in this paper. Constraint combinations and correlations are partly hardcoded; formula/program templates and parameter-abduction shape the generated distribution. Appendix F uses Fourier–Motzkin elimination on negated CNF clauses in linear integer arithmetic. For a single clause, negations of derived consequences suggest sufficient assumptions; contradiction can prove validity. For multiple conjuncts, the implementation returns the union of clause-local suggestions rather than all conjunction combinations. F.2 explicitly withdraws the guarantee that any suggestion implies the entire input formula. Subsequent proof checks and recursive refinement matter; treating every abduction result as a complete proof would overstate the method. Appendix H uses separate Dynamic Graph Transformers for state probe and each action, a combiner for softmax action scores, and a value head for outcome/event predictions. AST Polish-order tokens retain syntactic edges, data/control-dependence edges and numerical comparison edges. Identifier IDs are randomized per choice point; overflow names share a flagged ID. - Zeebe Raft property tests (2022)
    - title: Bulletproofing Zeebe concurrency bugs
    - what: An engineer at Camunda describes how the team tests the Raft (replication and leader-election protocol) module of the Zeebe workflow engine. Random sequences of events (messages, drops, restarts, clock ticks) are run under a deterministic scheduler, and after each step the code checks two safety properties; for liveness it runs extra failure-free steps and checks that a leader exists and all records are replicated.

- IC3PO symmetry verifier (2021)
    - title: On Symmetry and Quantification: A New Approach to Verify Distributed Protocols
    - what: Introduces IC3PO, symmetric incremental induction extending finite-domain IC3/PDR. It infers the quantifier prefix of inductive invariants from protocol symmetries, then finds a finite cutoff whose proof generalizes to all instance sizes. Logical orbits map symmetry classes to quantified clauses; symmetry-aware clause boosting avoids searching fixed quantifier templates. A cutoff procedure checks that orbit representatives cover all relevant instances. IC3PO handles universal/existential alternations and produces compact quantified invariants automatically.
    - number: Evaluation set 29 protocols; IC3PO solved all 29, versus I4 13, UPDR 14, fol-ic3 23
    - number: 10 protocols solved by all: IC3PO total 232s/85 invariant assertions/12,160 SMT queries
    - number: IC3PO 1-hour timeout, 32GB memory, Intel Xeon X5670; Z3/Yices/CVC4 backends
    - number: ToyConsensus(3,3,3): IC3PO 192 SMT calls and 13 CTIs versus I4 7,814 calls and 443 CTIs
    - limits: Focuses on parameterized protocols whose sorts are sets of indistinguishable constants; ordered sorts and integer arithmetic are future work. Safety only, not liveness. Finite cutoff correctness depends on symmetry assumptions; evaluation uses bounded protocol models and SMT solver timeouts.

- Metastable failures (2021)
    - title: Metastable failures in distributed systems
    - what: Names a failure pattern in which a trigger (for example a short network outage) pushes a system into a bad state that a feedback loop (for example client retries) keeps going after the trigger is gone. Gives four production examples and a vocabulary: stable, vulnerable, metastable, trigger, sustaining effect, hidden capacity.
    - number: Retry example: 280 queries per second (QPS) plus a 10 s outage leads to 560 QPS of retries; recovery needs load under 150 QPS or retries under 20 QPS
    - number: Look-aside cache example: advertised capacity 3,000 QPS, hidden capacity 300 QPS; losing a 90% hit-rate cache gives 10x query amplification
    - number: Link imbalance example went unexplained for more than two years; fix was a single line in the connection-pool policy
    - number: A geo-distributed system with added retries and failover had worst-case work amplification over 100x
    - limits: A vision paper from operating experience: no formal definition of the property, no tool, no measured evaluation; the authors list how to find unknown metastable failures as open.

- Metha: Network Verifiers Need To Be Correct Too! (2021)
    - what: Metha generates network configurations, runs them on network analyzers and real-router images, and compares converged routing tables to expose model discrepancies. Semantic constraints activate meaningful protocol computations; minimum/middle/maximum parameter reduction and PICT pairwise tests cover selected feature interactions. Iterative delta debugging isolates all inclusion-minimal failure-inducing feature sets under explicit monotonicity/outcome assumptions. Records grammar versus semantic constraints, finite topology/parameter/test domain, exact coverage definition, iterative delta-debugging proof/complexity/masking limits, practical convergence oracle, eight detailed model-mismatch examples, complete bug/ablation/localization counts and commits, baseline parser exception and simulation-only NV boundary.
    - limits: §§1,3,7: Differential oracle is actual Cisco/Juniper router software, including vendor quirks and bugs. A discrepancy establishes disagreement with the selected implementation/version, not protocol-standard truth or independent proof that the analyzer is at fault. Developers confirmed 59/62 reports. Oracle fidelity, routing-table extraction, grammar/semantic constraints and comparison code remain trusted. §7: Convergence means ten unchanged routing-table snapshots sampled 10s apart (~100s), a heuristic rather than proof that the protocol has stabilized forever. Transients or oscillations slower than sampling, hidden state, timing/hardware differences and output normalization may affect comparisons. Paper gives no exact Cisco/Juniper image versions or host CPU/RAM. Batfish/NV source commits are cited. §§4–5: Search uses fixed physical/logical topology and provided features, finite recursive grammar, predefined strings, numeric minimum/maximum/one interior value. Pairwise coverage is over selected router×feature/value combinations, not all configurations, numeric values, protocol states, failures, traffic or code paths. Supporting prefix/community lists are not independently fuzzed. §5.1: Full t-wise coverage finds every bug only if each is exposed by at most t interactions of selected value dimensions and test behavior meets the premise. Empirical one/two-feature causes do not prove larger or unseen-boundary bugs absent. PICT yields a covering suite; no proof of globally minimum test count provided. §6/Appendix A: Theorem 1 concerns all inclusion-minimal failing subsets, not globally smallest cardinality or every underlying code bug. Iterative extension lifts the unique-minimal-fault assumption, retaining monotone failure and definite consistent outcomes; proof explicitly invokes monotonicity. No proof every real configuration discrepancy satisfies these assumptions. §6.2: Bugs whose triggers strictly contain an already failing subset cannot be identified separately until the smaller bug is fixed. Different minimal triggers can share a source-code cause; the black-box method cannot deduplicate by code or pinpoint erroneous code. Counts represent developer-assessed/configuration-trigger evidence, not automatically proved distinct code defects. §6.2: Uncached worst case is O(|Q|!); caching unique queued feature sets gives stated O(2^|Q|) bound. Router/tool calls and minimization also cost time. Algorithm 1 prints head(queue) without explicit dequeue; narrative describes consuming the queue until empty. This listing omission is not evidence of implementation nontermination. §§7–8: Routing-table comparison catches crashes and silent control-plane model bugs, but does not directly check packet forwarding, data-plane execution, every proof procedure, solver soundness or live production networks. Only NV simulation was tested; authors infer likely verification impact from its shared model. Table 3 marks confirmation of C-BGP’s three reports as unknown (?). Batfish’s 29 and NV’s 30 reports were confirmed. §8.1: Each of four approaches runs 1794 tests with the same four-router Cisco star features. Semantic baseline finds 16 bugs versus random’s 3; boundary values add one, pairwise adds three. Original Metha misses a random parser bug because OSPF area numbers are fixed; adding that dimension finds it. Discussion’s claim that all random bugs are found with fewer tests needs this augmented input space. §8.1/Figure 5: Coverage measures pairs of feature values, with continuous random numeric values bucketed into minimum/maximum/middle. It is not code coverage or proof soundness. Semantic and random approaches have similar coverage but different bug counts, illustrating semantic activation. No repeated random seeds, confidence intervals or sample variance reported. §8.2: Mean 14.1 extra tests per discrepancy, range 7–58, not per bug; 58 localizes five bugs. Complete testing takes several days; single test averages two minutes, PICT generation six minutes. Full cost includes fault localization; no precisely timed total or resource scaling study. §§9–11: Testing supplements unit/system testing. No universal absence-of-bugs proof or Verus/verified Rust/TLA+/LLM-written proof result. Testing configuration synthesizers is future work. Version and confirmation claims refer to source publication time.

- SWISS (2021)
    - title: Finding Invariants of Distributed Systems: It’s a Small (Enough) World After All
    - what: SWISS searches finite spaces of quantified invariants for RML distributed protocol models. Breadth accumulates small relatively inductive clauses; Finisher searches for a final clause that, with safety, is inductive. Failed SMT verification conditions yield reusable finite-model filters, with symmetry and redundancy pruning, model minimization and parallel search. It proves selected models including Paxos/Flexible Paxos and guided Multi-Paxos, and measures successes, partial progress, costs and failed optimizations against I4 and first-order separators. Reconstructs exact Finisher/Breadth obligations, conditional coverage guarantee and its implementation timeout exception, all three counterexample filters and fast bitset/trie evaluations. Saves every benchmark/table number and failed-protocol result, manual search tuning, full-space versus first-hit timing and all appendix ablations. Distinguishes model/spec correctness from code, safety-relative partial clauses from independent proofs, and the small-world hypothesis from general completeness.
    - number: solves 19 of 27 benchmarks with a six-hour timeout, median of five runs
    - number: Paxos and Flexible Paxos take about 4.4 and 5.1 hours
    - number: Multi-Paxos succeeds only with supplied templates
    - number: Paxos: around 4 hours on an 8-core machine; with guidance 20 minutes
    - limits: §§2–3: Inputs are a formal RML protocol transition system, desired safety condition and candidate-space configuration. No executable implementation/refinement theorem is supplied. Encoding an intended protocol so checks remain in extended EPR may itself be challenging and is explicitly outside SWISS scope. Safety/transition semantics, solver/encoder/filter implementation and execution environment are trusted; output invariants can be independently validated but this reading did not replay them. §§2.1–2.4: Proofs address all reachable states of the supplied transition model, not just finite test runs; no liveness proof. Atomic transition/failure/network assumptions come from each input protocol. SDL running example uniquely disallows packet duplication; do not generalize that restriction to every benchmark. EPR satisfiability uses ∃*∀* with no general functions; stratified functions and quantifier alternation are admitted in the extended fragment while preserving decidability. §§3.2–3.4: Finisher checks Init⇒S, Init⇒P, TR∧known∧S∧P⇒S′ and TR∧known∧S∧P⇒P′. Breadth checks initialization and inductiveness of new clauses relative to input clauses and S. Therefore intermediate clauses can be inductive only under safety, not independently proved reachable-state properties. Reader qualification: reuse partial output with its assumptions/joint invariant obligation intact; a partial clause count is not a partial correctness theorem about arbitrary code. §3.4 Claim 1: Ideal Solve succeeds only if a finite chain exists with all earlier clauses in B, final clause in F, each earlier prefix inductive relative to S, and final conjunction including S inductive; sound pruning must preserve valid candidates and VC checks must finish. This is relative completeness for the stated chain/space, not decidability of arbitrary invariant discovery. §§4.3,5.1 reader qualification: Actual implementation retries timed-out Z3 queries with CVC4 and may skip an invariant if timeout persists. Thus practical runs with skipped candidates do not retain the ideal exhaustive coverage guarantee. Skipping rejects potentially useful proofs; it does not justify accepting an unproved candidate. Unknown/timeout and finite search failure do not establish protocol unsafety. §4.1: User supplies formula term/depth limits k,d, quantified-variable limits m,q and quantifier type nesting order to retain extended-EPR VCs. Templates can be auto-enumerated within bounds or explicitly supplied. Auto mode is not no-user-configuration; search grows exponentially. Symmetry pruning imposes syntactic term order/first-variable order, not full logical-equivalence solving. §4.2: True(M) requires P on a model; False(M) requires not-P; Transition(M,M′) requires P(M)⇒P(M′). Filters are derived from failed VC obligations and are sound for candidates satisfying the same obligation context. False models need not be reachable; they are states every acceptable strengthening for that VC must reject, rather than arbitrary actual executions proving a bug. §§4.2–4.5: Precompute base-term evaluations on every variable instantiation in finite models and evaluate candidates with bitwise operations. FastImplies is a sound but incomplete syntactic implication check using subsequences/modulo variable renaming in a trie, with limited universal/existential substitution. allInv retains redundant clauses for filtering while indInv avoids redundant output. Model minimization has a 45-second budget, so a retained model need only be partially minimized. §4.5.2: Partition/randomize B/F and run independent per-thread searches, combining only phase results. Threads do not share counterexample filters or fresh invariants during a phase. This gives good filtering parallelism but replicates SMT model construction and does not establish universal linear speedup. §5.1: Defaults B(d,k,q,m)=(1,3,1,5), F=(2,6,1,6) were adjusted per benchmark after results: increase k/q on exhausted failed searches, shrink B on slow breadth. learning-switch-ternary uses q=0; async lock and sharded-kv enlarge k; no-lost-keys needs q=2. Results include manual tuning and guided Multi-Paxos, not a fixed blind default-only experiment. §§5.1–5.2 Table 1: 27 distinct benchmarks plus duplicate guided Multi-Paxos row. SWISS times are medians of five runs with six-hour budget, on eight-core i9-9900K 3.60GHz/125GB/Ubuntu19.10, eight threads unless noted. Z3 4.8.8 then CVC4 1.8-prerelease --finite-model-find after 45s. Baseline runtime entries are published comparisons without uniform per-baseline dispersion in this paper. §5.2.1: Paxos and Flexible Paxos complete automatically within the configured candidate templates; Multi-Paxos requires correct user templates and unguided run times out in Breadth. Fast/Stoppable/Vertical Paxos remain unsolved, despite introductory phrasing that can sound broader. I4/FOL/SWISS each win on some protocols; existential support alone does not dominate larger mutually inductive universal invariants. §5.2.2: Partial progress counts handwritten invariant clauses logically implied by generated clauses, not percent of execution space, correctness or remaining proof effort. Missing one essential clause can prevent the whole safety proof. Reported 79% Vertical Paxos is 14/17, about 82.4%; preserve printed discrepancy rather than treating prose percentage as exact. §§5.3.3–5.3.5: Table 2 timings enumerate entire template spaces even after finding a proof and start with supplied Breadth invariants. The claimed 20-minute/13.4× guided result concerns proof search stopping, not the listed 2036-second full-template enumeration. Table 3 is a single-thread single-template pruning experiment, not end-to-end automatic-template run. §5.3.4: mn/mn−1 are smallest known chain maximum term sizes, drawn from human or synthesized invariants; minimality is not proved. 25/27 have mn≤8 and 22 are smaller; mn≤6/mn−1≤3 is an empirical rule of thumb. Human comprehensibility implies finite intuition is motivation for a hypothesis, not a theorem guaranteeing succinct invariants. Appendices A–B: Formula-synthesis, BMC and eager invariant accumulation were unfavorable in tested cases; this is workload-specific evidence. Figure 5 uses preselected templates and a logarithmic y-axis. Parallelism experiments deliberately finish the whole F space to remove first-hit variance and analyze longest-running threads, so speedups do not directly imply normal first-proof latency. §§5–7: No production-code performance, LLM-agent experiment, verified Rust/Verus implementation or TLA+/TLC integration is evaluated. An unexpectedly easy Fast Paxos proof uncovered a disabled-action typo in the input spec, demonstrating the need to validate intended behavior despite valid invariants. External artifacts/proofs and source-code current state were not executed or audited.

- CoqGym (2019)
    - title: Learning to Prove Theorems via Interacting with Proof Assistants
    - what: The complete five-page official PMLR supplement is newly read: A.1 project compilation, A.2 proof-tree reconstruction, A.3 synthetic proof extraction and the full Figure A, then every production of grammar B across three pages. The original 11-page CoqGym/ASTactic main-paper record and evaluation are explicitly inherited. The supplement explains collection bias, the discarded multi-goal traces and why synthetic proofs are conditional proofs in augmented contexts; it supplies no additional success-rate experiment or system soundness theorem. Serializes kernel terms/runtime proof states through modified Coq and SerAPI while leaving the checking core untouched; represents proof trees and complete environments. Project-disjoint train/validation/test split has 43,844/13,875/13,137 proofs. Generates 1–4-step synthetic intermediate-goal proofs, but ASTactic training does not use them. Encoder embeds goal and premises with child-sum TreeLSTM; GRU decoder grows Ltac AST depth-first, attends over premises and predicts grammar productions. Argument generation selects available premise identifiers, classifies integers 1–4, or randomly selects quantified goal variables. Teacher forcing and cross-entropy train individual tactics; beam-search tactics feed DFS with duplicate-state backtracking. Combines learned decomposition with built-in automation or hammer at each goal.
    - number: about 71K proofs from 123 projects
    - limits: Compilation success and the manually installed dependency subset select the collected projects.123projects/3061files are the collected corpus, not ecosystem coverage. Tree reconstruction uses one disappearing goal per step. Tactics that affect multiple goals/goal selectors are discarded, with reported loss<1%; the denominator is described as data without a detailed sampling/count audit. Synthetic theorem contexts may include generalized descendant obligations H3/H4. This shortens a conditional proof; it does not establish an unconditional shorter proof of the original theorem or independent novel mathematical evidence. The grammar starts at tactic_expr, permits listed atomic tactics, identifiers and small integers1–4, and lists fixed hint databases. It has no arbitrary Ltac sequencing, custom tactics or arbitrary Coq-term synthesis; restriction is deliberate. Inherited main-paper limits still apply: at most10environmental premises plus local context; teacher-forced supervised tactic training; synthetic proofs not used by the reported ASTactic; search/tactic/time budgets; no modern LLM, distributed protocol, Rust/Verus or TLA+ experiment. Only printed grammar/proof examples were read. No repository, Coq implementation, dataset, generated proof corpus or solver/prover execution was inspected or run.
    - how much was read: most of it read

- Formal Verification of Usage Control Models: A Case Study of UseCON Using TLA+ (2018)
    - what: # Formal Verification of Usage Control Models: A Case Study of UseCON Using TLA+ (authored by agents unless marked 🧑)  Antonios Gouglidis, Christos Grompanopoulos and Anastasia Mavridou, MeTRiD 2018, EPTCS 272, pp.52–64, DOI 10.4204/EPTCS.272.5. The paper specifies concurrent usage-control lifecycles and reports finite TLC checks for pre-authorisation and ongoing-authorisation models. The authors claim correctness for “≤ 12 uses”; Table 2 gives measurements at 2, 8 and 12. These are bounded model results, not a proof for arbitrary populations or an implementation. Printed temporal formulas also have substantial specification issues that cannot be reconciled with the reported checks without the executable models (Q0,Q7–Q10).  ## Reading and evidence  Read all 13 pages, sections 1–5 and all eleven references. Inspected four figures, two tables and all displayed definitions/actions/formulas. Viewed eight individual renders, pages 5–12, including enlarged printed safety/liveness formulas and Table 2. The inventory, official arXiv and official EPTCS PDFs are byte-identical. Formula symbols lost in extracted text were checked against the renders.  No executable TLA+ module, TLC configuration or run log was acquired. Section 2 says “see TLA+ specification in [1]”, but reference [1] is Microsoft's TLA+ Tools page, not an identified UseCON model archive (Q1,Q11). The saved PDF's active links point to tools, cited publications and license information. This is a bounded artifact-location finding, not a claim that no source can exist elsewhere. No author code/model executed or imported, no TLC/compiler/checker/solver run and no serialized scientific results loaded.  ## Model and concrete cases  UseCON adds a use entity to subjects, objects and actions. A use records a request's subject/action/object identifiers, lifecycle status and mutable use attributes. U, the set of use records, is the only declared state variable. Subject/object/action records and their identifiers are treated as constants; administrative changes to their attributes are outside the modeled operation. Initially U is empty. Policy-related history can be read through existing uses (Q1,Q2).  Direct policies use attributes of the requesting subject, object and action. Indirect policies select a related entity using CHOOSE; the example checks whether a child's father has premium membership. Complex indirect policies aggregate selected entities; the example sums a customer's account balances. Correct interpretation of CHOOSE requires an appropriate selected entity; existence/uniqueness conditions are not developed as part of these examples. The paper illustrates expressiveness but leaves verification of complex and interacting ongoing policies as future work (Q3,Q10).  Induced use attributes come from participating entities, such as a service price. Observed attributes require an external observation, such as the system time when access is activated. Update procedures change status and other use attributes together. SystemTime is illustrative rather than a concrete bounded clock model. Administrators must supply policy-specific procedures and finite domains for any TLC instance (Q2–Q4; reader finite-model qualification).  The pre-authorisation lifecycle is initial→requested→activated→completed or requested→denied. Request and Complete are subject actions; preEvaluate is a system action. Next is their disjunction. Request nondeterministically chooses a subject/action/object tuple absent from existing records. Because terminal records remain in U and the uniqueness test uses only that tuple, the printed transition rules do not permit requesting the same tuple again in that model lifetime. This restricts the workload compared with repeated real-world accesses (Q5; reader inference).  For ongoing authorisation, Request is followed by Activate without a prior policy test. onEvaluate keeps the use active and updates attributes if the policy holds, or changes it to stopped otherwise; Complete changes active to completed. Next includes all four actions. The evaluation interval is explicitly left to implementation. Thus the model does not establish a maximum revocation delay or that a policy is continuously true between evaluations (Q6).  All update actions are assumed atomic. The authors justify this with a central policy decision point and the absence of updates to subject/object attributes. Concurrent operations are represented by interleaving these whole actions; message loss, replication, distributed transactions, crashes and partial updates are not modeled here. There is no implementation-refinement proof showing that a deployed access-control service realizes these atomic steps (Q4–Q6).  ## Intended properties and printed formulas  TypeCorrectness is U⊆Uses, where Uses is a finite-domain record type. Table 1 lists forbidden status changes and intended progress. Pre-authorisation forbids leaving completed or denied and returning from activated to requested/denied; it expects requested uses eventually to activate or be denied, and activated uses eventually to complete. Ongoing authorisation similarly forbids leaving completed/stopped or returning from activated to requested; it expects requested uses to activate and eventually complete or stop (Q7).  The printed safety formula is □(∃u∈U: u.st="completed" ⇒ □(u.st≠"requested")), where □ means always. This is existential, although the prose says the condition applies to all uses. With the natural quantifier scope shown by the parentheses, the existential is false when U is empty; Init explicitly sets U={} so the formula fails in the initial state. Further, u denotes a bound record value. The inner u.st does not look up that use's changing record in future U states. A completed bound record retains its completed field even if U later contains a replacement record with the same identifiers and a different status. These are semantic issues in the printed formula, not an executed TLC counterexample (Q4,Q7; reader interpretation).  The liveness formula is ∀u∈U: u.st="activated" ↝ u.st="completed", where ↝ means that whenever its left condition holds, the right condition eventually holds. U is quantified at the point where this formula is evaluated. As a top-level property evaluated initially, it is vacuously true because initial U is empty. If evaluated at a later state with an active record, that bound record value does not evolve into the completed record. Adding an outer □ alone would not repair this identity/update issue; the property needs stable identifiers and a state-dependent lookup (Q2,Q4,Q8; reader interpretation).  The paper supplies Init and Next but no complete temporal specification or fairness assumptions. A disjunction of enabled actions alone does not require an enabled completion or policy evaluation eventually to be selected. In the ongoing model, an active use can remain active through onEvaluate repeatedly while completion is postponed. If stuttering is allowed by the full specification, it gives another way to postpone progress. Scheduling/fairness and policy assumptions must therefore be explicit for an eventual-completion theorem. No such theorem can be recovered from the displayed transitions alone (Q5–Q8; reader separating behavior).  There are also printed notation inconsistencies: action guards and createUse use state while other definitions and updates use st; record definitions and extra braces in the ongoing actions are not a complete executable module. These discrepancies could differ from the authors' actual checked models. They prevent treating the printed listings as independently validated source. The reported no-error checks and the initial-state safety failure cannot both refer literally to the same displayed property under standard quantifier interpretation; the missing modules/configurations leave the correspondence unresolved (Q2,Q5–Q9).  ## Measurements and their scope  The authors use TLA+ Tool 1.5.6 and TLC 2.12 on a mid-2014 MacBook Pro, OS X El Capitan, 2.5GHz Intel Core i7 and 16GB 1600MHz DDR3. Table 2 contains six configurations. Each tuple below gives uses; reported diameter; generated states; distinct states; deadlock-check seconds; safety/liveness seconds (Q9).  - Pre: 2;6;21;12;1;3 - Pre: 8;23;277,969;16,832;2;5 - Pre: 12;31;45,533,665;560,128;53;138 - Ongoing: 2;7;33;16;1;3 - Ongoing: 8;25;367,873;23,808;2;7 - Ongoing: 12;37;79,112,449;1,224,704;118;241  All 36 numeric cells were transcribed from the rendered table. Diameter is retained as the paper's reported label; no TLC statistics were independently regenerated. State-count growth and interrupted larger runs demonstrate an exploration limit for this setup. Table 2 does not document every integer population below 12, all policy choices or all attribute domains. The exact subject/object/action split, policy instances, bounds and fairness configuration are not supplied in enough detail to reproduce these counts (Q9).  The paper calls the first experiment deadlock-freedom checking, then describes TLC stopping at a deadlock after every pre-authorisation use is denied or completed. Its conclusion says “The trace of the deadlock errors verifies that the defined specifications of the system operate correctly.” A permitted terminal state and an unexpected stuck state need separate acceptance conditions. These traces can support an intended-termination inspection, but their existence does not itself establish deadlock freedom, exhaustive termination of every behavior or the temporal formulas above (Q9,Q10).  The authors report no safety/liveness errors for the bounded configurations. No model files, logs, certificates or reproduced checks establish which executable formulas produced that result. The appropriate record is therefore an author-reported bounded check, accompanied by the printed-formula issues. Larger populations were stopped for time; no cutoff theorem extends the small checks to arbitrary numbers of uses (Q9,Q10).  ## Contribution to the topic  This is a concrete TLA+/TLC case study of security-related lifecycle modeling, finite-state exploration cost and intended safety/progress distinctions. Its central-state atomic model is relevant to concurrent systems but does not verify a distributed protocol or a Rust/Verus implementation. No LLM specification/proof agent is evaluated. Complex interacting policy verification, composition with secBIP and arbitrary-population guarantees are future work (Q10).  For evaluating generated specifications, the paper provides useful failure modes: temporal quantification over an initially empty set, confusing a fixed record with a changing entity, omitted fairness, treating a terminal deadlock as a general correctness argument, and incomplete correspondence between printed formulas and reported checker success. These are reader lessons from this paper, not author-demonstrated LLM failures.
    - limits: boundedatomiccentralmodel;constantentityrecords;policydomains/fairnessmissing;printedformulasnotverifiedexecutablemodels
    - how much was read: most of it read

- Ivy liveness-to-safety (2018)
    - title: Reducing liveness to safety in first-order logic
    - what: After a finite prefix of an execution, picks a finite projection of the state (the freeze point). Proves that no two later states can agree on that projection while all fairness obligations are met. This turns a progress proof into a safety proof about a finite bad pattern. Cases: ticket, alternating bit, TLB shootdown, Paxos, Multi-Paxos, Stoppable Paxos.
    - number: finding the invariants 'requires a sophisticated user'; conjecture counts for liveness are '2 - 4 times higher' than for safety (§7.2, pp.26:27-28)
    - number: six cases: ticket protocol, alternating bit, TLB shootdown, Paxos, Multi-Paxos, Stoppable Paxos
    - number: checking takes 3.7 to 219 seconds excluding invariant discovery
    - number: liveness needs two to four times as many invariant conjectures as safety (§7.2)
    - limits: The user still supplies the inductive invariants (facts preserved by every step). Humans applied the reduction by hand. Paxos assumes that the actions of a chosen leader or quorum are fair, that messages are eventually delivered, and that competing proposals eventually stop. Multi-Paxos and Stoppable Paxos cover one incarnation with fixed membership. The comparison counts invariants, not person-time.

It is designed for fair comparison and collaborative extension rather than proposing a new checker.

Translation to Uppaal may lose parameter/stopwatch information, and the library is a benchmark resource rather than a completeness guarantee.
