- Multi-Paxos TLAPS (2016)
    - title: Formal Verification of Multi-Paxos for Distributed Consensus
    - what: # Formal Verification of Multi-Paxos for Distributed Consensus (authored by agents unless marked 🧑)  Saksham Chand, Yanhong A. Liu and Scott D. Stoller. The original paper appeared at FM 2016, pp 119–136, DOI 10.1007/978-3-319-48989-6_8. This record reads the 37-page corrected and extended arXiv:1606.01387v4, dated 11 November 2019, classified cs.DC on its first page. The inventory's 2016 year identifies the original work; it does not date the corrected version read here (Q1,Q45; arxiv.html).  The contribution is a small TLA+ specification of Multi-Paxos and its preemption extension, an author-reported TLAPS proof of per-slot agreement, and concrete strategies for managing the much larger proof. Two qualifications matter. The authors explicitly retract the earlier completeness claim: “The claim of a complete proof in [7] was incorrect” (§1 p2, Q3), explaining a PTL backend bug and their replacement type-invariant proof. Separately, this read finds inconsistent maximum axioms in the printed appendix and pinned proof files as written (Appendix C p29, Q49,Q59,Q63). Thus the record preserves the corrected checking report but does not treat checker acceptance under those retained assumptions as independent certification of the assumption-free theorem. This is a mathematical assumption problem, not an observed execution failure or a counterexample to Multi-Paxos agreement.  ## Reading and evidence scope  Read all 37 PDF pages in extracted text and rendered images: sections 1–7, all six figures, both numeric tables, all 26 numbered equations, acknowledgments, all 63 reference entries, and Appendices A–C containing the specification, invariants and hierarchical proof. The figures are algorithm/proof listings and comparisons, not statistical plots. The two PDFs, one from the local inventory and one freshly fetched from arXiv, are byte-identical. The local PDF is the corrected v4, not the 2016 proceedings text.  Fetched the author-linked DistAlgo/proofs repository's metadata, commit and recursive tree. The pin is fb4a727ed8fab39197d9a20ed066d21abbb83573, last commit 4 November 2019. Read its full README and selected plaintext specification, assumption, type-invariant and final-theorem regions of the improved Multi-Paxos/preemption modules, plus the historical FM16 TypeOK regions. The README distinguishes these versions and says improved proofs were checked with TLAPS 1.5.6 (Q56). Downloaded Basic Paxos baseline and historical modules provide retained source context; this is not a whole-repository or whole-module audit. The explicit excerpt/range inventory is in source-scope.json. Also read the complete official issue 40 body describing the coalescing bug (Q66); metadata says opened 1 April 2017 and closed 17 May 2018. Closing an issue does not identify the fix commit or certify every installed tool version.  No author code, model, TLAPS, TLC, SMT solver or proof assistant was executed or imported. No serialized data/model/checkpoint was loaded. Reader checks use file hashes, literal source slices, arithmetic and symbolic reasoning. Raw Git files retain their bytes; .lf.txt files are identified newline-normalized derivatives for character and LF-line quote pointers. The PDF extraction renders □ as 2, primes as 0 and some negated existential/membership symbols unusually; rendered pages were used to interpret the formulas. quotes.json retains extraction bytes, including such artifacts.  ## The model and the exact theorem  The system has proposer, acceptor and learner roles, which may be colocated. Constants describe proposers P, acceptors A, quorum family Q and possible values V. Ballots B and slots S are natural numbers. Membership and the quorum family are fixed: dynamic reconfiguration is excluded. The sole stated quorum condition is Q ⊆ SUBSET A and pairwise nonempty intersection. Majority, tree or matrix quorums can instantiate it once that condition is established; the paper does not construct or evaluate those alternatives (Q6,Q13,Q19).  The state contains the set msgs of every message ever sent, proposer ballots pBal, each acceptor's highest seen ballot aBal, and aVoted, a set of ballot-slot-value records retaining the highest voted ballot for each slot. Init uses no messages/votes, proposer ballot 0 and acceptor ballot −1. Next chooses one proposer or acceptor phase. Spec = Init ∧ □[Next]_vars permits an action or stuttering at each step (Q13–Q19).  The messages-as-history abstraction lets processes select any previously sent message, repeatedly and in any order. An unhandled message models arbitrary delay or loss; repeated handling models duplication. Crashes are modeled by not scheduling the crashed process, and recovery resumes its retained state. The authors require “all state (vars) is stored in stable storage” (§3.3 p11, Q20). The model has no Byzantine message fabrication, volatile-state reset, implementation of durable storage, or explicit network delivery queue. This is a safety abstraction of benign failures.  There are no fairness or eventual-delivery clauses in the displayed Spec. Repeating Init forever is a valid behavior through UNCHANGED vars and never chooses a value. This reader witness demonstrates the absence of decision progress, without challenging agreement. A quorum of functioning processes and delivery assumptions would matter for liveness; no such liveness theorem is established here (Q9,Q15,Q19; math-witness.json).  The target is Spec ⇒ □Safe, where Safe means any two chosen values for one slot are equal. The paper identifies C1 proposed-value validity, C2 agreement and C3 learner correctness, then says C1/C3 are excluded from the formal proof; §3.3 and §4.8 discuss them informally (Q5,Q22,Q33). There are no explicit learner variables/actions. Validity means a chosen slot/value occurred in a protocol 2a proposal, not that a value came from an external client or satisfied an application invariant. Equations 24/26 use sent although the state variable is msgs; the record treats this as the printed informal derivation, not a checked new theorem.  The precise Chosen definition used in the proof is ∃b ∈ B: ∃Q ∈ Q: ∀a ∈ Q: VotedForIn(a,b,s,v). VotedForIn witnesses a sent 2b message with that ballot and slot/value. The introductory equations 3/4 place ∃b inside ∀a, allowing different ballots for different acceptors. Those predicates differ; use equations 10–12 and Appendix B for the actual theorem (Q6,Q23,Q48). A separating predicate valuation in math-witness.json makes the difference explicit, without claiming a reachable protocol counterexample.  ## Minimal algorithm changes  Basic Paxos carries one latest accepted ballot/value pair. Multi-Paxos carries a set of ballot-slot-value records in each promise. Phase1a sends a ballot. Phase1b responds only to a strictly higher ballot, returning its voted-record set and recording the promise. Phase2a waits for same-ballot promise replies covering a quorum and sends at most one 2a message per ballot. It selects the maximum accepted ballot separately per slot, retaining that slot's value, and adds new slot/value pairs. Phase2b accepts a ballot at least its promise, sends a 2b reply, and replaces voted records only for the slots in that proposal (Figures 2–5, Q14–Q18).  MaxBSV filters the voted records to maximal ballots per slot; MaxSV drops ballots; UnusedS finds absent slots; NewSV chooses a set of unused-slot/value pairs with no duplicate slot carrying different values; PropSV combines old and new pairs. Ballots are shared across slots, and old unfinished slots can be reused. The authors explain that this is more involved than separately running independent Basic Paxos instances (Q15,Q17).  NewSV has no explicit nonemptiness, finiteness or client-input requirement. The empty set satisfies its uniqueness predicate. CHOOSE denotes a fixed mathematical choice for the same argument, not a newly sampled nondeterministic result each time. The formulas are useful abstractions for agreement, but do not by themselves ensure an executable finite batch or useful new work. Finite msgs does not imply finite message payloads (Q17,Q47; math-witness.json).  Preemption adds an addressed reply containing an acceptor's highest ballot when it rejects a proposer ballot. A proposer receiving a strictly higher preempt ballot updates pBal above it. Phase1a then sends pBal, and the two acceptor actions have extra rejection branches (Figure 6/Appendix A, Q34,Q35,Q47). In Phase1b equality also enters the else branch, although the prose often says lower ballot. The proposer's strict-higher guard prevents equality from raising pBal.  Two source differences constrain exact correspondence. The printed NewBal requires only b>b2; the pinned NewBallot also excludes ballots already used by a 1a message, matching §5.1's fresh-ballot explanation. Conversely, the pinned non-preemption Phase1a omits the no-prior-1a guard displayed in Figure 2. Both retain the global no-prior-2a-per-ballot guard. These differences are documented, not silently normalized or labeled agreement violations (Q14,Q35,Q36,Q47,Q57,Q62).  Actual software must allocate ballots, for example ordered counter/process-identifier pairs, rather than inspect a global history oracle. Reconfiguration, message/state reduction and failure detection are omitted. A 1b reply can contain votes for every previously used slot. There is no verified Rust implementation, code refinement or operational throughput experiment in this paper (Q21).  ## Invariants, decomposition and final argument  Inv conjoins TypeOK, AccInv and MsgInv. TypeOK constrains every message/function/record field; the corrected preemption version additionally requires finite msgs. AccInv relates each acceptor's promise and current voted records to the historical 2b votes: stored records were actually voted, every historical vote is covered by a same-slot record at least as high, and no vote lies above the current same-slot maximum (Q25,Q26,Q48).  MsgInv1b records the sender's promise, actual historical votes in its returned set, and absence of votes between that slot's stored maximum and the promised ballot. MsgInv2a says every proposal is SafeAt its ballot, a message has at most one value per slot, and there is only one 2a message per ballot. MsgInv2b ties each vote to a matching 2a message and bounds its ballot by the acceptor's highest seen ballot (Q27,Q28).  SafeAt(b,s,v) says that for every lower ballot, some quorum consists of acceptors that voted v or have promised above that ballot and never voted there. WontVoteIn expresses the latter alternative. VotedInv carries SafeAt from proposals into votes; VotedOnce states same-ballot/same-slot votes have the same value, even from different acceptors. Their role is concrete: at equal chosen ballots use VotedOnce; at unequal ballots intersect the chosen quorum with the SafeAt quorum. The common acceptor's old vote excludes WontVoteIn, leaving equal values (Q24,Q50,Q55).  The proof first establishes Init ⇒ Inv and Inv ∧ [Next]_vars ⇒ Inv′, with separate action cases and an explicit unchanged-state case. PTL then derives Spec ⇒ □Inv and transfers Inv ⇒ Safe to the temporal Safety theorem (Q29,Q52–Q55). The final temporal QED is present; its presence still does not prove the assumed mathematics consistent.  The reusable method separates unaffected old elements from each action's new message or voted-record increment. Invariance lemmas preserve old VotedForIn/SafeAt facts; the new element is proved from the sending action and local invariants. Conjuncts and actions form a mechanical decomposition, while difficult bounds and witnesses require manual facts. The paper illustrates this fully for Phase1b and MsgInv and shows the longer Phase2a proof in Appendix C (Q30,Q31,Q51,Q54).  Proof syntax matters to performance. A common acceptor voting both values in the equal-ballot case is supplied explicitly; the authors report about 90 seconds reduced to a few milliseconds, calling it a 10,000× local speedup. Replacing direct record membership by an existential record with field equalities made another proof proceed after the original stalled for one or two minutes. These are local development observations, with no repeated-run distribution or uniform ablation (Q32). Tuple access is a concrete proof-engineering choice, not evidence of an algorithm runtime speedup.  ## The disclosed checker bug and the remaining assumption problem  The authors report that historical PTL coalescing could “prove” a primed state formula from its unprimed assumption outside a valid temporal context. It incorrectly accepted TypeOK′ under TypeOK ∧ [Next]_vars. The official issue's example falsely preserves xyz=0 while Next permits increasing xyz. Choosing increment 1 from xyz=0 gives xyz′=1, refuting that preservation claim symbolically (Q36,Q66). Historical FM16 modules retain PTL at primed message-type leaves; improved excerpts instead give explicit action proofs (Q60,Q64,Q65).  The repair proves type preservation for all phases. Preemption needs a fresh larger natural ballot to exist. With finite msgs, the used 1a ballots form a finite set T; max(T∪{received ballot})+1 supplies a larger unused witness. The authors added IsFiniteSet(msgs), proved preservation under message addition and supplied this witness. They report initial TypeOK proof growth 54→93 lines and 75→138 lines for the two variants; subsequent improvements reduce those to 54 and 77 (Q36,Q37,Q52).  A separate issue remains in the retained text. Appendix C calls two axioms “straightforward consequences” but writes MaxInSet as ∀S∈SUBSET N: Max(S)∈S (Q49). Substitute S=∅: it asserts Max(∅)∈∅, false for every CHOOSE result. The pinned source writes the domain as (SUBSET Nat) \\ {}, which subtracts no elements and still includes ∅ (Q59,Q63). Removing the empty set as an element would require {{}}. That change alone would still leave unbounded infinite subsets: applying MaxInSet and MaxOnNat to Nat gives a natural maximum k, but k+1 is a larger natural. A sound maximum lemma needs nonempty finite or bounded domains and proof that each use meets the restriction.  These are static mathematical witnesses to inconsistent assumptions as written, not TLAPS runs, discovered new protocol executions, or an assertion that every leaf exploited inconsistency. The retained files cannot alone support an assumption-free machine-certification claim. Agreement may be true and the intended invariant argument may be repairable, but repairing and replaying it is outside this literature read. The disclosed PTL repair does not address these maximum axioms. This distinction is particularly useful when evaluating automated proof agents: green obligations and completed syntax must be accompanied by an audit of explicit axioms and the actual target.  ## Evaluation and quantitative evidence  The evaluation compares Basic Paxos, Multi-Paxos and Multi-Paxos with Preemption, plus older incomplete and corrected/improved proof variants. It measures text size, proof-tree structure, lemma reuse, obligations and proof-checking cost, not network performance or application throughput. Table 2 specifies a four-core Intel i7-4720HQ at 2.6 GHz, 16 GB RAM, 64-bit Ubuntu 17.10 and TLAPS 1.5.6. Earlier elapsed times from 1.5.3 are marked separately. No raw timing logs, repeat counts, variance or matched cross-tool benchmark are provided (Q40–Q42).  The corrected new rows report specification lines excluding comments of 52, 55 and 74; proof lines 310, 787 and 831; obligations 239, 779 and 825; total CPU seconds 40, 175 and 180; total elapsed seconds 14, 51 and 90. CPU time and elapsed time are distinct; the total CPU values are close to the headline “about 3 minutes,” whereas the elapsed values are 51/90 seconds. Adding Multi-Paxos slots costs only three specification lines but 477 proof lines and 540 obligations; adding preemption costs 19 specification lines, 44 proof lines and 46 obligations. The per-slot data structure creates most of the proof expansion (Q40,Q41).  The new proof trees have maximum level 10 and degree 17, against 7 and 3 for Basic Paxos. Multi-Paxos/preemption use five/six invariance lemmas 32/35 times and three proofs using set increments each. §4.6's five lemmas used 27 times describes an earlier count; Table 2 provides the updated reuse counts (Q31,Q41). Reflowed appendix counts, including 528 proof lines excluding comments, are layout alternatives, not another method or independently smaller proof.  The three improvements factor message predicates, merge similar unchanged-state cases and remove leaf scaffolding TLAPS can establish automatically. Reported reductions for preemption include 75 lines from predicate factoring, 27 from merged cases, 110 from about 25 unnecessary-obligation instances and 61 from 22 TypeOK instances. The full repaired proofs shrink 1049→787 and 1117→831 lines; those are 25%/26% reductions from the repaired versions. Old incomplete rows marked ^^ are not valid proof-performance baselines (Q3,Q37–Q41).  Table 1 gives improved TypeOK elapsed times 8/25 seconds; Table 2 gives 7/24. Their old preemption TypeOK elapsed entries also differ, 21 versus 22. Preserve these printed differences rather than inventing a run-variance explanation. numeric_tables.json retains every metric, alternate count and footnote marker. The report that adding preemption took less than an hour explicitly excludes TypeOK; it cannot be used as total authoring time for the corrected proof (Q34,Q37,Q41).  ## Relationship to the requested research topics  This is directly useful for TLA+ and formal verification of distributed protocols: concrete fault abstraction, per-slot agreement, quorum reasoning, stronger invariants, hierarchical proof feedback, old/new-set decomposition and sensitivity to formula representation. Its strongest evaluation lesson is the explicit correction history, reinforced by the separate maximum-assumption findings in this read.  The work contains no Verus, verified Rust or LLM-agent experiment. Transfer to those topics is a reader proposal: preserve the exact property and fault model; measure changes in executable specification separately from proof scaffolding; give agents small action/conjunct obligations with explicit contexts; distinguish finite-instance testing from parameterized proof; and check axioms, omitted obligations, tool versions and source correspondence before reporting success. No agent success rate or Rust implementation result follows from this paper.  Related-work proof-size comparisons with IronFleet, Verdi, EventML/Nuprl, EPR and other systems are author descriptions of differently scoped work, not controlled evidence that TLAPS is uniformly faster or requires fewer lines. The separate executable-program work in reference 45 reportedly found a safety violation missed by testing/model checking, but that example is cited rather than reproduced and verified here (Q43,Q44). Those leads can be followed as separate full reads.

AuthorscorrectpriorPTLgap;readerfindsretainedmaximumaxiomsinconsistentaswritten;notindependentassumption-freesafetycertification. Noauthorcodeexecution.

- Meeting a Powertrain Verification Challenge (2015)
    - what: Reports an extension of C2E2 that computes local trajectory-divergence bounds automatically and applies bounded-time reachability to a polynomial powertrain model. For two selected driver-behavior families, seven of ten printed properties are satisfied and three stricter variants are refuted, with50–53validated simulations for successes and2–4for failures. This is a model-verification case study; correctness/relative-completeness theory and detailed local-bound algorithm are delegated to earlier papers/report, not machine-checked here. Explains validated simulation plus discrepancy bloating, iterative initial-region refinement, Jacobian symmetric-part eigenvalue bounds, local coordinate changes and inactive-state reduction. Shows how selected STL obligations become timer-based safety requirements and preserves the limits of closed models and selected switching signals. Full result table exposes both positive and deliberately stronger negative specifications, with precise timings and hardware. Input schedules, initial radii, numerical validation and deferred proof assumptions prevent independently reproducing or generalizing the reported verification.
    - limits: Target is polynomial hybrid Model3 from the cited benchmark, not the full higher-fidelity engine suite. Four modes are startup, normal, power and sensor_fail. Continuous engine states are manifold pressure p, air/fuel ratio lambda, pressure estimate pe and integrator i. Introduction says five continuous variables; Figure1 lists these four plus throttle input, and an auxiliary timer is introduced for properties. The paper does not fully reconcile its state-dimension count; do not silently treat it as five autonomous engine states. Figure1 transitions: startup→normal when timer=Ts; normal→power when throttle>=70degrees; power→normal when throttle<=50degrees; normal→sensor_fail on sensorFail. Normal-mode equations are nonlinear polynomials in engine states with parameters ci, engine speed omega and throttle theta_in; Fc and mass flow are auxiliary expressions. Constants and other mode equations are not fully supplied, so this paper alone is not a complete numerical model specification. C2E2 handles closed automata only. Piecewise-constant driver inputs are closed by constructing a family of switching signals for each behavior of interest. Verification covers initial uncertainty and that selected family, not every possible driver, sensor-failure schedule or arbitrary open input. Authors choose two behavior sets said to visit all modes; exact signal ranges/schedules and branch-wise allocation to table rows are not printed. Initial uncertainty is a state-space ball. Its center, radius, exact cover and coordinate units are not supplied. Time horizon T=20 and startup Ts=9 are reported. Figure2 shows an illustrative trajectory/reachable envelope over startup/normal/power/normal; it does not by itself demonstrate sensor-fail coverage or reproduce all selected schedules. Validated simulation is defined as time-stamped regions enclosing the ODE solution on each interval with bounded diameter. A discrepancy function bounds distances between any compared trajectories and vanishes as initial points converge. These are mathematical obligations supporting bloated reachability; ordinary sampled trajectories without valid bounds would not suffice. Algorithm covers the initial region with delta-balls, validates a representative trajectory, bloats by discrepancy, then refines ambiguous balls. Disjointness from unsafe states establishes safety; violation needs a genuine unsafe execution or a certified enclosure contained in the unsafe region at a relevant time. Mere overlap of an overapproximation with unsafe states is inconclusive, not a counterexample. The terse interval/set exposition does not fully formalize this temporal decision rule. Soundness and relative completeness are attributed to the discrepancy properties and Theorem13 of ref6; full assumptions are not repeated. Vanishing enclosure error alone is not a claim that every boundary-touching model terminates or that safety is decided under all numerical conditions. Imported proof and its implementation correspondence have not been independently checked. Automatic local discrepancy uses Jacobian Jf and a Lipschitz constant Lf, a coarse short-time reachable enclosure, and an upper bound on the maximum eigenvalue of the symmetric Jacobian over that region. Rates may be negative. Full construction/correctness is deferred to ref10. Tool still requires a user-supplied Jacobian, so removal of discrepancy annotations is not removal of all mathematical input or proof obligations. Implementation uses Eigen for eigenvalues and interval arithmetic to maximize error-matrix norms. No exact arithmetic, outward-rounding contract, user-Jacobian validation or certified Eigen error bounds are described. These are uninspected implementation/trust boundaries, not demonstrated failures. Model translation from Simulink to the C2E2 automaton is not proved correct here. Local coordinate changes mitigate spurious positive divergence bounds for systems with complex eigenvalues, trading exponential-rate improvement against a multiplicative error factor. The coordinate-update horizon is tuned; no numeric setting or ablation is given. Startup/power reduction omits i from discrepancy computation only where i_dot=0 and i is absent from other right-hand sides; this is not unrestricted integrator elimination in normal mode. Properties reducible to bounded-time invariants use timers since trigger events. Here rise denotes throttle changing from0 to60. For rise, lambda stays within a reference band after eta and until zeta. Table settling intervals are open(eta,zeta), unlike some closed invariant intervals; boundary conventions matter. Arbitrary STL, unbounded liveness and path-integral properties are not covered; integral obligations were explicitly excluded. Reported parameters eta=eta_power=1, eta_s=.5, Ts=9,T=20,lambda_ref=14.7,lambda_power_ref=12.5,zeta=4. Seven positive rows use50or53simulations and615–718seconds; three negative rows use2or4simulations and24,43,45seconds. Simulation counts describe representatives/enclosure construction, not independent random tests or enumeration of every initial state. Experiments use an Intel Quad-Core i7,8GB RAM and Ubuntu11.10. No repeats, variance, parallelism settings, exact CPU model, solver tolerances or raw traces are printed. Faster negative rows are attributed to early exit, not a statistically established universal relation between satisfiable/violated-property runtime. Flow* is said to be unable to verify these models due to nonlinear complexity, but no version, resource limit, settings or comparison table is supplied. Treat as an author report, not a controlled head-to-head benchmark. First-verification priority is also an author knowledge-qualified claim. Tool/model link is http://publish.illinois.edu/c2e2-tool/powertrain-challenge/. Artifact contents, user Jacobian, initial set, switching family and returned witnesses were not opened/run. No machine-checked proof or independently reproduced verification result is established in this reading. Higher-fidelity variants with delay differential equations, actuation delays and lookup tables are future work and explicitly unsupported. No verified Rust/Verus, TLA+, LLM specification/proof agent or distributed-system implementation is evaluated. Methodological relevance is the separation between simulation testing and validated overapproximation, and between selected environmental assumptions and universal model coverage.
    - how much was read: most of it read

- Spin Paxos (2014)
    - title: Model Checking Paxos in Spin
    - what: Models Paxos in executable Promela with proposer, acceptor and learner roles, then uses Spin plus reduction theorems and code transformations to check finite instances. Formalizes safety as learner agreement, proves finite reductions for proposers/learners, and introduces atomic broadcast and counting-guard transformations. Experiments extrapolate quorum constraints and show ordered channel insertion nearly halves state space versus FIFO sends.

    - how much was read: parts read

- VeriEquivBench (2026)
    - what: Given a natural-language problem, the task is to produce Dafny code and a spec. Dafny then proves that the code implies the spec and the spec implies the code.
    - number: 2,389 problems
    - number: Claude-4-sonnet 75.81% on CloverBench but fails on all instances here at pass@4; GPT highest mutual-equivalence rate 2.65%
    - number: 10% of expert-written DafnySynthesis specs wrongly claimed as ground truth, another 18% found by their review
    - limits: A trivially simple matched pair still passes.

- Verified Detection and Prevention of Concurrency Anomalies in Multi-Agent Large Language Model Systems (2026)
    - what: The paper writes four concurrency anomalies of shared memory between LLM agents in TLA+, checked with TLC. It verifies Rust detectors and runtimes in a Verus crate.
    - number: 274 Verus obligations (zero assume, zero admit)
    - number: 24 obligations for the spec-implementation chain; refinement obligations 31 (pessimistic), 18 (SSI projection)
    - number: 274 Verus proof obligations
    - limits: Nothing mechanical links the TLA+ and Verus parts. The TLAPS proofs only check that the texts agree, and the Verus refinements are against Verus state machines. It covers safety only, and the authors state that liveness is a gap. It has a single author and the results are self-reported. The notes read about 25% of the paper.

- 3SF model checking (2025)
    - title: Technical Report: Exploring Automatic Model-Checking of the Ethereum specification
    - what: The authors translate the Python spec of Ethereum's 3-slot-finality (3SF) consensus protocol into TLA+ and use the Apalache model checker to test Accountable Safety: if two conflicting chains are both finalized, at least one third of validators can be shown to have broken a voting rule. They then add hand-made abstractions and re-encode the problem in SMT (CVC5) and Alloy to check larger instances.
    - number: Direct translation (Spec2): checking Accountable Safety timed out after more than 40 hours (Table 1)
    - number: Alloy with Kissat: Accountable Safety holds for up to 6 blocks; e.g. 5 blocks, 25 checkpoints, 12 votes in 234 s (Table 5)
    - number: CVC5: verified up to 5 blocks with 5 checkpoints (1h 40m) or 3 blocks with 6 checkpoints (74h) (Table 4)
    - number: A 7-block Alloy run went over 16 days without finishing
    - limits: Only the finality gadget and only Accountable Safety, on small bounded instances (at most 7 checkpoints and 24 votes); every validator has stake 1. The hand-made abstractions are not proved equal to the Python spec. Appendix A (translation rules) and B (soundness proofs) were skimmed, not read closely.
    - how much was read: parts read
    - [source](https://arxiv.org/abs/2501.07958); [notes: ## Exploring Automatic Model-Checking of the Ethereum specification (2](claude_readings.md)
    - record id: `3sf-model-checking-2025`
- Clock-SI TLA+ (2025)
    - title: Formal modelling and analysis of Clock-SI with TLA+
    - what: A master's thesis that models the Clock-SI protocol (snapshot isolation for a partitioned database using loosely synchronized clocks) in TLA+ in two versions, a detailed one and a simpler one, and uses the TLC model checker to test that the protocol gives Snapshot Isolation, stated with a client-centric specification.
    - number: Abstract model, Snapshot Isolation check: 2 keys, 2 partitions, 2 txns 3 s; 3 txns 3 min 28 s; 3 keys, 2 partitions, 3 txns 11 h 23 min 20 s; 3 keys, 3 partitions, 3 txns timed out (Table 10)
    - number: Detailed model: 3 keys, 2 partitions, 2 txns 21 min; most larger cases timed out (Table 9)
    - number: Symmetry and view features cut one 2-key, 2-partition, 3-txn state-space run from 2 h 54 min to 2 min 2 s (Table 8)
    - number: Machine: i7-7700HQ, 16 GB RAM; each time is the average of 3 runs; maximum runtime 12 hours
    - limits: Bounded to at most 3 transactions, keys, partitions; the target of 3/3/3 was not reached. The abstract model removes the message system, so it does not cover the real messaging. Only the safety property (Snapshot Isolation) is checked; no liveness. Chapters 2-3 (background) and the model listings in 4.1-4.2 were skimmed.
    - how much was read: parts read
    - [source](https://hdl.handle.net/1822/100401); [notes: ## Formal modelling and analysis of Clock-SI with TLA+ (2025)](claude_readings.md)

- Design and Modular Verification of Distributed Transactions in MongoDB (2025)
    - what: A TLA+ spec of MongoDB distributed transactions, with a modular spec of the interface. A follow-up blog generates conformance tests against WiredTiger.
    - number: TLA+ spec about 571 lines plus storage spec about 332 lines
    - number: 87,143 generated tests in about 40 minutes on a small finite model (2 keys, 2 transactions)
    - limits: It is tested only. Code is at https://github.com/mongodb-labs/vldb25-dist-txns.

    - record id: `design-and-modular-verification-2025`
- Korg thesis (2025)
    - title: Verification and Attack Synthesis for Network Protocols
    - what: Proves properties of three network-protocol pieces with theorem provers (Karn's RTT algorithm and TCP's timeout rule in Ivy and ACL2s, and Go-Back-N efficiency in ACL2s), model-checks TCP, DCCP and SCTP handshakes in SPIN, then builds Korg, a tool that searches for attacker behaviour that breaks a given property.
    - number: Handshake properties: TCP 4 (phi1-phi4), SCTP 10 (gamma1-gamma10), DCCP has its own set
    - number: Korg asked for at most 10 attacks per property; default search depth 600,000, maximum 2,400,000
    - number: TCP phi3 (peers do not get stuck) took 72 min to 223 min per attacker model; other TCP properties took seconds
    - number: SCTP: one patched-vs-unpatched check; with the CVE-2021-3772 patch, Korg finds no off-path attack, which proves the patch closes it for the model
    - limits: Models are small by design to avoid state-space explosion; the handshake models are not linked to the Ivy/ACL2s models. Korg handles one centralized attacker, not coordinated multi-process attackers, and is exact only for the model, not the real code. Models took long to write by hand.
    - how much was read: parts read
    - [source](https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.01124); [notes: ## Von Hippel thesis on protocol verification and attack synthesis (20](claude_readings.md)
    - record id: `korg-thesis-2025`
- PolySemist (2025)
    - title: Accelerating Protocol Synthesis and Detecting Unrealizability with Interpretation Reduction.
    - what: A tool that fills in the missing guards and updates of a half-written TLA+ distributed protocol (a sketch) so the protocol meets a safety or liveness property. It loops: the TLC model checker finds a counterexample, the tool rules out every candidate that shares it, and it skips candidates that behave the same on the states seen so far.
    - number: 7 benchmark protocols: 2PC, consensus, decentralized lock, lock server, sharded KV store, two reconfigurable Raft variants
    - number: faster than the previous tool Scythe in 160 of 171 realizable problems; total time about 9,000 s vs 32,000 s on those both solve
    - number: whole distributed-lock protocol (6 expressions over 2 actions) synthesized in 125 s; Scythe timed out at 1 hour
    - number: detected 'no protocol exists' in 80 of 123 unrealizable problems vs 16 for Scythe (easy 46/50, medium 21/35, hard 13/38)
    - limits: A human supplies the sketch (state variables, actions, hole grammars) and the property; results hold only for the chosen finite parameters (e.g. number of nodes), and the all-parameters step is left to TLAPS proofs from prior work. Termination is guaranteed only when each hole grammar has finitely many expressions up to interpretation equivalence.
    - how much was read: parts read
    - [source](https://arxiv.org/abs/2501.14585); [notes: ## PolySemist: protocol synthesis by sketching (2025)](claude_readings.md)
    - record id: `polysemist-2025`

    - number: 21 hours, 33 total lemma nodes
    - number: grammar has 173 predicates
    - number: 33 lemma nodes
    - number: 173 grammar predicates

    - what: The report repeatedly refines TLA+ models and asks Gemini to implement an image-cache example as multithreaded Rust. Its history records a livelock (threads keep running but make no progress) and a premature termination. Both needed correction.

- Vest (2025)
    - title: Vest: Verified, Secure, High-Performance Parsing and Serialization for Rust
    - what: Users describe a binary format, and Vest generates Rust parsers and serializers verified in Verus. It uses a reusable combinator library.
    - number: about 6K library lines, verified in under ten seconds
    - number: DSL compiler 5,807 Rust lines
    - number: format descriptions: Bitcoin about 67 lines, TLS 1.3 about 500, WebAssembly about 600
    - limits: It proves that parse and serialize are inverses, plus memory safety. It does not prove consensus or handshake security. Timing side channels are not a Verus theorem. The DSL frontend is unverified.

    - title: SandTable: Scalable Distributed System Model Checking with Specification-Level State Exploration
    - what: It model-checks a TLA+ spec quickly, then replays the bug traces in the code. A conformance checker replays model traces to find places where the spec and code differ.

    - number: Half a person day to a few person days to fix modeling errors; two person weeks adapting ZooKeeper specs

- ByMC survey (2023)
    - title: Survey on Parameterized Verification with Threshold Automata and the Byzantine Model Checker
    - what: Protocols are modeled by counting messages and participants against thresholds. This lets the tool check safety, and some liveness, for any number of participants, under conditions like n>3t (n participants, at most t faulty).
    - number: Table 4: 13 asynchronous/randomized algorithms (FRB, STRB, ABA, NBACG, NBACR, CBC, CF1S, C1CS, BOSCO, Ben-Or, RABC, kSet, RS-BOSCO)
    - number: Table 6: 16 synchronous cases, 14 verified; HybridQueen and kSetOmit have no successful check

- Temporal prophecy (2021)
    - title: Temporal prophecy for proving temporal properties of infinite-state systems
    - what: Adds prophecy variables (recorded predictions about future execution) to the earlier abstraction method. This lets it keep distinctions that the abstraction lost, without strengthening the contract with the environment.
    - number: four cases: ticket protocol, alternating bit, hybrid reliable broadcast, TLB shootdown
    - number: user invariant conjectures 60, 70, 58, 102
    - number: checking takes 7, 25, 25, 240 seconds
    - number: Figure 3 (model lines; conjectures; checker seconds): ticket with task queues 90; 60; 7; alternating bit 143; 70; 25; hybrid reliable broadcast 166; 58; 25; TLB shootdown 468; 102; 240 (checking time, not authoring time)
    - limits: Prophecy formulas are extracted automatically only from an invariant the user supplies. Choosing the witness is manual. No claim of completeness.

- Elle (2020)
    - title: Elle: Inferring Isolation Anomalies from Experimental Observations
    - what: A test-time checker that runs random transactions against a database, records what clients saw, and rebuilds a dependency graph between transactions. A cycle in that graph is a proof that the database broke its claimed isolation level.
    - number: Found anomalies in all 4 databases tested: TiDB, YugaByte DB, FaunaDB, Dgraph
    - number: Checks histories of hundreds of thousands of transactions in tens of seconds; Knossos often fails after a few hundred
    - number: Linear time in history length
    - limits: Needs generated workloads where reads reveal the full write history (such as list append). Ignores predicates, so it cannot separate repeatable read from serializability. It finds bugs, not proof that a database is correct.
    - how much was read: parts read
    - [source](https://arxiv.org/abs/2003.10554); [notes: ## Elle isolation checker (2020)](claude_readings.md)
    - record id: `elle-2020`
- eXtreme Modelling (2020)
    - title: eXtreme Modelling in Practice
    - what: MongoDB compared two ways to link a model to code. One is model-based trace checking (MBTC), which checks whether logged runs fit the model. The other is model-based test-case generation (MBTCG). Trace checking of replication was impractical. For Realm Sync's operational-transformation rules, a Go generator turned TLC states into C++ tests.
    - number: Realm Sync table (§5.2): 795 TLA+ lines, 755 Go lines, 4 weeks total effort
    - number: Abandoned trace checking after 10 weeks
    - number: 4913 generated tests reached 100% branch coverage vs 21% handwritten and 92% AFL fuzzer
    - limits: Trace checking of replication struggled with the level of abstraction, concurrency, incomplete observations, and keeping code and model in sync. The generated tests cover local merge behavior, not the whole distributed product.
    - how much was read: parts read
    - [source](https://www.vldb.org/pvldb/vol13/p1346-davis.pdf); [notes: P04. eXtreme Modelling in Practice](../distributed_verification_review_b/deep_3_spec_tools_tla_to_code.md)
    - record id: `extreme-modelling-2020`
- Pretend Synchrony / Goolong (2019)
    - title: Pretend synchrony: synchronous verification of asynchronous distributed programs
    - what: Verifies Go distributed programs by automatically rewriting them into an equivalent synchronous program (messages become assignments), then checking ordinary verification conditions with the Z3 solver. Four case studies: two-phase commit, Raft leader election, single-decree Paxos, Multi-Paxos key-value store.
    - number: manual invariant annotations reduced by 6x versus Dafny
    - number: check time 1.73 s total vs 1455.84 s in Dafny for the three protocols
    - number: Paxos 504 lines of Go, 14 loop-invariant lines, 1.51 s; Dafny 72 invariant lines, 1141.35 s
    - number: Multi-Paxos KV: 847 Go lines, 1.64 s check, 118.5 req/ms, within 1.5-3x of unverified EPaxos code
    - limits: Safety only, no liveness. Needs structured loops, symmetric process ids, typed channels, pairwise communication and round non-interference; rules out Multi-Paxos stable-leader optimization, Stoppable Paxos and ring topologies such as Chord.
    - how much was read: parts read
    - [source](https://doi.org/10.1145/3290372); [notes: Pretend Synchrony (2019)](claude_readings.md)
    - record id: `pretend-synchrony-goolong-2019`

    - what: This is the public repository of TLA-Prover at commit 6427de9. The audit found two case lists (a 30-row holdout and a 20-row suite called 30-case).     - title: tlapm examples (SimpleEventually, Dekker, Allocator)
    - what: These are example TLA+ proofs. SimpleEventually is a 73-line proof that a toy spec eventually sets x to TRUE. Dekker (978 lines) proves mutual exclusion, no-stuck and starvation freedom. Allocator states liveness properties but proves only safety theorems.
